Wallenius Wilhelmsen · WAWI-NO · the new Memo conveyor's card of 27 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Wallenius Wilhelmsen

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,550 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is at a cyclical peak that the company is converting into contracted earnings. The demand driver (Chinese exports) is real but is a domestic-slump push exposed to a dated cluster of policy changes (port fees in November 2026, the hybrid cap in December 2026, the VAT rebate cut in April 2027). Supply arrives mainly in 2026–2027, is partly absorbed by containerised cargo returning, and collapses after 2028. The routing premium is the most fragile component. For the model: treat 2027 as contract-protected; treat 2028–2029 as the period where external forces meet the company's pricing (renewals, H&H, spot); treat cost inflation (charter-in at USD 85–110k a day) as the main external offset to repricing. The strongest unknowns to research are the 2027 renewal rate achieved, the port-fee outcome and recovery, and Suez timing.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0250C-0540C-0752C-0958C-3542C-3940
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-5011th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is at a cyclical low and rising. There are no price wars and carriers hold the leverage today. But capacity is arriving mechanically through 2027 into a market whose demand growth is policy-exposed. Intensity will show first in charter and spot rates (2027), and in Wallenius Wilhelmsen's own pricing only at the 2028–2029 renewals.

Reasoning.

  • Today: "no evidence of pricing wars" (NH Investment & Securities). Höegh signs new contracts above USD 100 per cbm net and reports USD 82 per cbm prorated in September 2026. One-year time charter rates are USD 85–100k a day for 6,500 CEU ships and USD 110k a day for 7,000 CEU dual-fuel ships, up 120–135% from the late-2025 trough. Carriers are prioritising customers.
  • Supply is rising: fleet growth of 8.4% (2024), 13.1% (2025) and about 8–8.8% (2026, to 5.49m CEU), then about 460k CEU (about 50 ships) in 2027. The orderbook is 20.6–21% of the fleet (147 vessels), down from a 42.4% peak in July 2024.
  • Named expansions: Hyundai Glovis adds 12 × 10,800 CEU ships in 2027 (+20%, aiming at Chinese cargo; 128 vessels by 2030). Höegh has 18 firm Aurora vessels plus 4 options and 4 slots. Chinese operators took 29 ships in 2025 (about 10% of the fleet including orderbook); COSCO targets 30 ships; BYD has 8 ships. MOL plans 130–150 vessels by 2035.
  • Exit is closed. Scrapping was zero in 2024 and 2025, and 59–60% of the fleet was built in 2011 or earlier. Under the Wisdom Library rule "competitive intensity is entry minus exit", entry is high and exit is nil, so any demand stall translates directly into intensity.
  • Offsetting buffers for 2026–2027: the container reservoir (more than 70 vessels of displaced demand against about 50 ships delivering in 2027), Cape routing, and yard slots unavailable until 2030. After 2028, fleet growth collapses (DNB: 2.8% by end-2028, 0.5% by end-2029) and scrapping candidates rise (about 10% of the fleet over 30 years old by 2030). The intensity curve therefore probably peaks around 2028 and eases from 2029–2030.

Implication for the model. Hold 2027 contracted pricing. Stress 2028–2029 renewal pricing and the cost of chartered-in capacity in opposite directions: falling charter rates are a cost relief that partly offsets weaker renewal rates.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F4021st of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Net headwind. Wallenius Wilhelmsen buys the same scarce asset it sells: ship capacity. The chartered-in third of its fleet and its newbuilds are priced in the same tight market that lifts its freight rates. Fuel is mostly a timing issue. Supplier concentration (one Chinese yard) is rising.

Reasoning.

  • Charter-in capacity: 41 of 121 shipping vessels were chartered in at Q1 2026. One-year rates are USD 100k a day for 6,500 CEU (October 2026, +120%) and USD 110k for 7,000 CEU dual-fuel (+135%). The May 2026 guidance cut was blamed on "net bunker and capacity costs". As charters roll in 2027, this cost re-fixes upwards. Shaper deliveries (14 vessels, 2026–2028) progressively replace charters. Höegh puts the cash cost of its own newbuilds at about USD 26 per cbm against about USD 80 per cbm for market charters, which shows how valuable ownership is at today's rates.
  • Fuel: zero fuel hedges. BAF lag is 2–4 months (Höegh: 5–6 months). Net bunker cost was USD 78m (Q1), 109m (Q2) and 65m (Q3, "normalised"). Q2 fuel costs rose 41% quarter on quarter (+USD 76m against a +USD 46m surcharge increase). Brent reached USD 111 on 22 October 2026, so another lag squeeze in Q4 2026/Q1 2027 is plausible.
  • Newbuild prices: USD 82.5–85m for a 6,500 CEU ship, 16–20% above the average since 2012. Yard slots are available only from 2030. Chinese yards hold 71% of the global orderbook; Japanese yards are full to 2030 at 10–15% premiums. Wallenius Wilhelmsen's new 4 + 8 order is at Jinling, which also builds all 14 Shapers. An USD 80m overrun has already come from engine reconfiguration.
  • Crew and opex: crew costs rose from USD 147m (2023) to 180m (2024); vessel opex +USD 11m in 2025; shipyard labour shortages (41% of yards).
  • Other costs: US West Coast stevedoring (a USD 12m retroactive charge), EU ETS and green fuel premiums (green methanol about 95% above conventional methanol).
  • Mitigants: about two thirds of the fleet is owned; Shapers were ordered at roughly pre-boom prices (about USD 1.6bn for 14 ships, about USD 11k per CEU against about USD 13k per CEU at today's 6,500 CEU price; my arithmetic).
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

A521st of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Macro forces bite hardest at the industry level, through the car-carrier supply–demand balance (Chinese export volume net of trade policy, and routing). They bite much less at the company level (contracts, a USD functional currency, hedging) or the sector level (I.1. Logistics is mixed). The perverse feature for modelling: China's domestic slump, a macro negative, is currently the company's largest tailwind.

Reasoning.

  • China (dominant). The property slump and weak retail push exports. A domestic recovery is a negative for the company (DNB: risk to exports beyond 2026). This is why consensus and management talk of "structural" demand while global vehicle sales fall.
  • Rates and consumer affordability (negative; moderate). High policy rates (Bank of England 4%, Fed hikes expected by some, a hawkish ECB) weigh on Western vehicle demand, dealer restocking (Stellantis dealer stock at 94 days in June 2026), and H&H (agricultural and construction capex). These act through volume on Western-origin trades and H&H mix.
  • Trade fragmentation (negative; rising). North American regionalisation (Canada and Mexico take 56% of US exports) and the tariffs listed under C-04.
  • Geopolitics and energy (mixed). Red Sea and Hormuz absorb capacity (positive for rates) but raise fuel (a BAF timing headwind). Brent at USD 111 (22 October 2026). Middle East revenue exposure is small (2–3% of revenue, against 17% of flows at Höegh).
  • FX (low). USD functional and presentation currency; NOK and SEK costs hedged on a 36-month layered programme; NOK bonds swapped. KRW moves the EUKOR put liability (USD 81m per 10%, non-cash).
  • Sector level. The FfF digest puts the Logistics sector at Neutral and the environment at "risk-off beneath an index at its high", with Fragility at Double Underweight and Momentum at Underweight. This matters for the multiple (C-82), not the earnings.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–58

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Four further dynamics matter for the model. Net, they are mildly positive for 2027 and two-sided for 2028–2030.

  1. Container reservoir (positive buffer). 1.0–1.5m cars in containers in H1 2026 (some estimates 2–3m a year) equal more than 70 car-carrier vessels. This is larger than the roughly 50 ships delivering in 2027, so the first wave of new supply mostly absorbs displaced cargo before it pressures rates.
  2. Yard ceiling and scrapping (positive from 2029). Slots only from 2030, with fewer than 500 active yards (from more than 1,000 in 2008). Fleet growth falls to 2.8% (end-2028) and 0.5% (end-2029). About 10% of the fleet will be over 30 years old by 2030, and CII/ETS rules penalise it (Pareto expects scrapping waves in 2029 and 2035). Sector digest: "full shipyards set the floor".
  3. Port congestion and schedule unreliability (positive now; reversible). 18% of fleet capacity in port operations or congestion; schedule reliability fell to 49.9% in August 2026. Idle vessels are 1.9% of the fleet (normal).
  4. Owner and parent dynamics (neutral to positive). Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding draws almost 50% of its free cash flow to equity from Wallenius Wilhelmsen (DNB, 29 September 2026). The families therefore want high distributions and will support funding (the parent took its pro rata 37% of the October placement).

Negatives: Hyundai Glovis's captive-volume expansion on China routes; Japanese peers' car-carrier profits already falling (MOL down 93% year on year in Q2 2026; NYK guiding automotive profit down ¥13.9bn), which shows the cycle can turn for weaker or more spot-exposed books before it turns for long-contract carriers; US government cargo timing (the Government segment missed by 33% in Q2 2026).

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F4220th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Entry is the most active it has been in decades. The threat ranks: standard entrants (Chinese state shipowners and lessors), then orthogonal entrants (OEM captive fleets), then adjacent entrants (container lines and port groups). The combined threat is medium now and medium-high in a 2028 downturn.

Reasoning.

  • Standard entrants: Chinese operators took 29 ships in 2025, reaching about 10% of the fleet including orderbook. COSCO targets 30 ships. Chinese yards build 81% of PCTC deliveries, so state-linked owners have access to slots. This is a medium-high threat because these owners are least likely to exit.
  • Orthogonal (OEM captive): BYD (8 ships), SAIC Anji. Experts call them an "emergency button", and OEMs still prefer contract operators. A medium threat to China-origin volume.
  • Adjacent: MSC (bought Gram), CMA CGM/CEVA in RoRo logistics, AD Ports/Ningbo (United Global Ro-Ro), and container lines carrying cars in boxes. Medium.
  • Innovative: none material (digital booking platforms are irrelevant to this contract business).
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F4021st of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where relevant, how are suppliers behaving: a support or a source of risk?

Judgement. A source of risk: cost, concentration and timing. Shipyards and ship owners have pricing power, and the company has concentrated its whole fleet renewal at one Chinese yard.

Reasoning.

  • Yards: Jinling builds all 14 Shapers and the new 4 + 8 (from mid-2030). Chinese yards hold 71.2% of the global orderbook; Japanese yards are full to 2030 at 10–15% premiums. Yards are recovering pricing power (active yards fell from more than 1,000 in 2008 to fewer than 500 in 2022) and can demand higher upfront instalments. There is a USD 80m overrun precedent, and labour shortages (41% of yards).
  • Charter owners: one-year rates of USD 100–110k a day.
  • Fuel: Equinor bio-methanol (two years); green methanol scarce and costly.
  • Ship management: crewing through related parties (Wilhelmsen Ship Management, Wallenius Marine) at cost plus 5%, which is acceptable governance but a related-party dependence.
  • Engines: Everllence/MAN dominance.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0162C-0347C-0440C-0870C-3266C-3740C-4345
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C-629th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Demand for deep-sea car-carrier capacity is exceptionally strong and still rising over the last and next six months, faster than in the prior 12–18 months. But the strength is a supply-push from China, not a pull from end consumers. Roughly half of today's tightness is structural and half is cyclical or temporary. On the demand side alone, the cyclical part does not have two years of visibility. The visibility Wallenius Wilhelmsen has to 2027–2028 comes from its contracts, not from demand.

Reasoning and critical evidence.

  • End-consumer demand is weak. Global light-vehicle sales fell 4% year on year to August 2026, and Morgan Stanley expects −1.7% for 2026. US sales fell 4% in H1 2026. Chinese domestic retail sales fell about 20% (to 18.9m units annualised, after new-energy-vehicle tax breaks were withdrawn).
  • Seaborne car-carrier demand is booming regardless. It has grown 81% since 2018 against 15% growth in vehicle trade. Chinese vehicle exports rose about 65–68% year on year in H1 2026, exceeded 1m a month in June and July, and CAAM's 2026 target is 10m (+50%). Chinese exports went from 1% to about 25% of global vehicle trade in a decade. More than half go to Europe and Latin America (long hauls), and tonne-miles grow faster than units.
  • The mechanism is a vent for surplus. Chinese makers export because their home market is shrinking (domestic registrations down more than 20%, house prices falling for 45 consecutive months). That makes the demand durable in the short run (factories need an outlet), but fragile to policy and to localisation.

Structural versus cyclical split (my judgement).

  • Structural: Chinese OEMs' globalisation, with their share of European passenger cars rising from 5% (2025) to 12% (2026e, Deutsche Bank) and 21% of hybrid registrations in H1 2026. Also the longer average haul, and the reservoir of 1.0–1.5m cars shipped in containers in H1 2026 (25–30% of China's deep-sea exports, "more than 70 standard vessels" of demand that wants to move back to car carriers once space exists).
  • Cyclical or temporary: the domestic-slump push; Cape of Good Hope routing (4–7% of effective fleet capacity; +43% steaming distance on affected lanes); port congestion (18% of fleet capacity tied up in port operations year to date in 2026).

Policy headwinds arrive in a cluster within 6–12 months. The EU–China hybrid cap of 350k a year from 1 December 2026, against a run-rate of about 900k (the Commission expects a cut of more than 50% over four years). China's NEV export VAT rebate falls from 13% to 9% on 1 April 2027. Brazil restores a 35% tariff; Mexico raises duties from 20% to 50%; Canada and the US have tariffs in place.

Visibility is poor and forecasts are unanchored. Morgan Stanley expects 9.3m Chinese exports in 2026 and 12.3m in 2027 (+33%). In August, DNB Carnegie's base case was +1% for 2027 and −2% for 2028 (low case −12% for 2027). In November DNB moved to +50% for 2026 and about +10% a year for 2027–2029, while also projecting vehicle tonne-mile growth of only 2% in 2027 and 1% in 2028. A demand variable that a leading broker revises by about ten points a year within three months has no two-year visibility.

Non-China cargo is weak. H&H (the highest-paying cargo) faces agricultural and construction downturns: John Deere expects −15% to −20% in large agriculture, and H&H seaborne growth slows from 4% (2026) to 2% (2027) and 1% (2028–2029), though mining is positive. Korean exports rose 2% and Japanese exports were flat in H1 2026. European OEMs are losing share.

What this means for the company. Shipping is about 90% contracted, so 2027 demand risk passes through as volume (contracts are a share of customer volume, not minimums), not as price. The demand risk becomes a price risk at the 2028–2029 renewals.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D4717th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Three disruption forces are strengthening and act against the company's long-haul volume base, mainly beyond 18 months: Chinese OEM in-housing, containerisation of cars, and near-shoring. One force acts for it: the regulated green transition, which favours modern large fleets. None is disproved, but the OEM-fleet threat is weaker than the bear case claims.

Reasoning.

  • OEM captive fleets (against; strengthening; bounded). BYD has 8 vessels (more than 1m vehicles a year; it claims 30–40% lower cost per vehicle); SAIC Anji has its own ships. Expert evidence says OEMs treat private fleets as "an emergency button" and prefer long-term contracts with established operators (24 September 2026). Wallenius Wilhelmsen is meanwhile signing multi-year deals with Chinese OEMs: a USD 700m three-year contract with a leading Asian maker (November 2026), and China contracts moving from one-year to up to five-year terms. Net: a cap on share gains in China, not a collapse.
  • Containerisation (against; strengthened by the shortage). 1.0–1.5m cars went in containers in H1 2026, plus flat racks and COSCO's converted pulp carriers (1,000 cars per voyage). Today this is a reservoir of demand that returns to car carriers, but it puts an alternative price ceiling on car-carrier rates. Some OEMs may adapt permanently. Morgan Stanley flags that OEMs who "faced soaring logistics costs" will push back.
  • Near-shoring and localisation (against; slow). BYD Hungary (150k units) and Brazil; EU local-content proposals; USMCA; PwC finds 48% of regional manufacturers plan to near-shore within five years. This is a five-to-ten-year drag on tonne-miles, consistent with DNB's tonne-mile growth of only 1–2% in 2027–2028.
  • Green regulation and technology (for the company; slightly delayed). Shaper dual-fuel ships are 27–40% more fuel-efficient. SBTi has validated the company's targets; there are green-premium contracts (a USD 766m H&H contract funds green fuel); EU ETS and FuelEU penalise older tonnage and speed scrapping. The IMO Net-Zero Framework vote was postponed a year, weakening the near-term push.
  • Minor forces: heavier EVs (2–7% more weight) reduce payload and raise fire risk; AI and digital booking have little effect on a contract business.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4024th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Regulatory change is heavy and, net, negative over the next 6–18 months. Trade policy and US port fees outweigh the competitive benefit of tightening environmental rules. One dated binary sits on the doorstep: the scheduled end of the US port-fee suspension around 10 November 2026.

Reasoning.

  • USTR Section 301 port fees. USD 46 per net ton on foreign-built car carriers, suspended for a year by the 30 October 2025 US–China agreement and due to resume in November 2026 unless extended. The files cannot confirm whether it was extended. Gross exposure is USD 350–400m a year before customer recovery, about 22–25% of 2026 EBITDA; Q4 2025 booked USD 21m. US trade was 17% of 2024 shipping revenue. Management plans recovery through customers and routing. Höegh, by comparison, guides only USD 60–70m gross. The reported effect depends on contractual pass-through, which is not disclosed.
  • Trade barriers on Chinese exports: the EU hybrid cap (1 December 2026), EU countervailing duties on BEVs (7.8–35.3%), Canada, Brazil (35%), Mexico (50%), US tariffs (15% for Japan and Korea under Section 232 deals), and the Chinese VAT rebate cut (April 2027). These act on industry tonne-miles in 2027 and beyond.
  • Environmental rules (cost, mostly passed through; a relative advantage). EU ETS reaches 100% of emissions from 2026 (70% in 2025); the provision was USD 32m by June 2026 against USD 13m of expense in 2025. FuelEU requires 2% (2025) rising to 6% (2030). MEPC global fuel standards start in 2028; CII ratings force corrective plans on older tonnage. BAF 2.0 and ETS surcharges pass costs to customers. These rules accelerate scrapping and favour the Shaper fleet.
  • Supportive: US Maritime Security Program subsidies (USD 10m per ship a year) for the Government segment.
  • Latent: Western measures against Chinese-built ships. The entire newbuild programme, existing and new, is at China's Jinling yard.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B706th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Strongly. The Chinese export boom and longer tonne-miles are accelerating demand for Wallenius Wilhelmsen's capacity. Because the fleet is full, the acceleration converts into price, contract duration and customer selection, not volume. That conversion is happening now, in the 2027–2029 renewal round.

Reasoning.

  • Evidence: management is "effectively sold out out of Asia, requiring severe customer prioritisation". China contracts are shifting from one-year to up to five-year terms. The USD 700m three-year Asian OEM award (November 2026), the USD 130m one-year Asian contract for 2026, the European OEM's early extension to 2031 (USD 420m), and the USD 950m + 100m equipment-maker deal all show customers paying for security of capacity.
  • Industry gauges: charter rates up 120%+; spot USD 170 per cbm; Höegh new contracts above USD 100 per cbm; China Merchants Energy Shipping's RoRo profit up 687% year on year in Q3 2026.
  • The limit: volumes are not growing (Q2 2026 14.5m cbm, 2% below DNB). Revenue gains depend on how much of the market price the company captures at renewal. Its stated "lower-for-longer" approach (DNB, February 2026) trades some peak price for duration. The driver therefore benefits the company somewhat less, but for longer, than the spot-heavy peers.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

B664th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How is customer demand behaving, how far does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Customers are paying for security of capacity. They are extending early, lengthening contracts and buying shipping and logistics together. That strongly supports the 2027–2029 thesis. The support is fragile in two specific ways: contracts protect price, not volume, and OEMs who paid peak logistics costs will push back hard once capacity loosens.

Reasoning.

  • Support:
  • The European OEM extended early for mid-2028 to mid-2031 (USD 420m on top of USD 180m remaining).
  • A ten-year logistics extension from April 2027 (USD 2bn).
  • USD 700m over three years with an Asian OEM, and USD 950m plus a USD 100m option with an equipment maker (both November 2026).
  • Chinese OEMs moving from one-year to up to five-year contracts.
  • HMG's commitment of at least 50% of its exports.
  • 70% of the top 50 customers buy both services.
  • Fragility:
  • Contracts guarantee a percentage of a customer's volume, not minimums. H&H and European OEM volumes are weak; the US market −4%; dealer stock at Stellantis 94 days.
  • Concentration: 10 customers are 50% of shipping and logistics; the top five are 36% of shipping revenue; HMG is the largest.
  • OEMs "faced soaring logistics costs" (Morgan Stanley) and will demand concessions when capacity frees.
  • Green premiums depend on OEMs under cost pressure.
  • Year-end seasonality: dealer stocking in November–December, then a January–February slowdown.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F4023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. They mostly hurt demand (trade barriers) and help relative competitiveness (environmental rules that penalise old tonnage). Net effect over 18 months: negative. The position is fragile because the largest items (US port fees, Chinese export policy, possible action against Chinese-built ships) are binary political decisions.

Reasoning. See C-04 for the list.

  • Help: EU ETS (100% from 2026) and FuelEU (2% → 6% by 2030), CII ratings and MEPC 2028 standards speed scrapping and make the dual-fuel Shapers more valuable. Surcharges pass costs on (BAF 2.0, ETS). US MSP subsidies support Government Services.
  • Hurt: the USTR fee (USD 350–400m gross a year); the EU hybrid cap and BEV duties; Brazil and Mexico tariffs; the China VAT rebate cut; US tariffs.
  • The IMO Net-Zero Framework delay lowers near-term regulatory pressure on older fleets. That narrows the company's green advantage for a few years and weakens the case for its USD 80m dual-fuel add-on (DNB, February 2025).
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4523rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what growth rate does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement. The company already holds about 16–20% of a mature market. The realistic addressable market allows only about 1–3% a year volume growth over the mid term (2027–2030), despite this year's boom. Mid-term revenue growth must come from price, mix, logistics and share, not market volume.

Reasoning.

  • Market size: global seaborne vehicle trade reached a record 32.3m units in 2025; the deep-sea PCTC fleet was 773 vessels / 4.9m CEU at end-2025.
  • Long-run growth: seaborne car trade has grown 3–4% a year; car-carrier demand rose 81% since 2018 because of tonne-miles.
  • Forward: DNB sees tonne-mile growth of 2% (2027) and 1% (2028). H&H growth falls to 1–2%. Chinese export forecasts range from −12% to +33% for 2027.
  • Logistics: a large but fragmented finished-vehicle logistics market where the company has about USD 1.1–1.2bn of revenue. The ten-year contract and new terminals give modest growth.
  • Company growth is capacity-limited: net fleet additions to 2028 are probably high single digits after charter redeliveries (my estimate).

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C-6311th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Revenue has sat on a high plateau since 2022, and in 2026 profit went through a cost-driven trough. Both are now turning up modestly. Q3 2026 was the first quarter of year-on-year revenue growth in five quarters, and contracts signed after the quarter lock in a price step-up for 2027. The turn is in price and backlog, not volume, because the company is sold out.

Reasoning.

  • Revenue: 5,045 (2022), 5,149, 5,308, 5,240 (2025). Quarterly: 1,253 (Q1 2026, −3.4% year on year), 1,305 (Q2, −3.3%), 1,401 (Q3, +5.3%). Consensus: 5,383 (2026), 5,538 (2027), 5,451 (2028).
  • Profit: adjusted EBITDA 1,901 (2024) → 1,811 (2025) → about 1,600 (2026 guidance); quarterly 389/361/428. Consensus 2027 is 1,827. Underlying 2026 is about USD 1.66bn once the excess Q1–Q2 net bunker cost (about USD 55–60m above the Q3 normalised run-rate) is added back (my arithmetic).
  • Volumes: −8% in 2024 and −1% in 2025, partly by choice (the company declined renewals at unacceptable rates). Utilisation 96%: "completely sold out".
  • Net freight rate: rose from 41.4 (2020) to 64.6 (2025), fell for the first time in the cycle in Q2 2026 (61.4), and rose again in Q3.
  • Backlog: fell from 7.8bn (Q3 2025) to 5.8bn (Q3 2026) as new contracting slowed (USD 870m signed in Q4 2025, 460m in Q2 2026). Then USD 1.7bn was awarded after Q3: USD 700m for three years with an Asian OEM, and USD 950m plus a USD 100m option with an equipment maker (shipping plus logistics). Management expects to contract "the vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity by end-2026, at rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions".
  • Extent of the turn. Revenue will grow modestly (consensus +3% in 2027). Volume growth is capped by fleet: the Shaper deliveries add gross capacity of roughly 18–19% (14 × about 10,500 CEU against roughly 790k CEU in the shipping fleet), but net of charter redeliveries and sales probably high single digits (my estimate). The profit turn is larger: consensus EBITDA +13% in 2027.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C+608th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Tailwinds dominate today and through 2027 in the company's contracted P&L. Headwinds are building and concentrate in a 6–24 month window (port fees, hybrid cap, VAT rebate cut, 2027 deliveries, possible Suez normalisation). They hit spot rates, charter costs and the multiple first, and the company's contract rates only from 2028. The external picture is a strong peak with a downward-skewed and wide cone for 2028–2029.

Driver by driver (value chain / industry / sector):

Driver Now (last and next 6m) 2027–2029 Level where it acts
Chinese export boom Strong tailwind Uncertain; policy headwinds; forecasts from −12% to +33% Industry (demand)
Fleet supply and orderbook Mild headwind (absorbed) Headwind 2027–28; tailwind 2029+ (growth to 0.5%, scrapping) Industry (supply)
Routing and geopolitical premium Strong tailwind Fragile; container Suez transits back to 54% of pre-crisis by October 2026 Industry
Decarbonisation regulation Mild tailwind for modern fleets; costs passed through Tailwind (scrapping, premiums) Value chain
Protectionism and near-shoring Growing headwind Headwind on tonne-miles Industry/value chain
Input costs (charter, fuel, newbuilds) Headwind Eases if the cycle turns (charter relief) Value chain
Western OEM and H&H weakness Headwind (mix) Mixed (mining up; agriculture and construction down) Value chain
Logistics sector themes (automation, contract logistics) Neutral Neutral to positive Sector

Overall. For 2027 the company is unusually protected: renewals are signed at peak, and the container reservoir and Cape routing support utilisation. For 2028–2029 the paths skew to the downside for market rates (DNB's August path: utilisation 85% in 2027 and 80% in 2028–2029; one-year charter USD 72.7k → 51.3k → 38.9k a day), while the company's renewal exposure is smaller because so much is being locked now. The cone is very wide because the decisive variable, Chinese export volume, is policy-driven.

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Management is doing most of the right things for a cyclical peak. It is locking in duration, renewing the fleet with the industry's largest and most efficient ships, tightening its financial framework, removing the EUKOR overhang and pushing logistics margins. The internal change is not transformational: the strategy and leadership are continuous, M&A is absent and the perimeter barely moves. Two internal choices shape the model more than any other. First, how much of the peak the 2027–2029 renewals capture (a duration-first, "lower-for-longer" posture against "rates reflecting the current market"). Second, the capital trade-off between high payouts and a stepped-up newbuild programme, which has already required equity and will likely suppress extraordinary dividends in 2027 before free cash flow inflects in 2028. Execution risk sits in digital, in logistics leadership (interim) and in commercial continuity during the CCO handover.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

D3816th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The company's own earnings risk over 18 months is moderate, because 2027 is being contracted at peak terms. The share-price risk is high, because the valuation (P/E at the 100th percentile of 10 years; P/B about 1.8x against 0.7–0.9x historically) already capitalises DNB's newly raised mid-cycle EBITDA of USD 1.4bn. Several dated and correlated triggers sit within 6–18 months: US port fees in November 2026, the EU hybrid cap in December 2026, the Chinese VAT rebate cut in April 2027, about 50 new ships in 2027, and possible Suez normalisation. Fair trough downside is about 40–48% (stress 57–66%), partly offset by about 25% of the price in expected dividends to 2028. For the model: the main downside scenario should be lower 2028–2029 renewal rates and volumes with charter-cost relief, a USD 1.0–1.2bn EBITDA run-rate by 2029, and a dividend cut. The main near-term event risk is port fees, whose cost recovery the Consumer must research.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D6016th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Improving moderately and unevenly. Commercial discipline, balance-sheet management and logistics margins have improved visibly. Digital execution, safety measurement in logistics and some accounting history (the EUKOR put) show lower standards that are being repaired rather than an outlier culture.

Reasoning.

  • Better:
  • Commercial discipline: the company declined unacceptable renewals (volumes −8% in 2024) and locked more than 70% of 2025 volumes early.
  • Logistics turnaround: a record USD 46m adjusted EBITDA in Q2 2026, a 24% beat, with temporary staff replaced by permanent staff in the US.
  • BAF 2.0 multi-fuel mechanism (January 2025); ETS surcharges.
  • Treasury: 1.2bn of ship loans converted into flexible RCFs maturing 2030–2031; deliberate cash reduction; interest rate hedge ratio about 70%.
  • Working capital recovered: cash conversion 72% in Q2 to 110% in Q3.
  • Safety: logistics LTIF improved from 12.25 to 10.26; engagement score at maximum; staff turnover down from 44% to 38%.
  • Worse or weak:
  • A 2024 internal audit found inadequate internal controls on logistics safety metrics (described as a bottom-percentile control deficiency for a company of this size).
  • Shipping LTIF worsened from 0.41 to 0.60.
  • Digital: a USD 13m true-up on the legacy platform (Q4 2024) and recurring transformation costs.
  • The new auditor EY (from 2024) forced the EUKOR put to be recognised: −USD 929m of equity and +USD 847m of liability in Q1 2024. Prior accounting under PwC had not captured it.
  • "Not good enough at forecasting" the Government segment (management, Q4 2025).
  • 94 Alert Line cases in 2025.
  • Governance: the LTIP for 2026–2028 was simplified to ROCE (75%; vesting between 13% and 17% three-year average) and relative TSR (25%). There is malus and clawback. Related-party services are priced at cost plus 5% (OECD).
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

A422nd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Several are present. None is thesis-breaking on its own, but together they explain why 2026 earnings fell while the market boomed. They also show the company is not converting the peak into profit as efficiently as its pure-play peer.

Present:

  • 2026 guidance cut (−5% to −9% against the original range).
  • Q3 adjusted EBITDA 2.5% below Bloomberg consensus; net profit to owners 8% below (minority leakage to HMG in EUKOR; USD 28m dividend to non-controlling interests in Q3).
  • Net freight rate fell for the first time in the cycle (Q2 2026).
  • Shipping backlog shrank for a year (7.8bn → 5.8bn) and duration shortened (3.3 → 2.8 years) before the November awards.
  • Liquidity fell to the floor; a USD 300m dilutive raise; capex stepped up; the company is exposed to charter re-fixing at peak rates.
  • Government Services weak (Q2 −33% against DNB).
  • Digital churn and costs; CCO turnover during renewals; interim COO in Logistics.
  • Shipping safety LTIF worsened.

Plausible:

  • The "lower-for-longer" posture leaves part of the peak on the table compared with Höegh (whose net rate is about 29% higher).
  • Pro-cyclical newbuild ordering (4 + 8 at near-peak prices).
  • US port fees resuming with incomplete recovery.
  • BAF lag returning with Brent at USD 111.
  • The family shareholder agreement and dividend dependence of the parent biasing allocation towards payouts over buybacks or balance-sheet resilience.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C589th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. Reasonably congruent, not exceptional. Goals (returns, resilience, green leadership), strategy (integration, contracts, fleet renewal), incentives (LTIP 75% ROCE, 25% relative TSR; sustainability in short-term pay) and decisions (declining unacceptable renewals, selling non-core MIRRAT) point the same way. The incongruences are digital execution, logistics controls, and an ownership structure that rewards payouts over buybacks or balance-sheet slack.

The pay ratio fell (2,052% → 1,768%). The Wisdom Library's "incentives drive behaviour" applies: management is paid on returns, and the families are paid through dividends.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D4216th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. Five risks matter for the model, ranked by plausibility × magnitude over 18 months. The first two are near-term and dated.

  1. Suez/Red Sea normalisation (plausible; medium-high magnitude). It releases 4–7% of effective capacity (estimates range up to 12–15%). Container Suez transits were back to 54% of pre-crisis by early October 2026. Car carriers are not returning yet; DNB assumes late 2026 or early 2027. Effect on the company: small on contracted 2027 revenue; large on spot, charter-market rates, 2028 renewals and the multiple. It also cuts fuel use, which is a cost tailwind. My estimate of the hit: a 5–10% fall in EBITDA versus consensus in 2027–2028, and a much larger share-price reaction (sector digest: "the premium leaves within weeks of the routes reopening").
  2. US port fees resuming around 10 November 2026 (dated; plausibility unknown from the files; high magnitude). USD 46 per net ton; gross USD 350–400m a year for the company before recovery (22–25% of 2026 EBITDA). Q4 2025 cost USD 21m. Mitigation through customer recovery and routing is unquantified.
  3. A sharp break in Chinese exports (low-medium; high magnitude). Domestic stimulus, a trade war, the EU hybrid cap and the VAT rebate cut. DNB's low case is −12% for 2027. It would reach the company through volume in 2027 (contracts are a share of OEM volume) and through renewal price in 2028+.
  4. Action against Chinese-built ships (low-medium; medium-high). The company's entire newbuild programme is at Jinling, and US port fees target foreign-built ships. Yard delay or cost overrun (there is an USD 80m precedent) adds execution risk.
  5. Loss or renegotiation of a major customer (low before 2029; high magnitude). Ten customers are 50% of the business; HMG is committed to 2029 and the options deferral runs to 2031.

Lesser: a vessel fire (heavier EV battery loads), KRW strength (EUKOR put +USD 81m per 10%, non-cash), Hormuz closure (2–3% of revenue; one ship trapped in Q1 2026), OEM counterparty failure, cyber.

Correlation. Risks 1 and 3 are correlated with the supply wave. The risk report's "synchronised downturn" (tariffs plus oversupply plus route normalisation by 2027) is the compound scenario to stress.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F418th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The drivers are highly concentrated: in one variable, in a few contracts and in two dates. For 2027 a single variable, the price achieved on the renewing 41% of the shipping book, produces about 85% of the rise in EBITDA and more than all of the rise in earnings per share. For 2029–2030 two renewals that fall together decide the outcome. This concentration is normally bad for predictability: the research written for this question concludes that car-carrier forecasts resting on one or two variables "have consistently failed", because routing shocks and tariffs keep overturning them. Here it has an unusual and helpful feature: the dominant variable for 2027–2028 is being fixed by contract now and will be largely visible on 17 February 2027. The case is concentrated but soon observable.

The concentration, measured (my arithmetic on the second set's bridge).

  • By variable, 2027. EBITDA rises by about 270: price net of mix, port fees and cost inflation about +230; fuel normalisation about +30; logistics about +15; everything else about −5. Earnings per share after the minority rise by about 0.42 (1.58 to 2.00): the EBITDA rise is worth about +0.53; higher depreciation and financial cost about −0.07; the 3% higher share count about −0.05. Price is the whole of it.
  • By contract. Three contracts carry about a third of Shipping revenue: HMG/EUKOR (about USD 840m a year), the equipment manufacturer (USD 950m over three years, about 317 a year) and the Asian manufacturer (USD 700m over three years, about 233 a year). Ten customers are half the business.
  • By date. The three-year contracts signed in late 2026 and the HMG contract all end on or about 31 December 2029; the first four Jinling ships arrive from mid-2030; the EUKOR put and call options become exercisable on 1 January 2031, at a price set from EUKOR's taxable results over the preceding three years. The better EUKOR earns in 2028–2030, the more the company may owe HMG in 2031, in the year its contracts reprice.
  • By outside force. Three variables set the market the 2029–2030 renewals will meet: Chinese export volume (DNB Carnegie's low case of −17% in 2027 and −21% in 2028 means 60% to 65% lower charter rates; its high case means 115% higher), the timing of the return to Suez (closed to 2029 means charter rates about 20% higher in 2027 and 50% higher in 2028), and deliveries of new ships (known: fleet growth of 6.6% in 2027 and 3.6% in 2028).

Against the last four to six years. The past was also driven by one variable, the freight rate, but through a different channel. In 2021–2024 rate and utilisation rose together across the whole market (utilisation from 58% in 2020 to 97% in 2024) and the company took the price as contracts rolled. In 2025–2026 the differences came from several costs at once (fuel lag, short-term charters, port fees, crew, emissions) and a weak Government segment: diffuse, and badly forecast by the company itself. From 2027 the difference is one price step on one tranche. The lines below EBITDA are the reverse: many small, mechanical and mostly knowable items (depreciation from deliveries, lease run-off, interest on delivery loans, the minority, the new shares). They are diffuse and individually predictable, and they are where consensus is least careful.

What this means. For 2027–2028 the single driver is contractual and will be disclosed early, so its concentration costs less predictability than it would in a spot business. For 2029–2030 the concentration is fully exposed, and the second set's figures for those years are a central path through a wide range.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5517th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The top is stable. Lasse Kristoffersen has been CEO since June 2022 and the CFO since spring 2025. The changes below them signal consolidation around insider operators, not strategic change. The one change that matters for the model is badly timed: the Chief Customer Officer changed on 1 November 2026, in the middle of the largest renewal round in a decade. It is mitigated by the successor's 36 years in the group.

Reasoning.

  • CFO. Torbjørn Wist left in July 2024. Jermund Lien was interim for about six months. Bjørnar Bukholm joined in April/May 2025; he is a returning insider (about eight years in Wallenius Wilhelmsen corporate finance, strategy and IR, then McKinsey and Sector Alarm). This was orderly, with no clustering of misses around the change.
  • Digital leadership churn. CTIO Gro Rognstad left in March 2025 and the COO of Digital Supply Chain Solutions left in July 2025. A Cognizant partnership followed. Digital transformation cost USD 12m in Q3 2026 alone, plus USD 2m in each of Q1 and Q2 and USD 12m in 2025. This signals an under-delivering digital programme, now outsourced.
  • Commercial and operations. Pia Synnerman (CCO) is leaving and supports the handover to 1 January 2027. John Felitto, COO Logistics since August 2024 (36 years of maritime experience, in the group since 2001), becomes CCO from 1 November 2026. Christian Holth (COO Supply Chain Solutions since August 2025) becomes interim COO Logistics. So Logistics, the segment with the most self-help margin targets, now has an interim head. Mike Hynekamp (Chief Strategy and Corporate Development Officer since August 2024; 17 years in the group; earned more than the CEO in 2024) provides bench depth.
  • Board. Rune Bjerke chairs. Lieve Logghe (former interim CEO of Euronav) replaced Margareta Alestig in April 2026; Line Hestvik replaced Anna Felländer in 2025. Five of seven directors are independent; 43% are women.
  • Key actors. The two families (Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding 37.87%, Wallenius Lines 37.82%) renewed their shareholder agreement (board representation, first right of refusal). HMG agreed to defer the EUKOR put and call options to 2031 (April 2026). There is no activist.

What the change signals. Continuity and an operator-led team. No change of course. Digital execution is the weak spot, and commercial continuity during the renewals rests on Felitto's credibility with customers.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D6214th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The strategy itself is unchanged since the September 2024 Capital Markets Day: an "integrated supply chain partner", "shaper not adapter", net zero by 2040. The material change in the last 6–12 months is in capital priorities. The company has moved from harvesting and deleveraging (2022–2025) to reinvesting for the 2030s: the newbuild programme rises from 14 vessels to up to 26 (4 firm plus 8 options at Jinling, from mid-2030), part-funded with USD 300m of new equity. The change is clearly articulated and financially resourced. It is thinly resourced in liquidity and in digital and logistics leadership.

Reasoning.

  • Articulated targets:
  • ROCE target over the cycle raised from 8% to 12% (Q2 2025).
  • Leverage cap lowered from below 3.5x to below 3.0x.
  • Minimum liquidity of USD 1bn introduced.
  • Logistics cash EBITDA margin from 2% to 10% by 2028.
  • 2030 carbon target upgraded to a 45% absolute reduction against 2019, from 27.5% intensity.
  • Net-zero end-to-end service pilot in 2027; Supply Chain Solutions unit (200 people, 14 countries); SG&A savings of more than USD 30m through digital standardisation (Cognizant).
  • Portfolio moves: MIRRAT (Melbourne) sold for about USD 210m with a USD 135m gain, as a public terminal with no end-to-end strategic link. Added: Gothenburg GIART (12-year concession), Drammen VPC joint venture (2027), Brunswick (20-year lease), Wallenius SOL Baltic cooperation.
  • Commercial posture changed twice. In February 2026 DNB reported a "lower-for-longer" renewal strategy, accepting lower rates for longer duration (DNB cut estimates). In November 2026 management said 2027–2029 contracts would carry rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions". My reading: the market moved so far (charter rates up 120% since late 2025) that even a duration-first posture now captures materially higher rates.
  • Fleet. The 14 Shapers were upsized (from 9,300 to 11,700 and 12,100 CEU, the largest car carriers ever) and split 7 methanol / 7 LNG dual-fuel, adding USD 80m of capex. Arctic Tern was delivered in July 2026. Post-delivery bank financing is secured for 11 of 14. The new order is 9,900 CEU ships at about USD 125m each if all 12 are taken (USD 1.5bn).
  • How well resourced. Capital: yes (banks plus USD 300m of equity at NOK 168; the family took its pro rata share). Liquidity: thin. Reserves were USD 1,026m at Q3 2026 against the USD 1bn minimum before the placement, after USD 258m of dividends in September and rising capex. People: digital leadership churn; logistics under an interim COO.

Is it sensible? Renewing an ageing fleet (59–60% of the world fleet built in 2011 or earlier) and replacing charters that now cost USD 85–110k a day is defensible. Ordering 2030 deliveries at near-peak yard prices (6,500 CEU at USD 85m, +20% against history) is pro-cyclical. Returns on the new order depend on 2030s rates (see C-50).

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D5215th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets been set or raised?

Judgement. Mixed. Near-term guidance was lowered in 2026 and has not been raised since. The mid-term ambition was raised in 2025: the ROCE target went from 8% to 12%. The other targets added conservatism rather than ambition (a lower leverage cap, a liquidity floor). There is no formal 2027 guidance.

Reasoning.

  • FY2026 adjusted EBITDA. Initial guidance was USD 1.65–1.75bn (February 2026). It was cut to "about USD 1.6bn" on 6 May 2026 because of higher net bunker and capacity (charter) costs and a slow start in Government Services. It was reiterated in August and November. The year-to-date figure (1,178) leaves about 422 for Q4 against 428 in Q3, so it is on track.
  • FY2025. The company aimed to stay "in line with 2024" (also described as flat to positive). Guidance was withdrawn in October/November 2025 when the US port fees appeared. Delivery was 1,811 against 1,901 (−4.7%).
  • FY2024. A narrowed corridor of USD 1,880–1,910m was met at 1,901.
  • Mid-term: ROCE 12% (current LTM 15.6%; 2025 18.4%); leverage below 3.0x; liquidity at least USD 1bn; logistics cash EBITDA margin 10% by 2028 (Q1 2026 was 5%; the Q2 accounting margin was 16–17%). The CEO says a quarterly logistics EBITDA "starting with a four is good, starting with a five is the target", which implies at least about USD 200m a year against about USD 160–185m now. That is an informal raise of the logistics ambition.
  • Informal raise for 2027–2029: "vast majority" of capacity to be contracted by year-end at strong market rates (see C-85). This is not formal guidance.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Reasoning.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5815th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Limited, adjacent and sensible. The company follows its customers. Chinese OEMs entering Western markets need destination processing and terminals, which is the clearest real-world case for the integrated model. The new products (Supply Chain Solutions, a net-zero end-to-end service) are small options, not bets.

Reasoning.

  • Geography: Gothenburg terminal (Nordic gateway, rail-connected, pre-delivery inspection); Drammen VPC joint venture with Bertel O. Steen (45k vehicles a year, from 2027; NOK 538m build); a Baltic link with Wallenius SOL (northern Finland and Sweden via Bremerhaven, May 2026); a Brunswick hub (20-year lease).
  • Customers: Chinese OEMs on multi-year contracts, a new customer set replacing share lost by European OEMs.
  • Product:
  • Supply Chain Solutions (2025; 200 people in 14 countries): orchestration and visibility.
  • Carbon Reduced Services with customer-funded green fuel (the USD 766m five-year H&H contract).
  • Net Zero End-to-End service pilot in 2027.
  • Syngin digital (99.5% owned).
  • Caution: an expert (formerly of Hapag-Lloyd, July 2026) warns that "full end-to-end logistics presents severe integration challenges that risk isolating existing customers". The capital at risk is small, so the downside is limited.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B+644th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement. Three phases, and the model must separate them.

  • 2021–2024: real. The net freight rate rose from USD 41.4 (2020) to 64.6 (2025), and EBIT margins rose from 18% (2022) to 33% (2024, FactSet).
  • 2025–2026: nominal. Cost inflation (bunker lag, charter-in, port fees, ETS, crew) outran the rates achieved. The brokers'-basis EBITDA margin fell from 35% to about 30%, and the net freight rate fell sequentially in Q2 2026.
  • 2027 onwards: latent but highly plausible, and increasingly stated by management. The renewal of about 40% of volume is being signed now, at terms management says reflect the current market. Real price rises are therefore likely in 2027 on that tranche, but with three dampeners, and the headline KPI will understate the like-for-like rise.

Reasoning.

  • Benchmarks for the renewal: legacy book about USD 54 per cbm (company, 17 September 2026); Höegh's new contracts above USD 100 per cbm net and its September prorated rate USD 82; spot USD 170 (+79% year on year); charter rates +120%. Management: "rates closely reflecting current strong market conditions" (Q3 2026).
  • Dampener 1: the company's own "lower-for-longer" posture (price traded for duration). The HMG/EUKOR volume (about a quarter of shipping) is fixed at about USD 55 per cbm to 2029.
  • Dampener 2: cargo-mix contamination of the KPI. More Chinese cars and less H&H lowers the average rate per cbm even when like-for-like prices rise. This may explain DNB modelling USD 60.60 for 2027–2028 against 63.40 in 2026 while still forecasting EBITDA up 13%.
  • Dampener 3: costs re-fix too. Charters cost USD 85–110k a day, and US port fees may resume from November 2026.
  • Hypothesis for the Consumer to test. A like-for-like uplift on the renewing tranche in the range of USD 10–25 per cbm above the legacy average is plausible given the gap between USD 54 and peers' new-contract rates. Each USD 10 is worth about USD 230–240m of EBITDA (preface arithmetic), which is larger than the entire consensus 2027 EBITDA increase (+USD 208m). Whether it reaches EBITDA depends on charter re-fixing and port-fee recovery.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5015th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. It is not stepping up M&A. Capital goes to ships and to small terminal and processing additions. This removes acquisition risk and offers no M&A upside. Structural moves around EUKOR (the April 2026 option deferral) are balance-sheet management, not acquisitions.

Reasoning. The only small transactions are the Gothenburg concession, the Drammen joint venture (USD 8m in Q1 2026), Syngin going to 99.5%, and the Wallenius SOL cooperation. The historic integration record (the 2017 merger of the two parents' businesses: USD 120m of synergies achieved in 16 months) shows the company can integrate. There is no sign it wants to. The capital-allocation framework (ROCE at least 12%, leverage below 3.0x) and the newbuild programme leave little room for acquisitions before 2028.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C589th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used at full stretch for two things at once: very high distributions (base payout plus extraordinaries) and a stepped-up newbuild programme. The combination ran cash down to the liquidity floor and needed a USD 300m equity raise a month after a USD 258m dividend. Distributions are high but of mixed quality. There are no buybacks at company level, and the dividend-then-raise round trip dilutes minority holders who do not take part.

Reasoning.

  • Policy: a semi-annual "pay-as-you-go" dividend of 30–50% of net profit, plus extraordinaries.
  • 2025: USD 892m declared (USD 2.11 a share, including a USD 200m extraordinary); USD 989m of cash dividends paid.
  • H1 2026: USD 0.61 a share, USD 258m including a USD 100m extraordinary, paid September 2026. That is 82% of H1 net profit (another source says 50%).
  • DNB expects about NOK 40–43 a share cumulatively through 2028. Consensus DPS is 1.37/1.58/1.35 (payment-period basis).
  • Balance-sheet trajectory: cash USD 1,705m (2023) → 1,393m → 1,071m → 526m (Q3 2026). Net interest-bearing debt (including leases) USD 1,729m (end-2025) → 2,119m (Q3 2026); leverage 1.0x → 1.3x. Liquidity reserves USD 2.0bn (end-2025) → 1,437m (March 2026) → 1,026m (Q3 2026), against the USD 1.0bn minimum. In October came the USD 300m placement (17.15m shares at NOK 168) to "partly fund the expanded newbuilding programme".
  • Capex steps up: 2026 year to date USD 349m; consensus 567m (2026), 742m (2027), 506m (2028), against 151–248m in 2023–2025.
  • Buybacks: none at company level. Buybacks are left to the parent.
  • What to model. Base dividends continue. Extraordinary payouts in 2027 are less likely while capex peaks (USD 665m Shaper plus Jinling deposits). Net debt drifts up in 2027 and falls from 2028 as Shaper capex ends and contracted cash arrives. That year is the likely free-cash-flow and dividend inflection.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–63

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Four are material:

  1. The EUKOR option deferral to 2031 (April 2026). The put and call cannot be exercised while the HMG contract commits at least 50% of HMG's exports. The liability was reclassified to non-current and fell by about USD 465m (to about USD 386m), lifting the equity ratio to 47–49%. This removes a balance-sheet overhang and ties HMG's volume to the company until at least 2029–2031.
  2. The Shaper fleet arriving in 2026–2028. Each delivery lowers unit cost (largest ships in the industry, 27–40% better fuel efficiency) and replaces charters costing USD 85–110k a day.
  3. Logistics self-help: a record Q2, a quarterly EBITDA target of USD 50m, USD 30m+ of SG&A savings, and new terminals.
  4. Asset values as a cash source. Second-hand values are high (Asian Captain gain USD 40m; Grand Uranus option USD 50m below market) and newbuild prices are 16–20% above average, which makes the owned fleet's replacement value support the equity.

Also plausible: green-premium contracts monetising the dual-fuel fleet, and a first formal 2027 guidance at Q4 2026 results that reflects the renewal round.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C558th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Very little scope for change, and the controlling actors' signals all point to continuity: high distributions plus support for fleet investment.

Reasoning.

  • The families (75.69% combined) renewed their shareholder agreement, which secures board representation and first right of refusal and "prevents potential activist investor pressure". Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding subscribed its 37% pro rata in the October placement. WWH relies on Wallenius Wilhelmsen for almost 50% of its free cash flow to equity and is itself pursuing buybacks and closing its holding discount, so it wants steady dividends from the company.
  • HMG (20% of EUKOR) accepted the option deferral to 2031 in exchange for the volume commitment.
  • No activist and no public dissent. The structure (single share class, no anti-takeover provisions, 24–25% free float) leaves a theoretical take-private route, but there is no evidence of intent.
  • For the model: assume the dividend policy (30–50% plus extraordinaries when cash exceeds needs) and the fleet programme continue. Do not assume buybacks.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6114th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Moderately strong, fairly broad, and resourced in capital more than in people or liquidity. The most valuable internal action is commercial: locking 2027–2029 volumes at peak-cycle terms. Behind it come the EUKOR deferral, the stricter financial framework and fleet renewal. Logistics self-help is real but small relative to shipping. The negatives (guidance cut, liquidity at the floor, the equity raise, digital churn) are about execution and timing. The structure that converts a cyclical peak into contracted multi-year cash is largely in place.

Reasoning. The breadth covers targets (ROCE 12%, leverage below 3.0x, liquidity floor), commercial (multi-year China contracts, USD 1.7bn of awards after Q3), balance sheet (RCF refinancing, EUKOR), fleet (Shaper 14 plus 4 + 8), logistics (terminals, margin targets) and green (SBTi, Equinor bio-methanol). Resourcing: bank financing for 11 of 14 Shapers and USD 300m of equity, but liquidity at the floor before the raise, the digital team rebuilt through an outsourcer, and Logistics under an interim COO. The quality of the changes is sound. Their earnings power depends mostly on the external cycle and on the renewal rates, which the company controls only partly.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C+606th of 25

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead within 6 months, 6–18 months and 18–36 months, how novel is each, and how well founded?

Judgement. There is a credible staircase, dense in the next six months and well founded on contracts. Most steps are already anticipated by the market and sell-side, so their novelty is moderate. The two steps with genuine novelty and good foundation are: (1) the first formal 2027 guidance and evidence of renewal pricing (February 2027), and (2) the 2028 free-cash-flow inflection as Shaper capex ends. Negative steps interleave on the same dates.

Within 6 months (to May 2027):

  • US port-fee decision (around 10 November 2026). Binary. Relief (another suspension or high recovery) would be novel and positive; resumption would be negative. Foundation: unknown.
  • Completion of the 2027 renewal round (target: "vast majority" of 2027–2029 by end-2026), with further awards announced with values like the USD 700m and USD 950m deals. Novelty medium; well founded (two awards already).
  • Q4 2026 results, 17 February 2027.
  • FY2026 delivery against about USD 1.6bn.
  • The first rise in shipping backlog value and duration in five quarters.
  • First formal 2027 EBITDA guidance. High novelty if it is above USD 1.83bn.
  • H2 dividend (an extraordinary is less likely after the raise).
  • The Asian Captain gain (USD 40m).
  • Shaper deliveries continuing; the IMO Net-Zero Framework vote.
  • Negatives on the same calendar: the EU hybrid cap (1 December 2026) and the China VAT rebate cut (1 April 2027).

Months 6–18 (mid-2027 to mid-2028):

  • Repriced contracts appear in reported net freight rates and EBITDA. Novelty: medium. Well founded if Q4 guidance confirms.
  • Logistics EBITDA reaching USD 50m a quarter.
  • The net-zero end-to-end service pilot (2027).
  • Drammen VPC opening (2027).
  • The Shaper fleet growing (fewer charters).
  • Negatives: about 50 ships (460k CEU) delivered industry-wide in 2027; utilisation falling from 98% (DNB's August path: 85% in 2027); possible Suez normalisation.

Months 18–36 (mid-2028 to 2029):

  • The Shaper programme ends (2028): capex falls from about USD 742m (2027 consensus) to about 506m (2028) and lower thereafter, until Jinling instalments resume ahead of mid-2030 deliveries. Free cash flow inflects, which supports dividends. Novel in magnitude; well founded.
  • Industry fleet growth falls to 0.5% (2029) with scrapping waves (Pareto: 2029).
  • The HMG/EUKOR contract (USD 55 per cbm, through 2029) comes up for renewal. The next large repricing step if the market holds. Novel; foundation depends on the 2028–2029 market.
  • IMO and MEPC global fuel standards (2028) favour the dual-fuel fleet.
  • Decisions on the 8 Jinling options.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5215th of 25

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5215th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The company is a top-two player in a concentrated oligopoly with high entry costs, direct OEM relationships, owned terminals, and customers currently paying for security of capacity. Those are the ingredients of a good industry at the moment. The structure has three weaknesses that history says matter when the cycle turns: no exit (zero scrapping), a wave of new entrants with captive or state cargo that is least likely to price rationally, and demand concentrated on one policy-exposed source. The support is therefore strong through 2027, at its most fragile around 2028, and could re-tighten from 2029–2030 as fleet growth collapses and old tonnage leaves. On stock-market precedent the company fits the late-cycle pattern (multiples at the top of their range, estimates upgraded deeply). On operating precedent it fits the resilient contracted-carrier pattern. For the model, industry structure argues for holding 2027 pricing, stressing 2028–2029 renewal rates and volumes, and treating charter costs as a partial natural hedge.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B+663rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Helping, with low fragility. The company sells direct to OEMs under contracts of affreightment, so forwarders are marginal (rate agreements, breakbulk, spot fill). It owns or controls key intermediary nodes itself (8 terminals, about 70 processing centres). The main hindrances are at ports outside its control: congestion, stevedoring costs and port fees.

Reasoning. Owned terminals give berth priority and fast turnaround, and capture the highest-margin stage of the chain (terminal services: an estimated 45% gross and 18% EBIT margin; 36% EBITDA in FY2023 logistics). Destination processing is what Chinese OEMs need to scale in Europe, the Americas and Africa. Hindrances: US West Coast stevedoring (a USD 12m retroactive charge), Zeebrugge congestion, ETS and port fees. Shipbrokers (for example Clarksons) are relevant for chartering and sale-and-purchase, not cargo.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D5514th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In the short term competitors help. Their high new-contract rates (Höegh above USD 100 per cbm, extending a major Asian producer to 2029 for USD 300m) put a price umbrella over the company's renewals. Their fleet expansion (Höegh to 18–26 Aurora ships, Glovis +20%, MOL to 130–150, NYK's dual-fuel deliveries) hurts from 2027–2028. The company's position against them is solid in Atlantic, H&H and integrated trades and weaker on China-origin spot cargo, where Glovis and Chinese carriers are positioning.

Reasoning. Höegh's Q2 2026 results show the cost pain affecting all carriers: EBITDA −16% quarter on quarter, fuel +39%, a 5–6 month BAF lag, and the dividend cut from USD 94m to 16m. The company's shorter BAF lag (2–4 months) and lower Middle East exposure (2–3% of revenue against 17% of Höegh's flows) are relative advantages. Glovis deploys ultra-large ships for "high-margin spot cargo negotiations with Chinese OEMs". That makes it the most direct competitor for incremental China volume.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4717th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption is acting on every node at once, mostly in ways that favour the company until 2027 and threaten it in 2028–2029. The company's position is most fragile at one intersection: new supply on the water in 2027–2028 meeting policy-dampened Chinese exports, with Suez normalisation as an accelerant.

By node:

  • Demand (OEMs): Chinese OEMs export (+) but localise and face tariffs (−). Western OEMs lose share and volume (−). OEMs adapt to containers (−, ceiling).
  • Competition: Chinese entrants and Glovis (−); incumbents disciplined while sold out (+).
  • Regulators and politicians: trade barriers (−); green rules (+ relative); port fees (−).
  • Suppliers: yards (pricing power; slots 2030; China concentration) (−, cost and risk); charter owners (rates +120%) (−); green-fuel scarcity (−).
  • Other: geopolitics absorbs capacity (+ now, − when it ends); port congestion absorbs capacity (+ now).
