C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B725th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where does the company rank by market share or size, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. Wallenius Wilhelmsen is the world's number one or two deep-sea car-carrier operator. On DNB Carnegie's end-2025 capacity data it is number one, with a 16.1% capacity share against NYK's 14.8%. It is also the largest integrated ocean-plus-land vehicle logistics provider, with 8 terminals and about 70 processing centres. Its lead is narrow. Its capacity share has been drifting down because it did not join the 2022–2024 ordering wave, and Hyundai Glovis's fleet grows 20% in 2027.

Reasoning.

  • Fleets:
  • Wallenius Wilhelmsen: about 121–129 vessels (121 in shipping at Q1 2026, 80 owned and 41 chartered in, plus 10 Government).
  • NYK: 117–134. MOL: 95–109, with a plan for 130–150 by 2035. K-Line: 99.
  • Hyundai Glovis: 96–97, rising to about 120 in 2027 and 128 by 2030.
  • Höegh: 39–44 (6% share, sixth largest; largest average vessel at about 7,500 CEU).
  • Grimaldi and Chinese operators (about 10% of the fleet including orderbook).
  • The drift (my inference). The often-quoted "20% market share" is older. The world fleet grew 8.4% in 2024 and 13.1% in 2025 while the company's fleet was roughly flat, which is consistent with a fall to about 16% of capacity. Revenue is USD 5.24bn, 3.67 times Höegh's USD 1.43bn.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C+667th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How concentrated is the market (top five above 30%?) and how steep is the share distribution?

Judgement. Highly concentrated at the top and getting less steep. The top five hold about 64.4% of capacity (end-2025), and the top carriers more than 70% of deep-sea capacity. The ratio of the top five to numbers six to ten is roughly 2.5–3 times (my estimate), not 5 times, because a second tier is growing fast: Höegh at about 6%, Grimaldi, MSC (Gram), and Chinese state and OEM operators (COSCO, CMES, BYD, SAIC Anji).

Reasoning. Entry barriers are high: newbuild prices of USD 85–135m per vessel, 40–50 vessels needed for a weekly global network, terminal access, and yard slots unavailable until 2030. That keeps the top tier intact. The tail is being filled by actors with captive or state cargo, not by small independents, which matters for behaviour in a downturn (C-29).

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4819th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Shares were stable among the Japanese, Korean and Scandinavian incumbents for a decade. In the last three years new entrants have taken the largest share shift in the industry's modern history. Chinese operators reached about 10% of the global fleet including orderbook (29 deliveries in 2025 alone), and MSC entered by buying Gram Car Carriers (18 vessels, late 2024). Wallenius Wilhelmsen has ceded capacity share by choice (price over volume) and has not lost customers.

Reasoning.

  • New-entrant evidence: BYD (8 ships, about 1m vehicles a year), SAIC Anji (9,500-vehicle ships on European routes), COSCO (target of 30 ships, converted multipurpose ships), CMES (RoRo profit up 687% year on year in Q3 2026), AD Ports/Ningbo (United Global Ro-Ro), CMA CGM via CEVA.
  • Customer retention evidence: the 2024 renewal season (45% of volume) and the 2025 season (more than 70% locked early). The early European OEM extension to 2031, the ten-year logistics contract from April 2027 (USD 2bn) and the USD 700m and USD 950m+ awards in November 2026.
  • What to watch: whether Chinese entrants keep growing into 2027–2028, when Chinese export growth may slow. That is when share instability would turn into price instability.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5518th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does market share consolidate towards the largest or the most advantaged competitors, and is the company among them?

Judgement. The industry is not consolidating towards the largest; it is fragmenting at the margin. Advantage is accruing to two groups: (a) carriers with captive or state-anchored cargo (Glovis with HMG; Chinese state and OEM fleets), and (b) owners of the newest, largest, multi-fuel ships (Höegh Aurora, and Wallenius Wilhelmsen's Shapers from 2026–2028). Wallenius Wilhelmsen belongs to the second group from 2026–2028 and partly to the first through HMG/EUKOR (at least 50% of HMG exports). It is among the advantaged, but not as the clear winner.

Reasoning.

  • Höegh shows the advantage of large modern pure-play ships: a 43.5% EBITDA margin and 26% ROIC against Wallenius Wilhelmsen's 34.4% and 17.9% (2025).
  • Integrated operators gain stickiness: 70% of the company's top 50 customers buy both shipping and logistics.
  • Glovis gains captive volume and is targeting Chinese spot cargo with ultra-large ships.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5015th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition (margin and return first, or volume first), and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. The incumbents put margin and returns first, and in this upcycle have been disciplined price takers of scarcity. The new entrants are volume-first or strategy-first. The record is clear that industry prices fall when volume falls, with a lag at contract renewal. This industry was also a cartel within living memory.

Reasoning.

  • Margin-first behaviour:
  • About 80–90% of volumes are under contracts of affreightment.
  • Wallenius Wilhelmsen declined unacceptable renewals in 2024.
  • Höegh's CEO says it will "be very careful about committing to rates substantially above newbuild parity" on charters.
  • Volume-first behaviour:
  • Glovis's 20% fleet growth (2027) aimed at Chinese spot cargo.
  • Chinese operators securing national export logistics.
  • Höegh's own precedent of renewing "at lower rates to secure backlog" (DNB, February 2025), which ended its outperformance.
  • Prices fall when volumes fall: in 2018 there was overcapacity pressure. In 2020, ocean volumes fell 45% in Q2 and charter rates bottomed at USD 12,625 a day. The company's net freight rate was USD 41.4 per cbm in 2020, against 64.6 in 2025.
  • Cartel history: the company settled its remaining antitrust cases with USD 149m paid in 2021. Höegh paid USD 21m to the US in 2017; Brazil fined it BRL 26m in 2022 (under appeal); there are Canadian class actions. Past "benign cooperation" was partly illegal, and current discipline must come from market structure, not coordination.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4516th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last 18 months to three years? Would competitors launch a "shark attack" if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. Pricing attitude has been stable because everyone is sold out. Capacity attitude was unstable: the orderbook went from low single digits to a 42.4% peak in July 2024. The new Chinese entrants are the situation-dependent, state-directed type of competitor ("Michael O'Leary instability"). In a 2028–2029 downturn they are the most likely to attack prices on China-origin lanes to keep ships full. That risk is real but not imminent.

Reasoning. A shark attack needs spare capacity, and none exists today (utilisation 96–98%, container spillover, yard slots from 2030). Wisdom Library: "the worst pricing comes from the rivals least able to exit". With zero scrapping in 2024–2025 and state-backed owners, exit will be slow when the cycle turns. Glovis's behaviour is predictable (it serves HMG first). Japanese majors are conglomerates (NYK, MOL and K-Line earn car-carrier profits inside diversified groups; MOL's car-carrier earnings fell 93% year on year in Q2 2026) and could choose either discipline or volume. Their behaviour in 2027–2028 is the swing factor among incumbents.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5514th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Supportive today and probably through 2027. Fragile in 2028–2029. It could tighten again from 2029–2030. The structure is an oligopoly with high entry barriers but no exit, whose demand is concentrated on one policy-exposed source (Chinese exports) and whose supply is arriving now.

Reasoning.

  • Supports: top-five concentration of about 64%; capital and network barriers; long contracts; terminals; yard slots from 2030; fleet growth falling to 2.8% (end-2028) and 0.5% (end-2029); an ageing fleet (about 10% over 30 years old by 2030) facing tighter carbon rules.
  • Fragilities: about 460k CEU (about 50 ships) delivering in 2027; Chinese entrants; Glovis +20%; Suez normalisation could release 4–7% of capacity; contracts set a share of OEM volume with no take-or-pay minimums; DNB's August path has utilisation at 85% (2027) and 80% (2028–2029), above the 75% breakeven but well below today's 98%.
  • Timing (the key insight for the model): the structure's weakest year is likely 2028. That is after the company has locked most of its 2027–2029 volume, and before supply growth collapses and scrapping arrives.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F4220th of 25full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4821st of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitutes are rising in volume but currently act as a reservoir for, not a drain on, the car-carrier market. Their lasting effect is a price ceiling and a lower long-run demand ceiling.

Reasoning.

  • Containers and flat racks: 1.0–1.5m cars in H1 2026, and 25–30% of China's deep-sea exports. COSCO's converted pulp carriers; Malaysia (13.7% of car transit in containers). OEMs prefer car carriers (handling and damage), so volumes return as capacity frees (Höegh: a deficit of about 74 ships). But some OEMs may adapt permanently (NH: this would "permanently lower the demand ceiling").
  • Local production and near-shoring: the true long-run substitute for deep-sea shipping (BYD Hungary and Brazil; USMCA; EU local-content proposals).
  • Overland: Mexico-to-US trucking and rail.
  • Trend: rising.

Group judgement

Company — Competitive advantage

D5814th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The company has a genuine but mid-ranking franchise. Its advantages are relationships and integration (stickiness), owned terminals (the highest-margin node), and from 2026–2028 the industry's largest and most efficient ships. Its pricing power is real and being used now, but it comes from the cycle, not from structure, and the company takes less of the peak than Höegh in exchange for duration. Scale makes it a market leader without giving it superior unit economics. For the model: competitive advantage justifies assuming high renewal success and customer retention through 2029, and a margin floor above the 2010s average because of fleet modernisation. It does not justify assuming that peak-cycle margins persist once utilisation falls.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D5817th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The company has a moderate, real but not dominant set of advantages. The strongest are the integrated land–sea network with owned terminals (production and customer related) and long OEM relationships. A modern-fleet cost advantage is now arriving as the Shapers deliver. Scale gives breadth more than lower cost: Höegh, a smaller pure play, earns structurally higher margins. The advantages are widening in fleet efficiency and China contracting, and narrowing in capacity share.

By type:

  • Customer related (moderate; slightly widening).
  • Multi-decade OEM relationships and multi-year contracts of affreightment (about 90% of ocean volume).
  • Cross-buying: 70% of the top 50 customers buy shipping and logistics together, so switching is a multi-service decision.
  • Processing centres and Syngin integrated into OEM supply chains.
  • Caveat: switching costs are flattered by today's shortage, and contracts set a share of volume, not take-or-pay.
  • Production related (moderate today; widening 2026–2028).
  • 8 terminals (Brunswick 20-year lease; Gothenburg 12-year concession) give berth priority and the highest-margin value-chain stage.
  • A 15-route network with joint planning and asset swapping reduces ballast legs.
  • The Shaper fleet: the largest car carriers built (11,700–12,100 CEU), 27–40% more fuel-efficient, dual-fuel, ordered at about USD 11k per CEU against about USD 13k at today's 6,500 CEU price.
  • Ramp and deck capability for H&H.
  • Scale (moderate; not translating into a cost lead). Number one or two by capacity, but EBITDA margin 34.4% and ROIC 17.9% against Höegh's 43.5% and 26.6% (2025). The Wisdom Library test "scale needs a bounded market" is partly met (a specialised fleet, a terminal network, about 40–50 ships needed for a weekly network), but the scale gap to NYK, MOL and Glovis is small.
  • Regulatory or political (modest). US-flag Government Services (MSP subsidy USD 10m per ship a year); the HMG/EUKOR relationship (at least 50% of HMG exports, protected by the option deferral to 2031); SBTi-validated green credentials.
  • Culture and knowledge (modest to moderate). Deep RoRo and H&H know-how, high engagement scores; digital capability weak.

Hard against soft (Wisdom Library "Moats and SRCA"). Hard advantages are B-/C+; soft advantages are C+. A durable but not top-decile franchise.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C-609th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The company is large in absolute terms: revenue USD 5.24bn (3.67 times Höegh), about 121–129 vessels, 8 terminals, 70 processing centres, 28 countries, about 3.0m units handled at terminals, and 3,666 seafarers through managers. Scale confers network breadth, the ability to offer global and end-to-end contracts, flexible fleet deployment (moving ships from the Atlantic to Asia), purchasing scale at yards (a 14-ship series), and access to bank financing. It does not confer lower unit cost than the best competitors.

Reasoning. Against the other majors (NYK, MOL, K-Line, Glovis) scale is comparable, so scale is a ticket to compete, not a winning edge. Against Höegh, Grimaldi and single-OEM Chinese fleets, scale wins global tenders and gives multi-trade backhaul optimisation. The integrated model's buffer value shows in downturns (logistics EBITDA USD 128m in 2025; record USD 46m in Q2 2026). The Wisdom Library test "linked businesses are not a conglomerate" is partly passed: the linkages are real (cross-selling, berth priority, backhaul planning). But a well-funded pure play (Höegh) attacks the ocean division alone and earns more, which suggests the integration is worth more in stickiness and in downturns than in peak margin.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C-6410th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Inferiority is disproved, and leadership is plausible in integrated, H&H and Atlantic services. Customers keep extending early, for longer, and across both services, in a market where they could shop around.

Reasoning.

  • Evidence it is not inferior: more than 70% of 2025 volume locked early; early extension with a European OEM to 2031; the ten-year USD 2bn logistics contract; the November 2026 awards (USD 700m and USD 950m + 100m); HMG's commitment of at least 50% of exports; 70% of the top 50 cross-buying.
  • Where it leads: end-to-end land plus sea (terminals, processing, inland), H&H handling, breadth of trade lanes, green offering (Shaper, Carbon Reduced Services, SBTi).
  • Where it does not lead: price per cbm against captive or Chinese carriers; China-origin spot availability (it prioritises contract customers); industry-wide schedule reliability.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C+606th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of relative customer utility, shown by evidence of application?

Judgement. Pricing power is real, applied, and cyclical rather than structural. It comes from capacity scarcity, not from a structural moat. Its application is demonstrable (2021–2024 repricing; the 2026 renewals now under way) and has not eroded customer utility (customers keep extending). But the company deliberately takes less than the market's full price in exchange for duration ("lower-for-longer"). When the market loosened in the past, prices fell.

Evidence of application:

  • Net freight rate up 56% from 2020 to 2025 (USD 41.4 → 64.6).
  • The 2024 renewal season (45% of volume) at higher rates, accepting volume losses (−8%).
  • The HMG contract (late 2024) at USD 55 per cbm, "historically high" when signed.
  • BAF 2.0 and ETS surcharges passed through.
  • Customer-funded green premiums (the USD 766m H&H contract).
  • Q3 2026: net freight rates rose again. Management: 2027–2029 contracts at rates "closely reflecting current strong market".

Limits:

  • Net rate (USD 61–64) is about 20–29% below Höegh's (79–82). This is partly cargo mix, partly Höegh's faster repricing.
  • The legacy book is about USD 54.
  • 2018–2020 shows rates fall with utilisation (charter USD 12,625 a day; net rate 41.4).
  • The legacy HMG block is fixed to 2029.

For the model. Price is the main earnings driver for 2027–2029. Model it as renewal-tranche repricing (about 40% of volume for 2027, the rest phased by contract expiry), with the HMG block flat to 2029. Use a like-for-like uplift separate from the mix effect.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B+554th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement. Partly, de facto, not as a stated philosophy. It shares through duration-first pricing ("lower-for-longer"), through BAF mechanics (Shaper fuel efficiency lowers customers' surcharges per unit), and through co-designed green services. Management also states it "does not concede pricing terms without getting commercial value in return". This is a measured, not a Costco-style, sharing. It builds goodwill for downturns (customers defer rather than cancel volumes; the CEO describes working with customers to "defer or move commitments") without giving away the cycle.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C-5712th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Mild positive mechanical forces work for the company relative to older-fleet peers over 2026–2030. They are roughly neutral against Höegh, which is renewing faster with ammonia-capable ships.

Mechanical tailwinds:

  1. Each Shaper delivery (2026–2028) lowers cost per CEU and replaces charters costing USD 85–110k a day.
  2. Contract repricing happens automatically at renewal (2027 tranche; the HMG block in 2029–2030).
  3. Tightening carbon rules (ETS 100%, FuelEU, CII, MEPC 2028) progressively disadvantage the 59–60% of the world fleet built in 2011 or earlier.
  4. The yard ceiling (slots from 2030) limits new supply after 2028.
  5. The logistics standardisation programme.

Counter-gravity:

  1. Industry fleet growth in 2026–2027 and Chinese entrants.
  2. Reversion of the routing premium.
  3. Mid-cycle reversion of rates at the 2028+ renewals.
  4. Rising D&A and interest from the newbuild programme, which reduce the EPS conversion of EBITDA gains.

Management quality

Group judgement

Company — Management

C599th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

A competent, disciplined, plain-spoken management team led by a strong operator CEO. It has repaired the balance sheet, removed the EUKOR overhang, renewed the fleet at good prices and is locking in the cycle through contracts. It is not an outlier team. It trails its pure-play peer on margins and returns, is weak in digital, prefers duration over peak price, and allocates capital in a way shaped by its controlling families (high payouts, no buybacks, equity raised when cash ran short). For the model, management quality supports high confidence in execution of contracts, guidance and fleet delivery. It does not justify assuming the company outperforms the cycle. The behaviour to expect in a downturn is decisive cost and fleet action (charter redelivery, lay-ups) and dividend cuts, as in 2020.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5416th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How big is the company's ambition or vision, particularly against peers?

Judgement. Moderate and disciplined, not outsized. The vision ("integrated supply chain partner", "shaper not adapter", net zero by 2040, the industry's largest and greenest ships) is coherent. But growth is not a priority: the company has let its capacity share drift down, and peers are expanding faster (Glovis +20% in 2027, Höegh to 18–26 Aurora ships, MOL to 130–150 vessels). This is a returns-first steward of a cyclical franchise, not a share-taker.

Reasoning. The ambitions are framed as returns (ROCE 12%), resilience (leverage below 3.0x, liquidity at least USD 1bn), distribution (30–50% payout plus extraordinaries) and decarbonisation leadership. The newbuild extension (4 firm plus 8 options for 2030–2031) is replacement and modernisation, not market-share growth. Höegh moved first and further on next-generation fuels (ammonia vessels in 2027–2028). One digest records the company's "delayed fleet renewal commitment relative to Höegh" as a weakness.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C6210th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy: right and bold priorities, widening the moat, growth, and dynamic enough for a plausible range of environments?

Judgement. A smart and robust strategy for this cycle, more right than bold. Its best feature is how well it handles different environments: long contracts, low leverage, about one third of capacity chartered (flexible), a logistics buffer and a modern fleet arriving. That keeps the company solvent and paying dividends in a downturn and earning well at the peak. Its weakness is upside capture. The duration-first pricing posture and slower fleet growth cap participation in extreme peaks.

Reasoning.

  • Right priorities:
  • Lock duration at peak (USD 1.7bn of awards after Q3; most of 2027–2029 to be contracted by end-2026).
  • Renew the fleet with large dual-fuel ships (Shapers ordered before the price boom).
  • Own terminals, which are the high-margin, sticky node.
  • Serve Chinese OEMs with destination logistics.
  • Fix the balance sheet (EUKOR deferral, RCFs).
  • Moat-widening: modest (fleet efficiency, integration, multi-year China relationships).
  • Gaps:
  • Digital (a churned team, now outsourced to Cognizant).
  • Growth (no capacity expansion beyond replacement until 2030).
  • New ships ordered at near-peak 2030 prices.
  • Wisdom Library check ("Customers' capital discipline is the asset owners' scarcity"): the length of the pricing window is set by build time (slots from 2030) and contract structure (a long backlog delays repricing). The company's long-contract strategy means it captures the window later but holds it longer than spot carriers. That is a sensible trade at the top of a cycle.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C-5510th of 25

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company put shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent is its governance?

Judgement. Shareholders broadly come first, but "shareholders" means mainly the controlling families. Minority interests are protected by form (single share class, five of seven directors independent, no anti-takeover provisions, cost-plus-5% related-party pricing, malus and clawback) more than by power (75.69% family control, a shareholder agreement with first right of refusal). Governance is prudent but not over-engineered. The October 2026 private placement, after a large extraordinary dividend, favoured holders able to take part.

Evidence.

  • For: modest CEO pay; an LTIP on ROCE and relative TSR; no executive on the board; a clean auditor change to EY that surfaced the EUKOR liability; antitrust matters settled (USD 149m in 2021) with no open investigations; an ISO 37000-based compliance framework.
  • Against or caution:
  • 75.69% family voting control means minorities cannot influence allocation.
  • The parent depends on dividends (almost 50% of WWH's free cash flow to equity), which biases policy to payouts over buybacks.
  • No buybacks even when P/B was about 0.7x historically.
  • The private placement at NOK 168 (pro rata for WWH) diluted non-participants.
  • Related-party ship management and services.
  • A 2024 internal-control weakness.

Company — Economic strength

D5511th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

On today's numbers the business is economically strong: ROCE about 16–18%, OCF/EBITDA about 95%, low leverage (1.3x), a structurally low tax rate, and EBITDA margins of about 30–35%. But its earnings are a cyclical peak, made on almost flat revenue. EPS is about four times as sensitive to EBITDA as EBITDA is to revenue, because of about USD 0.9bn of fixed D&A and interest. For the model, the economic strength lies in how much above-mid-cycle cash is contracted for 2027–2029 and how much it can distribute, not in growth or a permanently high return. Model these carefully:

  1. Repricing by contract tranche.
  2. Charter-cost re-fixing against Shaper replacement.
  3. D&A steps with deliveries.
  4. Minority leakage of about 10%.
  5. About 440m shares from Q4 2026.
  6. Capex of 567 / 742 / 506 with lease outflows deducted for equity FCF.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F4521st of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. EPS rose strongly from 2022 to 2025 with almost no revenue growth, driven by price-led margin expansion and deleveraging. That growth is not durable. Consensus already has EPS 25% lower in 2026, up 20% in 2027 and down 12% in 2028. EPS is extremely sensitive to the mid-cycle EBITDA assumption, because about USD 0.9bn of D&A and interest sits between EBITDA and pre-tax profit.

Reasoning.

  • Reported basic EPS: 0.22 (2019), −0.68 (2020), 0.32, 1.61, 2.02, 2.30, 2.41 (2025). Brokers' basis: 1.88 / 2.31 / 2.20 (2023–2025). Revenue 2022–2025 was roughly flat (5,045 → 5,240).
  • Consensus (brokers' basis): 1.66 / 1.99 / 1.75 for 2026–2028. FY2027 includes about 4% more shares. Note that a FY2026 EPS of 1.66 implies roughly USD 0.54 in Q4 against 0.45 reported in Q3. The USD 40m Asian Captain gain (about USD 0.09 a share) may explain the gap, if brokers include it; the Consumer should reconcile.
  • Mid-cycle EPS sensitivity (my arithmetic; D&A about 720, net finance about 180, tax about 4%, minorities about 10%, 440m shares):
Mid-cycle EBITDA Approximate EPS
USD 1.0bn about USD 0.20
USD 1.4bn (DNB's new long-term figure) about USD 0.98
USD 1.8bn (2027 consensus level) about USD 1.75

The equity value therefore depends on how many years of contracted above-mid-cycle earnings are banked, more than on any steady-state EPS.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4720th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The scope is mostly replacement at good returns, not growth. The Shapers (ordered at about USD 1.6bn for 14 ships) earn high returns against the alternative of chartering. Returns on the new 4 + 8 order (about USD 1.5bn, deliveries from mid-2030, near-peak yard prices) depend on 2030s rates and are uncertain. Logistics and terminal investments are small but high-return. With a mature market and low-growth reinvestment, the fair multiple is a cyclical one: a low mid-cycle multiple applied to mid-cycle earnings plus contracted excess cash, not a compounder multiple.

Reasoning (my rough unit economics, for testing). A Shaper (about USD 115m, about 10,500 CEU average) carries about 1.6–1.8 times the capacity of a 6,500 CEU ship. At DNB's long-term equivalent of about USD 40k a day per 6,500 CEU unit, its cash earnings support a pre-tax ROIC in the low to mid teens. Against chartering at USD 85–110k a day, ownership saves far more. Höegh puts its newbuild cash cost about 68% below the charter market. The new Jinling ships cost about USD 12.6k per CEU against about 11k for the Shapers. At 2030s rates near DNB's long-term level, they would earn roughly the company's 12% ROCE target, not more. Logistics: terminals earn 36% EBITDA margins; Gothenburg and Drammen are small. DNB's valuation already uses 7x EV/EBITDA on long-term EBITDA of about USD 1.4bn (raised from USD 1.0bn). That upgrade of normalised earnings, not near-term EPS, drove most of the move in DNB's target from NOK 150 to NOK 210.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C609th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Current returns are high (ROCE about 16–18%, after-tax ROIC about 18%) and above the 12% target. They are cyclical, falling from their 2024 peak, and well below Höegh's. Over a full cycle (2010–2019) returns were below the cost of capital. Returns on tangible capital are essentially the same as ROCE (few intangibles). ROE is inflated by the EUKOR-reduced book (30.8% in 2025).

Reasoning.

  • Adjusted ROCE: 17.9% (2023), 19.9% (2024), 18.4% (2025), 17.4% (Q1 2026), 15.6% (Q2 2026 LTM). DNB 2026e: 15.5%.
  • After-tax ROIC: 4.7% (2019) → 17.9% (2025).
  • Peers: Höegh ROIC 26%, ROCE 26.6% in 2025 (19.3% in 2026e); NYK ROIC 7.6% (FY2025).
  • Incremental returns: Shaper returns are high against chartering. Returns on peak-priced newbuilds for the 2030s are about at target (C-50).
  • Incentives: LTIP vesting starts at 13% three-year average ROCE and peaks at 17%. In 2026–2028 this is likely to be met through the cycle rather than through management action.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

B624th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Conversion before capex is strong (operating cash flow / EBITDA 94–96% in 2024–2025; OCF about 1.6 times net income). Free cash flow after capex will compress in 2026–2027 as Shaper capex peaks, then rise strongly in 2028. Working capital is stable over the cycle but swings within a year with fuel inventories. Two traps: FactSet's historical FCF line is after dividends, and no FCF measure deducts lease payments on about USD 1.5bn of lease liabilities.

Reasoning.

  • OCF: 1,623 / 1,644 / 1,640 (2023–2025). Cash conversion (company): 94% (2024), 96% (2025); 83% (Q1 2026), 72% (Q2), 110% (Q3, fuel inventory released).
  • Pre-dividend FCF (OCF − capex): about 1,472 / 1,449 / 1,395 (2023–2025), against reported net income of 853 / 972 / 1,018.
  • Consensus FCF: 840 / 961 / 1,196 (2026–2028), with capex of 567 / 742 / 506.
  • Leases: USD 505m of new vessel leases were added to lease liabilities in 2025. Lease principal is a financing outflow and should be deducted for an equity-FCF view.
  • Net working capital: 10–13% of LTM revenue (USD 543–700m); about 39 days; cash conversion cycle 23 days (2024) against Höegh's 17. Fuel inventory builds when routes lengthen or prices spike.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

D5516th of 25

Judgement. Margins are high but past their peak. Gross margin (FactSet) peaked at 41.6% in 2024. EBIT margins fall further than EBITDA margins because D&A rises with the Shapers (651 → 720).

Reasoning.

  • FactSet gross margin: 39.8% / 41.6% / 38.4% (2023–2025); Q2 2026 30.9%; Q3 33.3%.

  • Höegh: 43.5% (2025); 32.4% (Q2 2026).

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

B+584th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: the gap between adjusted and reported earnings, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage?

Judgement. Reasonably clean at EBITDA level, with recurring "one-offs", one structural tax benefit and two leakages (minorities and dilution) that the model must carry explicitly.

Reasoning.

  • Adjusted against reported EBITDA: the gap is small (FY2025: USD 10m net; USTR fees 21m and digital costs 12m added back).
  • Recurring "one-offs":
  • Digital transformation and restructuring every quarter: Q3 2026 USD 12m + 3m; earlier quarters USD 2–3m each.
  • Litigation provision USD 32m (Q3 2024); digital true-up −13m (Q4 2024).
  • Vessel sale gains most years (USD 16m in Q3 2025, USD 28m related-party in 2025, USD 40m Asian Captain in Q4 2026).
  • EPS bases: reported 2.41 against brokers' 2.20 (2025). The gap is gains and EUKOR/financial items.
  • Capitalised costs: dry-docking (USD 122m asset; depreciated over 2.5–5 years); vessels over 27–30 years. Both are normal.
  • Tax: 3.6–6.5% reported (Norwegian tonnage tax). Structural and durable, with small Pillar Two top-up (USD 1.8m in Q2 2026).
  • Leakage:
  • EUKOR minorities (20% held by HMG): about 9–10% of group net profit (Q3: 212 group against 192 attributable). USD 28m dividend to non-controlling interests in Q3.
  • The EUKOR put is revalued on KRW moves (USD 81m per 10%; non-cash).
  • About 4.1% share dilution from October 2026.
  • IFRS 16 flatters EBITDA against peers that expense charters.
C-76

C-6612th of 25

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is the financial leverage, how much does it amplify EPS and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. Comfortable and modest. Net debt including leases is about 1.3x EBITDA against a cap of below 3.0x. Interest is about 12% of EBITDA. EPS gearing comes mostly from asset leverage (D&A) rather than debt. Net debt will drift up in 2027 with peak capex, then fall from 2028. In a downturn to about USD 1.0bn of EBITDA, leverage would be about 2.5x: within the covenant cap, but dividends would be cut, as in 2020.

  • Net interest-bearing debt (including about USD 1.5bn of leases): USD 2,119m at Q3 2026 (1.3x; 1.2x at Q2; 1.0x at end-2025).

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A661st of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price is the single largest driver of the forecast. In 2027 it adds about USD 330m of revenue (+6%) and about 2.5–3 points of EBITDA margin, because about 40% of volume renews from an expiring average of about USD 54–58 per cbm into a market where peers sign above USD 100. The company captures less of the peak than Höegh (it sells duration as well as price), but more than consensus models. From 2028 price contributes less each year and turns negative in 2029–2030, when spot softens and the 2027 tranche and the HMG block renew in a lower market. The pricing power is real, applied and cyclical: it comes from scarcity, holds as long as the contract lasts, and is given back at renewal when utilisation falls, as in 2012–2018.

What the evidence says about the 2027 renewals.

  • Benchmarks. Legacy book average about USD 54 per cbm (company, 17 September 2026); realised net rate USD 64.4 in Q3 2026 (mix and short-term trades); Höegh new contracts above USD 100 per cbm net and USD 82 prorated across its book (September 2026); spot USD 170 per cbm on Asia–Europe; one-year charters USD 95–110k a day (+120–135% since late 2025).
  • Company statements. The "vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity will be contracted by end-2026 "at rates closely reflecting current strong market conditions" (Q3 2026); the system is "effectively sold out" out of Asia, "requiring severe customer prioritisation". The "lower-for-longer" posture DNB described in February 2026 has been dropped in favour of market rates, but duration is still being sold: the USD 700m Asian OEM contract is three years with a two-year mutual extension option.
  • Awards. USD 700m over three years (Asian OEM, November 2026; multi-fuel on all lanes); USD 950m over three years for shipping plus USD 100m for logistics (equipment manufacturer, October–November 2026, high-and-heavy); earlier the European OEM's extension to 2031 (USD 420m) and the USD 766m H&H green contract. Values are disclosed; rates are not.
  • Peer read-across. Hyundai Glovis's renegotiated affiliate contracts raised KRW rates per vehicle by 102.3% (Jefferies, 2025); Glovis's shipping operating profit rose 16.6% year on year in Q3 2026; China Merchants Energy Shipping's RoRo profit rose 687%. Höegh extended a major Asian producer to December 2029 at "observed market rates" (USD 300m, July 2026).

My base-case price assumptions and why.

Year Volume renewing Like-for-like change on that volume Effect on revenue Reasoning
2027 About 40% (about 23m cbm) +USD 18 per cbm (about 56 to 74), about 80% effective in-year About +330 Market rates far above legacy; a discount of about 25–30% to Höegh's new-contract level for duration, volume commitment and customer relationship
2028 Carry-over of 2027 (+60) plus about 20–25% renewing (+USD 5–6 per cbm) Spot and short-term trades (about 10% of volume) fall about USD 15 per cbm About +60 +60 -85 Charter rates fall to about USD 62k a day (DNB); still far above 2021 (USD 25.6k)
2029 Short-dated remainder renews about flat; spot softer -USD 2 to -3 per cbm on the reported KPI About -130 Utilisation 88% (DNB, November) or 80% (August path); OEMs push back
2030 The 2027 tranche resets (about -USD 9 on 20m cbm) and HMG reprices (about +USD 7 on 14m cbm) -USD 3 per cbm on the KPI About -150 Mid-cycle; fleet growth 0.5% (2029) and scrapping begin to tighten again

The reported net freight rate KPI in my model: about 63.3 (2026), 68.1 (2027), 67.3 (2028), 65.0 (2029), 62.0 (2030).

How price reaches margin. Of the USD 330m in 2027, I expect about USD 230m net to reach EBITDA after: mix (more Chinese cars, less H&H about -40), port fees (probability-weighted about -30), and the share of cost inflation that renewals were meant to cover. Under IFRS 16 the higher long-term charter costs pass below EBITDA (P-01), so the EBITDA conversion of price (about 70%) is better than in 2024–2025 (about 35–50%), but the EPS conversion is lower.

Why the reported KPI will confuse the market. DNB Carnegie, the most bullish broker, models the net freight rate down to USD 60.6 per cbm in 2027 and 2028 from 63.4 in 2026, yet its EBITDA (1,829 and 1,908) and revenue (5,818 and 5,792) are close to or above mine. The difference is composition: DNB gets its revenue from volume (Shapers and Chinese cars; revenue +6%) at a lower rate per cbm (car-heavy mix); I get it from like-for-like price on a fleet-capped volume (+2%). The KPI therefore cannot be used alone to monitor the case: monitor net freight revenue (volume × rate) and Shipping EBITDA. In Q2 2023 the rate fell to USD 50 per cbm on mix even while renewals were concluding at high levels (DNB Carnegie, August 2023).

Pass-through mechanics (what protects the margin).

  • BAF 2.0 (from January 2025) covers conventional fuel, biofuel and EU ETS, with a 2–4 month lag (the Q2 2026 lag cost USD 31m net; Q3 recovered USD 47m). In 2018, legacy contracts without BAF clauses left 20–25% of the bunker increase unrecovered; that gap has been closed.
  • US port fees: management's aim is to recover "all remaining direct costs" from customers and to re-route. Höegh's gross exposure is only USD 60–70m against the company's USD 350–400m. Contract pass-through terms are undisclosed.
  • Contracts set a share of each customer's volume, not minimum volumes (the first set's reading; the pricing report's claim of "minimum volume guarantees" is not supported by any company quote). Price is protected; volume is not.

The ceilings and the give-back.

  • Containers and flat racks (1.0–1.5m cars in H1 2026; 2–4m a year on DNB's estimate) set a ceiling on car-carrier rates and a reservoir that refills car carriers as capacity frees.
  • History of give-back: net rate 57.0 (2012) to 50.0 (2013) to 47.0 (2015) to 41.0 (2017–2018) as utilisation fell to 64–71%; charter rates fell to USD 14.5k a day (2017). It took three to six years of renewals for the give-back to complete. That is the speed I assume for 2029–2031: gradual, renewal by renewal.

Skew. For 2027, upward: each extra USD 5 per cbm on the renewal tranche is worth about USD 90m of 2027 EBITDA (about +5%). For 2029–2030, downward: a low Chinese-export case (DNB: up to 65% downside to rates over 2027–2029) would cut the 2030 reset by a further USD 5–10 per cbm.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7545P-0157
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F4518th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage, and how wide a range of outcomes does it create?

Judgement. Very material. In the short term the cost base is mostly fixed: owned and chartered ships, crew, terminals, and D&A of about USD 650–720m. Fuel is passed through with a lag. Contracts stop rates collapsing but not volumes falling. The outcome range is very wide: plausible 2028 EBITDA from about USD 1.2bn to 2.0bn+, and EPS from about USD 0.5 to 2.5. Contracts and charter flexibility narrow the downside compared with a spot carrier.

Reasoning (sensitivities, my arithmetic):

  • USD 1 per cbm on net rate ≈ USD 57–60m of EBITDA (about 3.5%) ≈ 7–8% of EPS.
  • 1% of shipping volume ≈ USD 40m of revenue, of which roughly 60–70% falls to EBITDA after variable voyage costs.
  • History: company EBITDA went from USD 571m (2019) to 1,901m (2024) (3.3 times) on revenue +77% (2,998 → 5,308). FactSet EBIT went from −353 (2020) to 1,766 (2024). In 2020 ocean volumes fell 45% in Q2.
  • Mitigants: about one third of capacity is chartered (it can be redelivered); lay-up and slow steaming; cost programmes (USD 210m in 2020); a logistics buffer; 90% of volume contracted.
  • Broker range for 2028: EBITDA 1,390 to 1,912. Morgan Stanley's car-carrier bear case: earnings −40% a year in FY2027–2028.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

B574th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The operating leverage is large, but over 2027–2030 it works mostly through price at renewal on a nearly fixed, capacity-capped volume base, not through volume. In 2027 it works for the company: about USD 330m of repricing revenue reaches EBITDA at roughly 80% after mix and cost offsets, lifting the margin from 29.8% to 32.7%. From 2028 it works gently against it, as spot and renewal prices soften while costs keep inflating at about USD 40m a year. Because about USD 0.87bn of D&A and net financial cost sits fixed below EBITDA, each 1% of EBITDA moves EPS by about 1.9%. The cone of outcomes is therefore much narrower for 2027 (contracts) than for 2029–2030 (renewals of the 2027 tranche and HMG), and narrower than in 2019–2024, when volume and price moved together.

The cost structure (FY2025, company annual report via the operating-leverage report): voyage costs USD 1,725m (fuel 794, cargo 505, port and canal 405, other 21), ship operating costs 392 (crew 199, maintenance 58, ship management and other 135), short-term charter hire 122, logistics processing 334, SG&A 435, other group costs. Total costs before D&A about USD 3.43bn against revenue of 5.24bn.

Cost block Approximate size (FY2025) Behaviour in the next 1–4 years
Fuel 794 Passed through by BAF with a 2–4 month lag (Höegh 5–6 months); a timing item, not a margin lever, except for Shaper efficiency the company keeps
Cargo, port and canal about 910 Variable with volume; ETS and port fees are separate and largely passed through
Ship operating costs and short-term charters about 515 Fixed per ship; reducible only by redelivery or lay-up
Logistics processing and terminals about 330 plus labour Mostly fixed in the short run; terminal leases sit below EBITDA under IFRS 16
SG&A 435 Fixed; USD 30m+ savings programme
D&A (including right-of-use for 34 long-term charters) 651, rising to about 740 Fixed; steps up with Shaper deliveries and charter re-fixing
Net financial items (including lease interest) about 125–150 Fixed; about 70% hedged

The leverage in numbers (my arithmetic, FY2027 base).

  • Price: USD 1 per cbm across the whole shipping book is about USD 59m of revenue and EBITDA (3.2% of FY2027 EBITDA, about 6% of EPS). But in any one year only the renewing tranche reprices; in 2027 that is about 40% of volume, in 2028 perhaps 20–25%, in 2029 the remainder of the short-dated book, and in 2030 the 2027 tranche plus HMG.
  • Volume: 1% of shipping volume is about USD 40m of revenue, of which about 60–70% reaches EBITDA (variable cargo, port and fuel costs), about USD 26m, or 1.4% of EBITDA and 2.6% of EPS. Volume is capped by the fleet (96–98% utilisation), so in the base case it adds only +2% in 2027 and about +0.5% a year after.
  • The fixed layer below EBITDA: D&A plus net financial items rise from about USD 815m (2026) to about 870–890m (2027–2030). EBIT margin therefore moves about 1.4 times as much as the EBITDA margin in percentage terms, and EPS about 1.9 times as much as EBITDA.

Against the last four to six years. The leverage is the same machine that took company EBITDA from USD 571m (2019) to 1,901m (2024), 3.3 times on revenue up 77%, and FactSet EBIT from -353 (2020) to 1,766 (2024). But the inputs are different now:

  • 2020–2022: price and volume and utilisation all rose together (utilisation 58% in 2020, 64% in 2021, 97% in 2024, DNB Carnegie). Leverage ran at full force.
  • 2023–2025: volume fell (-8% in 2024, -1% in 2025) while the net rate rose from about 57–58 to 64.6 per cbm. Revenue was flat, and the price gains were largely absorbed by cost inflation (fuel lag, crew, port, ETS, capacity). The historical drop-through of price to EBITDA in 2024–2025 was well under 100%, roughly 35–50% by my reading (net rate +USD 2.8 per cbm in 2025, about +USD 160m of revenue, against EBITDA -90).
  • 2026: a cost-driven trough (margin 34.6% to 29.8%), of which about USD 55–60m is excess bunker lag in H1 that reverses.
  • 2027–2030: volume is flat and fleet-capped, so the leverage is almost entirely a price lever, applied tranche by tranche.

How it shapes each line (base case).

  • Revenue: +6.5% in 2027 against +2.4% in 2026 (like-for-like price about +6%, volume about +1.5%, mix, H&H and lower other shipping revenue about -1.5%, logistics +0.6%), then -0.5%, -2.5% and -3.0% as spot and renewal prices soften. Range for 2027: +3% to +10%.
  • EBITDA margin: 29.8% (2026), 32.7% (2027), 32.2%, 31.8%, 30.9% (2030). That is below the 34–36% of 2023–2025 in every year, because costs re-based upwards in 2025–2026 (crew, ETS, port, logistics labour, short-term capacity) and are not coming back down.
  • EBIT margin: about 16.9% (2026) rising to 20.0% (2027), then 19.2%, 18.5% and 17.0%, against 22–25% on the same company basis in 2023–2025. D&A rises from 12.4% of revenue (2025) to about 13–14%.
  • EPS: about 1.60 (2026), 1.96 (2027), 1.85, 1.74, 1.49 (2030), against brokers'-basis EPS of 1.88, 2.31 and 2.20 in 2023–2025.

Why the leverage is likelier to help in 2027 and hurt only gently in 2028.

  • The 2027 tranche is being signed now, in a market where one-year charter rates are USD 95–110k a day, spot reached USD 170 per cbm and Höegh's new contracts are above USD 100 per cbm net. The company's expiring contracts average about USD 54–58.
  • The ships to carry it exist (sold out) and costs are mostly known; the cone for 2027 EBITDA is about USD 1.70–2.00bn.
  • In a 2028 downturn the mitigants work on both sides of EBITDA: about one third of capacity is chartered and can be redelivered (seven were redelivered in 2020), charter rates fall (DNB: USD 62k a day in 2028), and the 2020 precedent shows USD 210m of savings from 32 initiatives, 15 ships in cold lay-up and slow steaming. In Q2 2020, with ocean volumes down 45%, the company held its net freight rate at USD 41.4 per cbm (against 42.3 in 2019) because contracts held.

Skew and width. For 2027 the skew is mildly upward on price and downward on costs (port fees, bunker lag from Brent at USD 111): net symmetric to slightly positive. For 2029–2030 the cone is wide (EBITDA about USD 1.2–2.0bn) and skewed down, because the leverage then acts on price, and price then depends on Chinese export policy, Suez timing and how the 2027–2028 deliveries are absorbed.

Financial leverage

C-7666P-0258
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C-6612th of 25full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C-588th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage is modest and will stay modest: net debt including leases rises from USD 1.73bn (end-2025) to about 2.0bn (end-2026) and 2.4–2.5bn (2027–2029), about 1.25–1.4x EBITDA against the company's cap of below 3.0x. It amplifies EPS only slightly: net financial items are about 7–8% of EBITDA. The things that actually separate EBITDA from EPS are asset leverage (D&A), the EUKOR minority (about 9–10% of group profit) and the 4.1% dilution of October 2026, and all three move against EPS in 2027. The balance sheet's real constraint is liquidity, not leverage: the USD 1bn minimum liquidity rule, peak Shaper capex and the families' dividend expectations together cap distributions in 2026–2027.

The structure (Q3 2026).

  • Total debt including leases USD 2,646m (FactSet), of which lease liabilities USD 1,459m and interest-bearing financial debt about USD 1,187m. Cash USD 526m before the USD 300m placement. Company net interest-bearing debt USD 2,119m (1.3x).
  • Debt-like items outside these figures: the EUKOR put liability (about USD 386m after the April 2026 deferral; exercisable from 1 January 2031; KRW sensitivity USD 81m per 10%, booked in equity) and pensions (USD 30m).
  • About 70% of interest is hedged (fixed loans and swaps). The company's own sensitivity: +1 percentage point on rates costs about USD 4m a year in interest, offset by about USD 11m more interest income on cash (2025 annual report). This is negligible rate risk.
  • Post-delivery bank financing is secured for 11 of the 14 Shapers on 18–22-year profiles; RCFs of USD 1.2bn mature 2030–2031.

How it shapes EPS (base case).

  • Net financial items: about -125 (2026; consensus -128), then -145 (2027), -150 (2028), -140 (2029), -145 (2030). The increase comes from drawing post-delivery loans on about 6–7 Shapers in 2027 (about USD 500m at about 70% of cost), from lower interest income as cash is held near the floor, and from higher lease interest as charters re-fix. Consensus has -123 for 2027 and an implausible -61 for 2028 (EBIT less pre-tax profit of the medians; a median-mixing artefact). The 2028 consensus pre-tax line is therefore about USD 90m too high on interest alone.
  • Each USD 10m of financial cost is about USD 0.02 of EPS. The financial-cost differences between me and consensus are worth about -0.04 (2027) and -0.17 (2028) of EPS.
  • A possible new cost: since Q2 2026 the EUKOR put is measured at the net present value of its future exercise price. If the unwinding of that discount passes through finance costs rather than equity (not disclosed), it would add about USD 15–20m a year (about USD 0.04 of EPS). I have not included it; the Consumer should check the Q4 2026 accounts.

How it shapes the balance sheet (base case, net debt including leases).

Year-end Net debt incl. leases Net debt / EBITDA What moves it
2025 actual 1,728 0.95x
2026e about 2,000 1.25x Dividends paid USD 685m and lease additions about 260m, against free cash flow 389m, the USD 295m placement and the Asian Captain proceeds
2027e about 2,390 1.28x Peak capex (about 880) and new leases at peak charter rates; dividends cut to about USD 460m
2028e about 2,480 1.35x Free cash flow recovers (705) but dividends rise again
2029e about 2,420 1.38x Free cash flow peaks (911)
2030e about 2,570 1.55x Jinling delivery instalments; lower EBITDA

This disagrees with consensus "total debt" (2,508 / 2,357 / 2,437 for 2026–2028, which falls in 2027 while capex peaks); the first set flagged that as optimistic and I agree. The difference matters little for leverage but a great deal for the dividend: a balance sheet that stays near USD 1bn of liquidity cannot fund extraordinary dividends in 2027 without new borrowing.

Stress test. In a 2029–2030 downturn to EBITDA of about USD 1.2bn, with net debt about USD 2.6bn, leverage would be about 2.2x: inside the 3.0x cap, but dividends would fall to the 30% floor, as the company cut them to zero in 2020 (when leverage reached 6.4x). Add the EUKOR put (about USD 0.4bn, due in cash only if exercised from 2031) and adjusted leverage would be about 2.5x. Financial distress is not a plausible outcome within four years; a dividend cut is.

The dilution and the share count. 17.15m new shares were issued on 6 October 2026 at NOK 168 (gross USD 300m), taking the count from 422.8m to about 440.0m (+4.1%). The FY2026 weighted count is about 426.8m; FY2027 onwards about 440m. There is no buyback programme and the family structure (75.69% of votes) makes one unlikely; I assume none.

What the financial leverage means for the cone. It widens EPS outcomes only a little (interest is hedged and small) but the minority leakage and D&A are fixed in dollars, so EPS falls proportionately faster than EBITDA in a downturn: at EBITDA of USD 1.4bn (DNB's long-term level), EPS would be about USD 1.00; at USD 1.2bn about 0.60.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

C-7755P-0352
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C-559th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. In a downturn, about 10–15% of operating costs could be reduced within 12–18 months, mainly by redelivering chartered ships, laying up and slowing ships, and cutting SG&A and digital consultants. Owned-ship capital costs, terminal leases, port fees and carbon costs are not cuttable. The 2020 precedent (USD 210m of savings, about 6% of then-costs) is the best guide. In an upturn, there is little to cut beyond the USD 30m+ SG&A programme.

Reasoning by cost line (rough, from about USD 3.4bn of 2025 operating costs before D&A):

  • Fuel: passed through; speed cuts save fuel. Highly variable.
  • Charter hire (about one third of capacity; short-term charters in operating costs, long-term in IFRS 16 lines): redeliverable at expiry; seven redelivered in 2020.
  • Vessel operating costs (crew USD 180m in 2024, maintenance): reducible only by lay-up (15 ships in cold lay-up in 2020).
  • Port and cargo: variable with volume, not cuttable per unit.
  • Logistics labour: partly flexible (temporary staff).
  • SG&A: USD 30m+ savings target; USD 50m digital programme.
  • ETS and port fees: passed through or not cuttable.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C526th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The identifiable self-help is about USD 95–120m a year of EBITDA by 2028 against 2026 (about 6–8% of EBITDA), plus a larger saving below EBITDA as owned Shapers replace chartered ships. But it is not a margin-expansion story: annual cost inflation of about USD 40m (crew, terminals, wages, digital run costs) absorbs most of it. In the base case cost self-help roughly offsets inflation, so the margin path is set by price, not cost. The timing is front-loaded in logistics (2026–2027), and the Shaper benefits arrive 2027–2029.

The programmes, with what reaches EBITDA and when (my estimates):

Item Evidence Reaches EBITDA Timing
Logistics margin programme (standardisation, network, site volume) Cash EBITDA margin 1% (late 2025) to 7.6% (Q3 2026); target 10% by 2028; record Q2 2026 EBITDA USD 46m; CEO: a quarterly figure "starting with a five" is the target About +USD 30m by FY2028 and +45m by FY2030 against FY2026 (FY2026 about 170; FY2028 about 200; FY2030 about 215) Mostly 2026–2027 already in the run-rate; the last USD 30m in 2027–2028
SG&A savings via Cognizant (digital standardisation; 1,200 applications) Target USD 30m+; SG&A 435 (2025) against 440 (2024); USD 50m programme About +USD 20–25m net 2027–2028; the USD 12m-a-quarter digital transformation cost in Q3 2026 should fall away (partly an adjustment item)
Shaper fuel efficiency (25% lower fuel cost per CEU; up to 40% energy efficiency) 14 ships, about 18% of shipping capacity by 2028 About +USD 25–35m retained by the company; the rest passes to customers through BAF 2.0, which charges for fuel consumed Phased 2027–2029
Fewer short-term charters as Shapers arrive Charter hire USD 122m (2025), higher in 2026 ("capacity costs" in the May guidance cut); long-term charters fell from 41 (Q1 2026) to 34 plus 2 short-term (Q3 2026) About +USD 20–30m 2027–2028
Old-tonnage exits Turandot (2025); Asian Captain (Q4 2026, USD 40m gain, about USD 52m proceeds) Small opex saving; gains are below adjusted EBITDA 2026–2028
Total self-help About +USD 95–120m About 40% in 2027, 80% by 2028
Cost inflation (crew, terminals, wages, other) Crew 147 (2023) to 180 (2024) to 199 (2025); logistics wage inflation About -USD 40m a year Every year

Below EBITDA, the charter replacement matters more. A Shaper costs about USD 115m and carries about 1.6–1.8 times a 6,500 CEU ship. Its annual capital cost is about USD 9–10m (depreciation about USD 4m over 27–30 years plus interest about USD 5–6m). Chartering equivalent capacity at today's USD 85–110k a day would cost about USD 50–65m a year. The company's existing charters were fixed at much lower rates, so the saving is measured against re-fixing, not against today's cost; but it is the reason the D&A and lease lines rise only moderately while the company re-fixes in a peak market. Höegh's equivalent figure is a newbuild cash cost of about USD 26 per cbm against about USD 80 for market charters.

Large cuts in a downturn. The 2020 precedent is the guide: 32 initiatives worth USD 210m (about 6% of then-costs), seven charters redelivered at expiry, 15 ships in cold lay-up, slow steaming at 13.7 knots, USD 70m of debt instalments deferred, 4 old ships recycled. Today the redeliverable pool is 34 long-term plus 2 short-term charters, so a similar programme could take out about USD 150–250m within 12–18 months, about two thirds of it in charter and lay-up costs (much of which sits below EBITDA). Terminal and processing costs are not cuttable fast (long leases, labour contracts; logistics processing expenses USD 334m in 2025); the company's own logistics EBITDA margin fell from 16.3% (2024) to 11.7% (2025) on a modest volume dip.

What I put in the model. Logistics EBITDA +15 (2027), +15 (2028), +10 (2029), +5 (2030). Shipping cost self-help about +35 (2027), +35 (2028), +20 (2029). Against that, cost inflation of about -40 to -50 a year. Net cost effect on EBITDA: about -10 to -15 a year, i.e. cost is a slight net headwind every year, and the margin rises in 2027 only because price rises.

Where I differ from the sell-side. DNB Carnegie's 2028 EBITDA of USD 1,908m "assumes anticipated efficiencies from fleet renewals and land-side logistics improvements" (operating-leverage report). I think those efficiencies are real but are already partly in the 2026 run-rate (logistics) or are shared with customers (fuel). They do not, by themselves, justify a 2028 EBITDA above 2027.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4757P-0552
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C-5712th of 25full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C-526th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Seven loops are at work. In 2027 the reinforcing loops that favour the company dominate: scarcity lengthens contracts, which locks peak rates into 2027–2029 and funds the fleet renewal. The balancing loops (deliveries, stored capacity from zero scrapping, Chinese protectionism and localisation, Suez normalisation) are all real but delayed, and the company's contract book delays them further, so they reach its P&L mainly in 2029–2030. The loop that matters most for the model is one the first set did not name: the contract-lengthening loop concentrates the company's re-exposure in a single window. The three-year contracts signed in late 2026 expire together at end-2029, in the same year the HMG/EUKOR contract expires, the first Jinling ships arrive (2030), and the industry's re-accelerated ordering (orderbook back to 27% of the fleet in October 2026) delivers. That is why my EBITDA path is flat in 2027–2028 and declines in 2029–2030, rather than peaking in 2027 and falling in 2028 as consensus does.

The loops, their direction, their timing and what each does to the model.

Loop Type How it runs (evidence) When it reaches the company's P&L Effect on the model
1. Scarcity lengthens contracts, which locks earnings Reinforcing for the company in 2027–2029; becomes a cliff in 2030 China contracts move from one-year to up to five-year terms; USD 1.7bn of awards after Q3; Höegh extends a major Asian producer to December 2029 2027–2029 positive; 2030 re-exposure Holds revenue and EBITDA flat through 2028; concentrates price risk at end-2029
2. High rates lead to ordering, deliveries and later oversupply Balancing (delayed) Orderbook 42% (July 2024), 21% (March 2026), back to 27% (October 2026); deliveries 469k CEU (2026) and 400k CEU (2027); new orders deliver after 2030 (Jefferies) Spot and charter rates in 2028–2029; contract renewals 2029–2030 Softens spot (about 10% of volume) from 2028; lowers the 2030 reset
3. High rates suppress scrapping, storing capacity Balancing (amplifies the downturn) Zero PCTC scrapping in 2024–2025 against 67k CEU a year in 2012–2020; about 10% of the fleet over 30 years old by 2030 (Pareto) 2028–2029, then reverses as a supply relief in 2029–2031 Deepens the 2028–2029 softening; supports a 2030–2031 re-tightening
4. Scarcity pushes cars into containers, which return as capacity frees Balancing (a buffer) 1.0–1.5m cars in containers in H1 2026, more than 70 standard ships' worth, against about 50 ships delivering in 2027 (NH Investment & Securities); DNB: 2–4m vehicles a year on other ship types Absorbs most 2027 deliveries Keeps 2027 utilisation high; caps rates (a ceiling) and delays the downturn
5. Chinese domestic weakness pushes exports, which triggers protectionism and localisation Balancing (on demand) Chinese passenger-car exports +72% in Q3 2026 (37.8% of wholesale); EU hybrid cap of 350k a year from 1 December 2026 (against about 900k run-rate); VAT rebate 13% to 9% from 1 April 2027 (about 3.4 points of exporter margin); Brazil 35%, Mexico 50%; BYD Hungary (up to 300k units, late 2026); Chinese auto-parts exports +32% in October 2026 (a sign of knock-down assembly) Volume on China–Europe lanes in 2027; tonne-miles and renewal prices in 2028–2030 Limits my 2027 volume growth to +2%; a lower 2029–2030 price
6. Rerouting absorbs capacity but raises fuel costs with a lag Two-sided Cape routing +35–45% voyage length; about 20% of car-carrier volume used to transit the Red Sea; DNB base case: gradual Suez return through 2027, sailing distance 3,660 nm (2026) to 3,580 nm (2028); container transits back to 54% by October 2026 Fuel lag now (Q4 2026/Q1 2027 with Brent at USD 111); capacity release 2027–2028 -USD 15–20m in Q1 2027 from bunker lag; part of the 2028 spot softening
7. Carbon rules retire old ships and favour dual-fuel fleets Balancing on supply; reinforcing for the company's relative position ETS 100% from 2026; FuelEU 2% to 6% by 2030; CII; LNG-carrier precedent: 15 scrapped in 2025, average scrapping age down from 35+ to 26 years; IMO Net-Zero Framework delayed a year 2028–2030 Supports 2030 pricing; protects Shaper utilisation; small green premia

Two company-specific loops the Consumer should carry.

  • The family-dividend loop (reinforcing, pro-cyclical). Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding takes about half its free cash flow to equity from the company and received USD 162m in H1 2026 (sector digest). High payouts at the peak (USD 989m paid in 2025; 82–91% of H1 2026 profit including a USD 100m extraordinary) ran cash down to the liquidity floor (USD 1,026m of reserves at Q3), which forced a USD 300m equity raise one month after a USD 258m dividend. The same loop will now cut the cash dividend in 2027, when capex peaks, and restore it in 2028–2029. It is visible in the dividend line, not in EPS.
  • The customer-resentment loop (balancing, slow). OEMs who "faced soaring logistics costs" (Morgan Stanley) respond by building captive fleets (BYD's 8 ships), using containers, localising production and, at renewal, demanding concessions. It does little before 2029 but lowers the price at which the 2027 tranche and HMG renew for 2030.

Which loop dominates when.

  • To end-2027: loops 1 and 4 dominate (contracts lock in; containers absorb new ships), with loop 6 still absorbing capacity. DNB's November path has charter rates rising to USD 79.7k a day in 2027. Revenue and EBITDA rise.
  • 2028: loops 2, 3 and 6 begin to bite on spot and charter rates (DNB USD 62.0k a day); loop 1 still protects most of the book. EBITDA roughly flat (my 1,830 against 1,870).
  • 2029–2030: loops 2, 3 and 5 meet the company's re-exposure (loop 1's cliff); loop 7 and the collapse in fleet growth (0.5% by end-2029) start to rebalance. EBITDA falls to about 1,660 (2030), with a wide cone.

Precedents for timing. In the post-2008 car-carrier cycle the company's net rate fell from 57.0 (2012) to 41.0 (2017–2018): about five years of renewals to complete the give-back, with utilisation at 64–73%. In 2020 contracts held the rate (41.4 against 42.3) through a 45% volume collapse. Container-shipping precedent (Morgan Stanley, June 2009) shows that structural supply surpluses take several years of low rates to clear. Against that, today's supply wave is smaller and shorter (yards full to 2030; active yards halved since 2008; Morgan Stanley puts shipyard capacity at about 70% of its peak), so I expect a shallower and shorter give-back than 2012–2018.

What would change the loop balance. Upside: Red Sea reopening delayed to 2029 (DNB: +45% upside to 2028 rates); Chinese exports holding at about 10m a year; delivery slippage. Downside: DNB's low Chinese-export case (up to -65% to rates over 2027–2029); a fast Suez return in H1 2027; Japanese incumbents (MOL car-carrier profit -93% year on year in Q2 2026) choosing volume over price.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

D5215th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for the change in input forces?

Judgement. FY2026 guidance is realistic, not conservative. It is 12% below 2025 and 16% below 2024 even though market rates and utilisation are at records. The gap comes from costs (bunker, charter, port), Government weakness, and the legacy contract book not yet repricing. The input forces for 2027 (renewals into a market far above the legacy USD 54 per cbm) are stronger than in any year since 2022, and none of that is guided. The guidance stance is therefore conservative by omission for 2027–2028.

Reasoning. Guidance covers only the current year. The ROCE target of 12% is set for the whole cycle and is currently exceeded (about 15.6–18%). The leverage cap (below 3.0x) and liquidity floor are conservative. The first formal 2027 guidance (expected with Q4 results on 17 February 2027) is the next test of conservatism.

Caveats. Management also stresses its long-contract, duration-first posture, which caps the peak. Its statements carry no numbers. In the past it has been wrong-footed by external costs (bunker, port fees).

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement50C-7955D-0260D-0341D-0448D-0564D-0655
Group judgement

Risks

D506th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement on the third set as a whole. The forecasts are predictable where it matters most for the next 18 months and unpredictable where it matters most for the shares.

What is predictable. FY2026 is all but fixed. The 2027 rise in EBITDA is plausible on proven strengths, asks for only about USD 8 per cbm kept on the renewing tranche against the present run-rate, and depends on a single variable that is set by contract and will be largely disclosed on 17 February 2027. Disclosure is good enough to test it from outside, and the earlier sets hang together well. In the short run the company's earnings barely follow the charter market. On these grounds the 2027 EBITDA forecast deserves medium confidence now (about ±10%) and medium-to-high confidence after February (about ±5%).

What is less predictable. First, everything between EBITDA and the shareholder. Earnings per share move about 1.8 times as much as EBITDA; the company guides nothing below EBITDA; consensus and our own model both mishandle the minority, leases or depreciation; and the dividend depends on how the board weighs a liquidity floor against the owners' need for cash. Second, the average outcome is below the base case, because the risks are dated steps (port fees from 9 January 2027, the EU cap, the VAT rebate cut, Suez) and because volume can fall inside a signed contract. Third, 2028 is protected on price only. Fourth, and most important for an investor, the multiple is not the company's to control: 70% of the share price's movement is shared with its one listed peer, the multiple is at the top of its history, and the calendar will carry the 2029–2030 give-back into the forward earnings window during 2027 and 2028 whatever 2027 delivers.

The steer. Treat this as a case in which earnings can be forecast to within about 10% and the share price cannot be forecast from earnings. The memo should present the base case as the most likely path with a lower average; use earnings per share after the minority throughout; restate the model's return on that basis; and make 17 February 2027 the point at which the case is confirmed or cut back, using the three tests in D-05.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

C-5510th of 25

Judgement. Narrow by end market (finished vehicles and H&H for OEMs), broad by geography (15 trade routes; logistics in 28 countries) and by service (ocean, terminals, processing, inland, government). The perimeter is stable, with small adjustments (the MIRRAT exit; Gothenburg, Drammen and Baltic additions; Supply Chain Solutions). Predictability is high inside the contract window (about 2.8 years of shipping backlog; 7.9 years in logistics) and low beyond it, because one cyclical end market and one policy-driven demand source dominate.
