The logistics and government segments diversify but are correlated with the same OEM end markets, except Government.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D604th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The expected change for the next 18 months is a recovery of EBITDA from about USD 1.60bn in 2026 to about 1.87bn in 2027, made almost entirely by repricing 41% of the contract book. It is plausible, and it rests mainly on things the business has shown it can do: contract with large manufacturers in a tight market, recover fuel through surcharges, take delivery of ships on time and lift logistics margins. But it asks for one thing the company has not shown in the last three years, and assumes away a second: turning a price rise into profit, and recovering US port fees from customers. The change is also smaller than it looks. Against the run-rate of the second half of 2026 (about USD 425m a quarter, or 1.70bn a year), 2027 needs about USD 170m more, which is about USD 8 per cbm kept on the renewing tranche after costs (my arithmetic). That is a modest ask in a market where the closest peer's whole book averages USD 82 per cbm and its new contracts exceed USD 100, against the company's book average of USD 54.

What delivery shows the business can do.

  • Contracting. The 2024 renewal season (45% of volume, at higher rates, accepting an 8% volume loss); the HMG/EUKOR contract (USD 4.2bn over five years, with EUKOR's share of Korean exports raised from 40% to 50%); USD 1.7bn of three-year awards since the third quarter. The company needs no new capability to sign the 2027 round.
  • Fuel recovery. The surcharge mechanism lags by two to four months and then catches up: net fuel cost fell USD 47m in the third quarter of 2026 and Shipping EBITDA rose 19% on the quarter. That quarter is the proof that when costs stop rising, price and surcharge reach profit at once.
  • Fleet delivery. Two Shapers delivered on schedule (July and October 2026); one USD 80m overrun, from a change of engine specification the company chose, without delay; financing arranged for 11 of 14.
  • Logistics. The cash EBITDA margin was 7.6% in the third quarter of 2026 against 5.1% a year earlier and 2% for 2025. The 2028 target of 10% is within reach, though the record is volatile and a specialist peer earned only a 3.5% operating margin in the past (GEFCO, 2009), so 10% is a high bar for this kind of business.
  • Self-help in a downturn. USD 210m of savings in 2020; contracts held the rate through a 45% fall in volume.

What it has not shown.

  • Price into profit. From 2020 to 2025 the net freight rate rose 56%, but in 2025 it rose 4% (about USD 160m of revenue) while EBITDA fell by 90, and in 2026 EBITDA falls a further 210 in the tightest market on record. The largest single contract (HMG, about a quarter of volume) repriced upward at the start of 2025, and EBITDA still fell. The causes were falling volume, fuel lag, short-term charter costs, port fees and a weak Government segment. Three of those have eased (fuel recovered in the third quarter; short-term charters cut from six to two; port fees suspended). The base case needs about 70% of the price rise to reach EBITDA, against 35% to 50% in 2024–2025. Peers show the same dependence on the cost phase: Hyundai Glovis lifted its shipping operating margin to 14.8% after a 63% rate rise from its affiliates, but from a very low base and after returning all five of its short-term charters; Höegh Autoliners' EBITDA fell 16% in the second quarter of 2026 despite record contract rates.
  • Port-fee recovery. Management's aim is to recover all of it. The only evidence is the fourth quarter of 2025: USD 21m of cost in about four weeks, close to the unmitigated run-rate, with recovery still "subject to unresolved negotiations". The company removed the cost from adjusted EBITDA; that protects the headline, not the cash.
  • Digital savings. The USD 30m of SG&A savings is unproven; 2026 carried USD 15m of restructuring cost and the digital leadership changed twice in 2025.
  • Holding price when the market loosens. History says it cannot (USD 57 per cbm in 2012 to USD 41 in 2017–2018). This matters for 2029–2030, not for the next 18 months.

Why the balance is favourable for 18 months. The capabilities the thesis needs first (signing contracts, operating ships, passing on fuel) are the proven ones. The unproven one that matters most (price into profit) has just been shown for one quarter under the conditions the forecast assumes. The thesis does not need the company to out-earn the cycle, win share or change its business.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?F418th of 8full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

D485th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Over the next 18 months (the fourth quarter of 2026 to the first quarter of 2028) the cone is narrow for 2026, moderate for 2027 EBITDA (about ±10% around the base case), and about twice as wide for earnings per share (about ±20%). It is skewed modestly to the downside: the most likely 2027 EBITDA is about USD 1.87bn, the average of the outcomes about 1.83bn. The case is more predictable than a shipping company's normally is, and less predictable than 90% contract cover suggests, because the contracts fix the price but not the volume, the costs or the timing. The cone should narrow sharply on 17 February 2027.

The cone (my judgement; ranges are meant to hold about four times in five).

Low Base case High Average of outcomes
FY2026 EBITDA 1,580 1,599 1,615 1,597
FY2027 EBITDA 1,650 1,868 2,000 about 1,830
FY2027 EPS after the minority 1.60 2.00 2.30 about 1.92
Dividends paid in 2027 (per share) 1.00 1.40 1.75 about 1.38
Q1 2028 EBITDA 400 440 480 about 435

Why the width is what it is.

  • What the record says. Outcomes against the first guidance of the year were 0% (2024), −5% (2025) and −6% (2026). Quarterly EBITDA has landed within 5% of consensus; net profit has missed by 4% and 8% in the last two quarters. The sell-side range for 2027 EBITDA is 1,612 to 1,829 (−12% to 0% against the median), with four of five brokers between 1,795 and 1,829.
  • What moves 2027. The renewal price: each USD 5 per cbm on the renewing tranche is about USD 85m to 90m of 2027 EBITDA (5%) and about 0.17 of earnings per share. Port fees: nil to about USD 200m a year net, with the first quarter after any resumption likely to bear most of the gross cost (USD 60m to 90m) before recovery starts. Fuel: a lag of USD 15m to 20m in the first quarter of 2027 from Brent at USD 111 in October, reversing if oil falls. Volume: 1% of shipping volume is about USD 26m of EBITDA.
  • Why the market's own cone is much wider than the company's. One-year charter rates fell 43% in the year to January 2025, fell further to about USD 42,500 a day in mid-2025, and then more than doubled to USD 95,000 by October 2026. Over the same two years the company's EBITDA moved from 1,901 to 1,811 to about 1,600: down 5% and 12%. EBITDA fell while charter rates rose, because a rising charter market reaches the company first as cost (short-term capacity) and only later as price (renewals). In the short run the company's earnings therefore move little with the charter market, and partly against it. The new AlphaSense report that calls the 18-month range "extraordinarily wide and negatively skewed" describes the charter market; it overstates the range for this company.

Why the skew is down, not up as the second set had it for 2027. The second set called the 2027 skew "symmetric to slightly positive", but its own scenarios (25% on a bear case of 1,550; 20% on a bull case of 2,050) average to 1,826, below its base. I agree with the scenarios, not the label, for four reasons.

  1. The downside events are discrete and dated; the upside is gradual. Port fees (from 9 January 2027, then every two to three months), the EU hybrid cap (1 December 2026), the Chinese VAT rebate cut (1 April 2027) and a return to Suez are steps. A higher renewal price or a longer Suez closure accrues slowly.
  2. Volume can fall inside a signed contract. Shipments are being pulled forward ahead of the December and April deadlines, which sets up a gap in the second half of 2027; volume already fell 3% in the third quarter.
  3. The record of converting price into profit is poor (D-02).
  4. Renewals may take effect later than assumed. The second set has 80% of the price rise effective within 2027. The company describes 41% of capacity as up for renewal "over the coming year"; if contracts start through the year rather than in January, the first half carries less.

Against these, the upside is real: a renewal price above USD 74 per cbm, port fees cancelled, Suez closed for longer, oil falling.

What narrows the cone. After 17 February 2027 (the book's average rate, the backlog and the first 2027 guidance) I expect the range for 2027 EBITDA to shrink to about ±5%, which is the size of the company's own guidance misses. The first-quarter 2027 report in early May then shows the new contracts in the reported rate and whether the fuel lag has passed. Tail events outside the cone are few and familiar: an abrupt reopening of Suez (large for the shares, small for 2027 contracted earnings), a break in Chinese exports, or a casualty.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

B642nd of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Helpful, and more helpful than the earlier sets allowed. The next six months contain two quarterly reports (17 February 2027 and early May 2027) and four dated outside events, and the company publishes each quarter most of what is needed to test the one variable that carries the case. The earlier sets treated renewal prices as confidential and unobservable. They are confidential contract by contract, but three regular disclosures let the Consumer measure the renewal round from outside: the average net rate of the contract book, the split of each quarter's change in the freight rate into price and mix, and the backlog with its duration. What the disclosure does not give is the like-for-like price on renewed contracts, the terms on which port fees pass to customers, volume by trade, any monthly data, or any guidance below EBITDA. One practice actively hides a driver: adjusted EBITDA adds back port fees.

The monitoring calendar to mid-May 2027, and what each item decides.

Date Event What to read
1 December 2026 EU cap on Chinese hybrid imports begins Chinese export data for December to February (monthly, China Passenger Car Association): how large the pull-forward was
9 January 2027 US port-fee suspension expires (then probably every two to three months) Extension, cancellation or resumption
Monthly Höegh Autoliners' trading update (volume and net rate across its book: USD 81.8 per cbm for October 2026); one-year charter rates; Suez transits The market the company's renewals were struck in. Höegh reprices faster, so it leads the company in both directions
17 February 2027 Fourth-quarter 2026 report See the three tests below; first 2027 guidance; the dividend for the second half of 2026; reported EBITDA beside adjusted EBITDA
1 April 2027 Chinese VAT rebate on exported new-energy vehicles falls from 13% to 9% Export data for April to June: the size of the gap after the pull-forward
Early May 2027 First-quarter 2027 report (the 2026 guidance cut came at this report) First quarter on the new contracts: the price part of the freight-rate bridge; net fuel cost after Brent at USD 111; volume; whether guidance is held

Three tests for 17 February 2027 (thresholds are my arithmetic from the base case).

  1. The average net rate of the contract book (USD 54 per cbm at 30 September 2026; USD 55 in February 2026). The base case has 41% of the book repricing by about USD 18, which would lift the average by about USD 7. A figure of about USD 60 or more supports the base case; USD 57 or less points to the bear case (an uplift of USD 8 or less). Cargo mix affects it: the equipment contract (high-and-heavy cargo) pulls the average up and the Asian car contract pulls it down, so read it with test 2.
  2. The shipping backlog and its duration (USD 5.8bn and 2.8 years at 30 September 2026, down from 7.8bn a year earlier; USD 1.7bn awarded since). If "the vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity has been contracted, the backlog should be above about USD 8bn with duration back above three years. A backlog that has grown on duration alone, with the average rate still near USD 54, would show that the company again sold length rather than price, as it did in early 2026.
  3. Reported EBITDA beside adjusted EBITDA, and cash. If port fees resume, the adjusted figure and guidance may exclude them, as in the fourth quarter of 2025. The cost would then show only in reported EBITDA, net profit and cash.

What else is disclosed that the earlier sets under-used. The lease note (liabilities, additions, repayments and undiscounted maturities: 387 due in 2027, 322 in 2028, 276 in 2029); the fleet table (owned, long-term and short-term chartered ships); the schedule of twelve purchase options (two in 2027, two in 2028, four in 2029, two in 2030, two in 2031); remaining Shaper commitments and the number of ships financed; liquidity against the USD 1bn floor; contract awards announced with values. Together these let the lines below EBITDA be rebuilt each quarter.

What is missing or unhelpful.

  • The reported net freight rate cannot be used alone. It rose in the third quarter mostly on mix, fell in the first half of 2026 while spot rates more than doubled, and DNB Carnegie models it down in 2027 while forecasting EBITDA up. Monitor net freight revenue (volume times rate), the price element of the bridge and Shipping EBITDA.
  • No monthly data, unlike Höegh Autoliners; no volume by trade; no detail on EUKOR; a Government segment the company admits it forecasts poorly.
  • Guidance is one figure, adjusted EBITDA, for the current year. Nothing is guided on depreciation, financial cost, the minority, lease payments or dividends.
  • Thin coverage (five brokers) means fewer questions are asked in public.
D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C-554th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. From the company's statements to EBITDA the trace is clear in direction and mechanism and incomplete in size, because the one number that sets the size, the renewal price, is not given. From EBITDA to earnings per share the trace is mechanical and each step is disclosed after the event, but the company guides none of it, there are five steps, and this is where consensus is inconsistent and where our own model carries a 10% error of basis. The external transmission, from the car-carrier market to earnings per share, is clear as a mechanism but slow, and it changes sign with the horizon: a tighter market first lowers the company's earnings through costs and only later raises them through renewals. That makes near-term earnings easier to forecast than the market, and it means earnings and the share price will respond to different things.

From statement to line to earnings per share.

What the company says Operational driver Line it reaches What can be seen Size (my arithmetic)
FY2026 adjusted EBITDA of about USD 1.6bn Quarterly EBITDA (389, 361, 428; the fourth quarter needs about 422) Group EBITDA Fully visible —
The "vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity contracted by end-2026 at rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions" Price on the renewing 41% of the book (about 22m cbm) Net freight revenue in Shipping Services; Shipping EBITDA Book average rate, backlog, price-and-mix bridge. Not the rate itself Each USD 1 per cbm on the tranche: about USD 22m of revenue a year, about USD 15m of EBITDA, about 0.03 of earnings per share
Logistics cash EBITDA margin of 10% by 2028; a quarterly EBITDA "starting with a five" Standardisation, site volume, new terminals Logistics Services EBITDA (about 170 in 2026 to about 200) Visible quarterly (7.6% in the third quarter) About +0.06 of earnings per share
SG&A savings of more than USD 30m from late 2026 Digital standardisation with Cognizant SG&A (435 in 2025) Visible, net of restructuring cost (USD 15m in 2026) About +0.06 if kept
14 Shapers by 2028, 25% lower fuel cost per unit of capacity, replacing chartered ships Deliveries; charters redelivered or bought out Fuel (shared with customers through the surcharge), short-term charter hire, depreciation, lease payments Deliveries and fleet table visible; the fuel saving kept is not Small in EBITDA; mostly below it
Returns of at least 12% on capital employed; leverage below 3.0 times; liquidity of at least USD 1bn; 30% to 50% of profit paid out, plus extraordinary dividends Capital allocation Dividends and debt, not earnings Visible —

From EBITDA to earnings per share: five steps, none guided. Depreciation (about 690 to 735, of which right-of-use assets were 265 in nine months of 2026); net financial items (about 125 to 150); tax at about 5%; the EUKOR minority at about 9% to 10% of group profit (the third quarter: 212 for the group, 192 for owners); and 440.2m shares. A rule the Consumer can carry: earnings per share after the minority are roughly EBITDA less about 850 to 870, divided by about 509, so each USD 100m of EBITDA is worth about 0.20 a share and each 1% of EBITDA about 1.8% of earnings per share. Three accounting features blur the trace:

  1. IFRS 16. The cost of long-term chartered ships sits in depreciation and interest, so EBITDA rises for accounting reasons when owned ships replace short-term charters, without a matching rise in profit or cash.
  2. Adjusted EBITDA leaves out digital and restructuring costs every quarter and left out port fees in 2025.
  3. The EUKOR put is carried at the present value of an exercise price that depends on EUKOR's future profits; whether the unwinding of the discount will pass through finance costs is not disclosed.

Consensus shows the difficulty: the medians imply a net financial cost of only 61 in 2028 against about 150, and the leading broker holds depreciation flat while the second set had it rising by 50.

The external transmission. The market balance (Chinese exports, routing, deliveries, scrapping) sets fleet utilisation and so charter and spot rates. These reach the company by five channels, each with its own lag:

  • Spot and short-term cargo (about 10% of volume): at once.
  • Contract renewals: only as contracts expire. In a normal year 20% to 30% of the book renews (41% this year); the HMG block not until 2030. In 2012–2018 the full give-back took five to six years.
  • The cost of chartered capacity: short-term charters at once, inside EBITDA (two ships now, six in early 2026); long-term charters when re-fixed, below EBITDA.
  • Volume under contracts: at once, with customers' output.
  • Fuel: through the surcharge, two to four months late.

The evidence that the sign changes with the horizon is recent. One-year charter rates more than doubled during 2026 and the company cut its guidance, citing capacity costs. The same tight market raises 2027 through renewals. DNB Carnegie's forecasts embody the lag in the other direction: it has charter rates falling 22% in 2028 and the company's EBITDA rising 4%. The practical conclusion is that no simple coefficient links the charter rate, Chinese exports or Suez to the company's next 18 months of earnings; the link runs through the contract calendar. Those outside variables matter now mainly for the multiple (D-07) and for the price at which the 2029–2030 renewals will be struck.

The Investment Analyst's heading What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere that could hit the share price regardless of fundamentals?

Judgement. The sentiment risk is high. The stock sits at the dearest point of its history after a 110% rise in 12 months, in a sector the APM describes as "priced on a cycle it does not control". Several external events could de-rate it without any change in its contracted earnings.

Specific triggers:

  1. Red Sea and Suez headlines, including container-line returns (Maersk resumed some Red Sea transits in July 2026). These read across instantly to all shipping.
  2. Peer prints. Höegh's results or flat guidance (it guided Q3 EBITDA flat at about USD 122m). Japanese car-carrier profit declines (MOL −93% in Q2; NYK guiding down). Glovis capacity news.

  4. Technical and positioning. The price (NOK 162.8) is below the October placement price (NOK 168), so new holders are under water and there is overhead supply (Wisdom Library: "issue price as technical anchor"). The price is below the 50-day average (173.05) but above the rising 200-day (140.8). Free float is about 25%; days to cover were 2.2 (against 0.5 at Höegh).
  5. Regime. The FfF digest: risk-off beneath index highs, Fragility at Double Underweight, Momentum at Underweight. The Wisdom Library entry "extended trend = banked upside" applies after +110% in a year. Norway is held Overweight on oil above USD 85, and an oil-led rotation out of Norway would hit liquidity.
  6. Parent and family flows (WWH's discount dynamics; any family selling, though there is no sign).

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D4514th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its CEO in upgrading guidance?

Judgement. The CEO behaves as an honest tracker, not a sandbagger and not an upgrader. Guidance is set close to expected outcomes, narrowed or cut quickly when costs move, and withdrawn rather than guessed when a policy shock is unquantifiable (US port fees, late 2025). The files show no case of mid-year guidance upgrades in his tenure. Do not expect "beat-and-raise" behaviour, even in a strong year.

Reasoning.

  • FY2024: a narrowed corridor of USD 1,880–1,910m; delivered 1,901 (a record).
  • FY2025: aim of flat to positive / in line with 2024; withdrawn on USTR; delivered 1,811 (−4.7%).
  • FY2026: USD 1.65–1.75bn cut to about 1.6bn (May); held since.
  • Contrast: DSV (a peer file) is described as a systematic sandbagger whose results land in or above its initial range for four years. Wallenius Wilhelmsen is not of that type.
  • Wisdom Library (sandbagging archetype): does not apply.

Weights I would attach (my judgement).

  • FY2026: high (one quarter left; guidance about USD 1.6bn; Q4 needs about USD 420m against 428 in Q3; port fees very probably suspended to January 2027). Risk only from the bunker lag with Brent at USD 111 in late October.
  • FY2027 revenue: high for the contracted base; medium for the renewal price (rates confidential). FY2027 EBITDA: medium-high. FY2027 EPS: medium.
  • FY2028: medium for EBITDA (most volume contracted; spot and costs uncertain); medium-low for EPS.
  • FY2029–FY2030: low. The outcome depends on renewals in a market no one can see; my figures are a central path in a wide cone (EBITDA about USD 1.2–2.0bn).

Direction of lean against consensus, and why.

  • EBITDA 2027 slightly up (mine 1,870 against 1,827): the renewal tranche is being signed at peak terms that consensus (median net rate assumptions and the stale Pareto estimate) does not fully carry; DNB raised its 2027 EBITDA 6% after the Q3 awards and sits at the median, Fearnley (1,612) below.
  • EPS 2027 slightly down (1.96 against 1.99): D&A and net financial items, and the record of net-profit misses.
  • EBITDA and EPS 2028 up (1,830 and 1.85 against 1,679 and 1.75): consensus embeds a normalisation the contract book prevents.
  • 2029–2030 down against extrapolation: the contract cliff (P-05).
  • Dividends 2027 down (paid 1.05 against consensus 1.58): cash, not profit, binds.

Peer read-across on track records. Höegh (pure play, faster repricing) cut its dividend from USD 94m to 16m in Q2 2026 on fuel and costs; Japanese carriers' car-carrier profits are already falling (MOL -93% year on year in Q2 2026; K-Line lowered expectations). The company's contracted model has so far delivered steadier, if lower-peak, results than both, which supports the weight I give 2027–2028.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

B563rd of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial model for the next four years is set less by the cycle than by the calendar of the company's contracts. About 40% of volume reprices for 2027 into a market far above the expiring book, which lifts revenue by about 6.5% and EBITDA by about 17% to USD 1.87bn, slightly above consensus. The book then holds 2028 at about the same level (USD 1.83bn, 9% above a median that still embeds a normalisation the contracts prevent). The balancing loops that every broker worries about (deliveries, zero scrapping, Chinese protectionism, Suez normalisation) are real, but they reach this company's P&L mainly in 2029–2030, when the 2027 tranche and the HMG/EUKOR contract renew together and the new Jinling ships arrive; I model a gentle give-back to USD 1.66bn by 2030, not a collapse. The quality of the earnings is lower than the EBITDA suggests: the fleet renewal moves cost into D&A, interest and lease payments, the share count is 4% higher, HMG takes about 9–10% of profit, and gains like MIRRAT do not recur. So EPS peaks at about USD 1.96 in 2027, below 2023–2025, and equity free cash flow halves in 2026–2027 before recovering in 2028–2029. The forecasts deserve high weight for 2026 and 2027 revenue, medium weight for 2027–2028 EBITDA, and lower weight for EPS and anything after 2028. For the third set, the case is predictable in 2027 to the extent the renewal price is as management describes; for the fourth set, the market narrative (a cyclical top, "priced on a cycle it does not control") is right about 2029–2030 and probably too early about 2028, while consensus is too generous on 2027 dividends and on the bridge from EBITDA to EPS.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?F4521st of 25full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F4414th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Below operating profit, every item moves against the shareholder over the next four years compared with 2022–2025, except tax. Compared with FY2025: D&A is about USD 40–95m higher, net financial items are roughly flat to lower than the reported 2025 level but rising from 2026, the share count is 4.1% higher, there are no large gains like MIRRAT (USD 135m in 2025), and lease principal of about USD 380–410m a year absorbs a third to a half of operating cash flow after capex. The result: EPS stays 11–32% below FY2025's brokers'-basis 2.20 in every year, and equity free cash flow halves in 2026–2027 before recovering to about USD 0.9bn in 2029. Net income is a better guide to dividend capacity than consensus "free cash flow", which omits leases and is about USD 400m a year too high as a measure of equity cash.

Item by item.

  • D&A: 651 (2025) to about 690 (2026; 9-month run-rate 515), 725 (2027), 740 (2028), 735 (2029), 745 (2030). Drivers: Shaper deliveries at about USD 4m a year each (about 3 in 2026, 6–7 in 2027, 4–5 in 2028); right-of-use depreciation rises in 2027 as charters re-fix at peak rates (lease additions USD 505m in 2025 and 194m in 9M 2026) and falls from 2028 as Shapers replace charters; first Jinling deliveries from mid-2030. Consensus is 701 (2027) and 720 (2028). Vessel lives of 27–30 years are standard (Höegh 30).
  • Net financial items: about -125 (2026), -145, -150, -140, -145 (2030). History (FactSet net interest expense): -197 (2022), -260 (2023), -259 (2024), -198 (2025). The fall from 2023–2024 reflects deleveraging and conversion of ship loans to RCFs; the rise from 2026 reflects Shaper post-delivery loans and lease interest at higher charter rates.
  • Tax: 5% of pre-tax profit (Norwegian tonnage tax; reported 3.6–6.5% in 2022–2025; Pillar Two top-up only USD 0.8m in Q3 2026 and USD 1.8m in Q2). Low risk. A tonnage-tax dispute precedent (Ugland, 2022, reversed in 2025) is not a material risk.
  • Minorities (HMG's 20% of EUKOR): about 9.5% of group net profit in 2026 (Q3: 212 group, 192 to owners), falling to about 9% in 2027–2029 because the HMG contract inside EUKOR is fixed to 2029 while the rest of the book reprices, then about 10% in 2030 when HMG reprices. In dollars: about 71 (2026), 86 (2027), 80, 76, 73. Cash dividends to the minority were USD 28m in Q3 2026 alone; I assume about USD 60m a year. The model has no minority line, so the model's EPS will be about 9–10% above mine unless it is deducted.
  • One-offs and gains: recurring "one-offs" (digital transformation USD 12m and restructuring USD 3m in Q3 2026) are mostly excluded from adjusted EBITDA; vessel sale gains (Asian Captain USD 40m in Q4 2026; Grand Uranus option declared about USD 50m below market) flow through reported but not brokers' EPS. I exclude gains from adjusted EPS; FY2026 reported EPS would be about 1.68 including Asian Captain. Gains will be smaller from 2028 as second-hand values fall.
  • EUKOR put: about USD 386m, measured at the present value of the exercise price, with KRW moves (USD 81m per 10%) booked in equity. Not an EPS item unless the discount unwinding passes through finance costs (P-02). Cash outflow only if exercised from 2031.
  • Share count: 422.8m to 440.0m (October 2026 placement at NOK 168, USD 300m gross; about USD 295m net). No buybacks: the controlling families prefer dividends and the parent itself buys back. I assume no further issuance in the base case, but the Jinling options (up to 8 ships, about USD 1bn) could require equity again around 2029–2030 if they are all declared while dividends stay high.
  • Lease payments: principal about USD 380m (2026), 410 (2027), 390 (2028), 350 (2029), 320 (2030), plus lease interest of about USD 90–110m inside net financial items. Q3 2026 lease repayments were USD 130m (principal and interest).

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B574th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. My base case is a contract-protected plateau: a cost-driven trough in 2026 (EBITDA about USD 1.60bn), a price-led recovery in 2027 (1.87bn) as about 40% of volume reprices into the tightest market on record, a flat 2028 (1.83bn) because the book is locked, and a gradual give-back in 2029–2030 (1.76bn and 1.66bn) as the 2027 contracts and the HMG/EUKOR block renew into a softer, better-supplied market. EPS peaks at about USD 1.96 in 2027, below every year from 2023 to 2025, because the fleet renewal moves cost below EBITDA and the share count is 4% higher. Equity free cash flow troughs in 2026–2027 (about USD 0.37–0.39bn) and recovers in 2028–2029 (0.7–0.9bn) as Shaper capex ends. Against consensus, the case is a little higher on EBITDA in 2027, clearly higher on 2028, a little lower on 2027 EPS, and much lower on 2027 dividends. I attach about 55% probability to this path, 20% to a bull path and 25% to a bear path; the probability-weighted 2027 EBITDA (about USD 1.83bn) equals consensus, while the probability-weighted 2028 EBITDA (about 1.79bn) is 6% above it.

Where my base case differs from the first set, and why. The first set implied 2027 consensus might be conservative but that charter re-fixing would absorb much of the repricing; I find the absorption sits mostly below EBITDA (IFRS 16), so EBITDA upside is real but EPS upside is not. The first set used DNB's August market path (utilisation 85% in 2027); the November path (charter rates rising in 2027, utilisation 88% in 2029) moves the downturn later, which is why I hold 2028 flat. The first set treated 10 November 2026 as the port-fee binary; the files now point to January 2027.

What the Consumer should test first (in order of how much each moves the case).

  1. The like-for-like rate achieved on the 2027 renewal tranche (each USD 5 per cbm is about USD 90m of 2027 EBITDA and USD 0.18 of EPS). First evidence: the Q4 2026 report on 17 February 2027 (backlog value, duration and any first formal 2027 guidance).
  2. The port-fee decision around January 2027 and the share recovered from customers (net cost range USD 0–200m a year).
  3. Whether the company declares the 8 Jinling options and how it funds them (capex 2028–2031; possible equity).
  4. The dividend for H2 2026 (paid in 2027): an extraordinary payment would contradict my liquidity reading.
  5. The pace of the Suez return for car carriers and Chinese export volumes after 1 December 2026 (EU hybrid cap) and 1 April 2027 (VAT rebate cut).
  6. Whether the EUKOR put's discount unwinding passes through finance costs.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D6113th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Meaningful scope, concentrated in a few big areas: contract repricing (largest, 2027–2029), fleet efficiency and replacing charters (large, 2026–2028), and logistics margins (medium). Peer precedent (Höegh's pure-play margins; terminal economics) shows these are achievable. They match the stated strategy. All three are partly offset by the cycle.

Reasoning.

  1. Repricing: about 40% of volume renews for 2027 against a USD 54 legacy average. Each USD 10 per cbm is about USD 230–240m a year (preface).
  2. Shaper programme: 14 ships with 27–40% better fuel use, replacing USD 85–110k-a-day charters.
  3. Logistics: cash EBITDA margin from 2% to 10% by 2028 on about USD 1.1–1.2bn of revenue is about +USD 90m of cash EBITDA. Management's quarterly target of USD 50m+ implies at least USD 200m a year of EBITDA.
  4. Small items: SG&A savings USD 30m+; green premium services (optional upside).
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D5515th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The company's revenue engine has decelerated to a plateau. Revenue fell 1.3% in 2025 and in Q1–Q2 2026, then rose 5.3% in Q3. This is not because the input force is slowing: Chinese exports are accelerating (+50% in 2026). It is because the company is capacity-capped and its legacy contract book has not yet repriced. The input force faces a policy-driven slowdown from December 2026 to April 2027, which is the real deceleration risk.

Reasoning.

  • "Normal" rate: 2010s roughly flat; 2021–2023 boom (+74%, +30%, +2%); 2023–2025 flat.
  • Volumes: −8% (2024), −1% (2025).
  • Net rate: dipped in Q2 2026, then recovered.
  • Backlog: additions slowed through mid-2026 (USD 870m in Q4 2025 → 460m in Q2 2026) before the USD 1.7bn of awards after Q3.
  • Input force: Chinese exports above 1m a month; CAAM 10m for 2026. Forecasts for 2027 range from −12% to +33%. DNB tonne-miles: +2% in 2027, +1% in 2028. H&H slowing.

Judgement. Net, the next four years look like a plateau a step below 2023–2025, not a collapse and not a new high. How the items in this set combine.

  • Operating leverage (P-01) and price (P-04) together add about USD 270m of EBITDA in 2027 and hold it in 2028; they take about USD 70m (2029) and 100m (2030) away.
  • Cost self-help (P-03) about offsets cost inflation every year: a net -10 to -15 a year.
  • Feedback loops (P-05) set the timing: up to 2028 the reinforcing contract loop dominates; from 2029 the balancing supply and protectionism loops reach the company.
  • Financial leverage and below-the-line items (P-02, P-08) take about USD 870–890m a year between EBITDA and pre-tax profit (against about 830m in 2025), plus about 9–10% to the minority and 4% to dilution.

The width of the cone around this net picture. For 2027, EBITDA about USD 1.70–2.00bn (the renewal price ±USD 8 per cbm; port fees; bunker); for 2028, about 1.55–2.05bn; for 2030, about 1.2–2.0bn. The skew is mildly upward in 2027 (price) and downward in 2029–2030 (supply, policy, Suez).

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

D434th of 8

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement. The gap between our assessment and what guidance, sell-side estimates and the market narrative already reflect is small, and on balance slightly unfavourable for an investment at today's price. There is no stored-up good news in guidance; consensus for 2027 already equals our view; the part of our view that is better than consensus (2028) is modest and half known; and the parts that are not known are mostly negatives that arrive by the calendar. Against that stand one dated confirmation, on 17 February 2027, and a dividend for the second half of 2026 that should meet consensus.

The three parts together.

  • Guidance (I-01). An honest management that neither sandbags nor raises. The first 2027 range should be a clear step up on 2026 and about in line with consensus and with us. A cut during 2027 is somewhat more likely than a raise (about 30% against 20%). The first 2028 range, in February 2028, is the one piece of guidance likely to stand above today's estimates.
  • Sell-side estimates (I-02). 2027: in line on the median, 2% to 4% of upward drift in the mean. 2028: 5% to 8% of upside to the median over a year. 2026: a small trim. The pace of upgrades falls from 52% in six months to low single digits. Dividends: in line with the median for 2026, a little below it for 2027, and well below the most optimistic broker.
  • Narrative (I-03). The positives are known and priced. The multiple is at the top of its ten-year range and is set by the industry (D-07). The little-known items are mostly unfavourable and concentrate from 2028.

What this means for the questions the memo must answer.

  • Scope for upgrades over the next 6 to 12 months: limited to 2028, and to laggards catching up. The largest single positive is confirmation, not surprise.
  • Momentum: at its peak in estimates, ratings and price (the dashboard's A, A and F). On our numbers the first two fade over the next six months without anything going wrong.
  • Where the stock is in its cycle: reinforcing, and late. The thesis broadened during 2026 (estimates up 50% to 110%, Buy ratings from about a third three months ago to two thirds, DNB Carnegie from Hold to Buy). One more reinforcing step lies ahead in February. After it the likelier next phase is stalling: estimate upgrades slow, the first 2029 estimates show a decline, and the forward earnings window moves into 2029–2030.
  • The fair multiple. The guideline multiple of 7.69x is the top of the ten-year range (the 90th percentile is 7.70x). That is defensible for the next twelve months, while forward earnings are rising or flat and contracted. It is generous beyond that: once 2029–2030 is in the window, the lower bands of the same history (6.5x and 5.3x) are the better reference. Today's multiple on earnings after the minority (about 9.2x to 9.5x) is above even the top band.
  • The return. On the proper earnings basis the return at the guideline multiple is about −12% over 18 months and about −14% over three years (preface), or about −10% and −11% with my higher dividends. Dividends of about 8% a year are the only positive component; the multiple and the forward earnings both subtract. By my rough arithmetic, at the guideline multiple the shares would need to be about a quarter to 30% lower (roughly NOK 115 to 125) before the 18-month return reached a low double-digit annual rate.

What would change the judgement. In favour: a February report that passes all three tests in D-05 with a first range centred above USD 1.85bn; cancellation of the US port fees; evidence that the 2026 contracts carry minimum volumes; or a fall in the industry's multiple that takes the shares down with it while the contracted earnings stand, which is the setting in which the company's contract cover would be worth paying for. Against: a book average rate still near USD 54; any sign that the board will fund dividends with new equity again; or car carriers returning to Suez before the 2027 estimates have been confirmed.

Guidance

3 answers
C-1352C-6855I-0150

C-68To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?C+555th of 25full answer under 6a Guidance conservatism
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C+503rd of 8

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement. Guidance will most probably do three things over the next six reports: confirm 2026; set a first 2027 range that is a step up of 13% to 19% on 2026 but about in line with consensus and with our base case; and then be held, with a cut more likely than a raise. This management does not sandbag and has never raised guidance within a year in the record we hold. Its first guidance of the year has turned out too high in two of the last three years (by 5% and 6%). Guidance therefore holds no hidden conservatism for an investor to collect. The favourable element is narrower: the first 2027 range should end the dispersion among brokers, and a first 2028 range in February 2028 near the 2027 level would stand well above today's 2028 consensus.

The six events, with my probabilities.

1. 17 February 2027: 2026 outcome, first 2027 guidance.

  • FY2026 "about USD 1.6bn": met within 2%: about 85%. The fourth quarter needs about 422 against 428 in the third; port fees are suspended to 9 January; the fuel lag from Brent at USD 111 in October is the risk (a shortfall of more than 2%: about 12%).
  • First FY2027 guidance. Form: a range about USD 100m wide, as for 2026 (USD 1.65bn to 1.75bn), stated as adjusted EBITDA and probably before any port fees. Level, measured by the midpoint against the FactSet median of 1,827: clearly above (over 1.85bn): about 30%; in line (1.80bn to 1.85bn): about 40%; below 1.80bn: about 30%. Our base case (1,868) would sit in the upper half of the range I think most likely.
  • Why not higher, given management's confident words. A year ago, after the report that carried the first 2026 range, the leading broker cut its 2026 estimate by 12%, and the range itself was cut in May. Management has been caught by costs it did not foresee in each of the last two years, and port fees remain open. Against that, the second-half 2026 run-rate is already 1.70bn and the renewals are signed. A range that starts below 1.75bn would be a negative surprise, and I think it unlikely.

2. Early May 2027 (first quarter): the report at which a cut is most likely. The first quarter is seasonally the weakest, carries the fuel lag and is the first after the 9 January port-fee date; the 2026 cut came at this report. Held: about 80%; cut: about 15%; raised: under 5%.

3 and 4. August and November 2027. By then the renewal prices are in the reported figures. Held or narrowed at each: about 80% to 85%. A raise becomes possible in August if the price element of the freight rate and volume both hold (about 10% in August, a little less in November). A cut or withdrawal (about 10% in August, a little less in November) would come from a shortfall in volume after the Chinese pull-forward, from port fees, or from Suez.

Across 2027 as a whole: held or narrowed about 50%; cut or withdrawn at some point about 30%; raised at some point about 20%.

5. February 2028: 2027 outcome, first 2028 guidance. The record puts the outcome 0% to 6% below the first midpoint. First 2028 guidance: most likely a range around the 2027 outcome (about USD 1.8bn), because the book is contracted to 2029. The chance that its midpoint is above today's FactSet median for 2028 (1,679): about 70%.

6. May 2028. As for May 2027, with less fuel and port-fee history to guide it.

What would drive each outcome.

  • Raise: a renewal price well above the base assumption appearing in the second and third quarters; falling oil (the surcharge lag then works in the company's favour); port fees cancelled; Suez closed for longer, lifting spot cargo.
  • Meet: the base case. Price as assumed, costs steady, volume flat.
  • Cut or withdraw: port fees resumed and not recovered (the company withdrew guidance in late 2025 for this reason); another fuel spike; volume lost under contracts in the second half of 2027; a casualty or a Government shortfall. Because adjusted EBITDA excludes port fees, a resumption could leave guidance formally "met" while reported profit and cash fall. Guidance is therefore a weaker guide to earnings per share than it looks.

Mid-term targets and anything for 18 months to three years out.

  • Return on capital employed of at least 12% over the cycle: being exceeded (15.6% over the last twelve months; about 15% to 16% in 2027–2028 on the base case, falling to about 12% by 2030). Met; a further raise is unlikely after the increase from 8% in 2025.
  • Logistics cash EBITDA margin of 10% by 2028: 7.6% already. Met by 2028: about 65%; an earlier or higher target is possible and is the most likely formal raise.
  • Leverage below 3.0 times; liquidity of at least USD 1bn: met throughout the base case. The liquidity floor is the target most likely to be tested, in 2027.
  • Dividend policy (30% to 50% of profit, plus extraordinary dividends): the company has paid 90% to 100% for a year. A restatement of the policy upward is possible; a cut to the 30% floor is the response to be expected in a downturn.
  • Guidance for 2029 and 2030 (given in February 2029 and February 2030) is more likely to be flat or lower than the year before than higher, as spot cargo softens and then as the 2027 contracts and the HMG contract reprice. No multi-year guidance exists and none is likely; a capital markets day (the last was in September 2024) is the only setting in which one might appear.

The gap with our assessment. Guidance, when it comes, should sit close to our base case, not below it. The value of the February event is confirmation and the narrowing of the range of outcomes, not an upside surprise against our numbers.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

B502nd of 8

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus is more likely to drift up than down over the next two or three events, but slowly, mostly because the lower brokers will move towards the higher ones, and mostly in 2028. The steep phase of upgrades is over. FY2027 earnings per share were raised 15% in the last month and 52% in the last six; against the FactSet median our base case now has nothing left to add for 2027 (2.00 against 1.99) and about 8% for 2028 (1.89 against 1.75). Net breadth should therefore stay positive while its size falls sharply, so the "A" for sell-side momentum on the dashboard describes the last six months, not the next six. The exposed points are small and near: FY2026 earnings per share, and the lines below EBITDA, where the company has missed consensus net profit in each of the last two quarters.

Year by year (earnings per share after the minority; my probabilities).

Consensus (FactSet median; mean) Base case after my changes Most likely movement in consensus Probabilities over the next two to three events
FY2026 EPS 1.66; 1.66 1.58 Trimmed by 3% to 5% at or after 17 February 2027 Cut about 60%; held about 35%; raised about 5%
FY2027 EBITDA 1,827; 1,782 (Bloomberg about 1,725) 1,868 Median held; mean up 2% to 4% as Fearnley Securities (1,612) and the September estimate from Pareto Securities (1,795) move to the guidance range Mean higher about 60%; within ±2% about 25%; lower about 15%
FY2027 EPS 1.99; 1.93 2.00 Median held; mean up 2% to 4% As for EBITDA
FY2028 EBITDA 1,679; 1,682 (Bloomberg about 1,603) 1,831 Median up 5% to 9% over about twelve months Higher about 60%; held about 20%; lower about 20%
FY2028 EPS 1.75; 1.75 1.89 Median up 5% to 8% As for EBITDA

Why.

  • FY2026. Consensus of 1.66 implies about 0.54 for the fourth quarter against 0.45 reported for the third. On EBITDA of about 420 I get about 0.42 before the USD 40m gain on the sale of Asian Captain and about 0.50 with it. Brokers excluded such gains in 2025, so a small miss is the likelier outcome. It matters little for the case and somewhat for the tone of the day.
  • FY2027. Three of five brokers already sit at 1,827 to 1,829 of EBITDA, which is where I expect the first guidance range to centre (I-01). The leaders have no reason to move. The mean rises only because the laggards do. Earnings per share get no further help below EBITDA: consensus depreciation (701) matches my revised 705, and the implied financial cost (123) is about USD 20m lighter than mine, worth 0.04 a share against it.
  • FY2028. This is where our assessment differs from the estimates. The five forecasts fall into three groups: DNB Carnegie and Clarksons Securities at 1,908 and 1,912; Nordea and Pareto at 1,666 and 1,696; Fearnley at 1,390. Ours (1,831) sits just below the top group. For the median to rise, the middle group must accept that three-year contracts signed in 2026 hold 2028 prices. The evidence that would move them is the first 2027 guidance, the backlog and its duration in February, and the price element of the freight rate in May and August. The average of our outcomes for 2028 is lower than our base case (about 1,780), so the true gap to the median is nearer 6% than 9%.
  • The record below EBITDA. Net profit to owners missed consensus by 4% and 8% in the last two quarters while EBITDA was within 5%. The causes (the minority, financial items, one-off costs) recur. Expect small misses of earnings per share on the day even when EBITDA is in line.
  • Dividends. The second set expected a large disappointment (1.05 paid in 2027 against 1.58). On my reading of the board's conduct and of the delivery loans (D-01), the dividend for the second half of 2026, announced on 17 February 2027, will be about 0.70 to 0.80 a share, which is what consensus implies. DNB Carnegie's 1.83 and 2.01 for 2027 and 2028, a yield of over 10%, are above what I expect.
  • Ratings. The share of Buy ratings is 67%, up 44 points in a month. The two Holds (Fearnley and Clarksons Securities) have price targets at or below the share price. Further upgrades of rating would need a lower share price, not higher estimates: Clarksons Securities already has the highest 2028 forecast of the five.

What would turn revisions negative. A first 2027 range below USD 1.75bn; a book average rate still near USD 54 in February; port fees resumed without recovery; a fuel-driven shortfall in the first quarter of 2027; a volume gap in the second half of 2027; or car carriers returning to Suez. In each case the cuts would fall mainly on 2028 and on the brokers' charter-rate assumptions, because 2027 is contracted.

Eighteen months to three years out. There is no FactSet consensus for FY2029 (no broker contributes). It will appear during 2027. Our figures are EBITDA of 1,763 and earnings per share of 1.77 for 2029, and 1,661 and 1.52 for 2030. DNB Carnegie's own charter-rate path falls to USD 57,800 a day in 2029, so the first published 2029 estimates will probably show earnings below 2028 even at the most optimistic house. Three things follow. First, the first sight of 2029 will put a falling year into the forward window that investors use to value the shares. Second, revisions to 2029 and 2030 will follow charter rates, Chinese exports and Suez more than anything the company reports: cut about 45%, held about 30%, raised about 25%. Third, the number in the sell-side's work that carries most of the valuation is not a forecast year at all: it is DNB Carnegie's "long-term" EBITDA, raised from USD 1.0bn to 1.4bn during 2026, on which its NOK 210 target is built at seven times. That assumption was moved by 40% in a few months on a change of view about Chinese exports and can be moved back as fast, without any change to 2027.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0737
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

F378th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The share price moves with the car-carrier industry and with shipping, not with the Logistics sector it is filed under, and the multiple is set by the industry, not the company. Over the latest six months 70% of the daily variation in the share price is shared with Höegh Autoliners alone. Against the whole sector basket the figure is 43%, with a beta of 1.8, both back at the top of their two-year ranges. The two listed car carriers have returned almost the same over twelve months (+110% and +117%) and both trade on the highest price-to-earnings multiples of their ten-year histories, although one reprices its book within a year and the other over three. The market is pricing one trade, the car-carrier cycle, and is not yet paying for the difference in contract cover that the whole of our case rests on. Where the shares have moved on their own in the past year, the cause was a policy exposure (US port fees), not anything the company did.

The evidence (co-movement table, rolling 126 trading days, to 13 November 2026).

  • Against the 17-stock sector basket: correlation 0.66; beta 1.82; 43% of variance explained. Six-month return +29.8% against +13.8% for the basket.
  • Peer by peer: Höegh Autoliners 0.84 (70% explained); Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding 0.69 (the parent, which owns 38% of the company, so partly mechanical); NORDEN, a dry-bulk and tanker operator, 0.64; Hapag-Lloyd 0.46; CMB.TECH 0.36; Clarkson 0.32. Against the freight forwarders and postal companies the correlation is about nil (DSV 0.14, DHL 0.09, Kuehne + Nagel 0.27, STEF 0.00). The "sector" result is produced by five or six shipping names.
  • Over 24 months: the share explained by the basket was 36% to 47% through 2025, fell to 14% to 20% between February and June 2026, and has climbed back to 43%. Beta fell from about 2.0 to 0.84 and is back at 1.8.

Reading the pattern (inference). The windows with low co-movement cover November 2025 to mid-2026. In that period the shares first rebounded by nearly half from the November 2025 low (NOK 79.5) after the US port fees were suspended, a fee to which the company's exposure (USD 350m to 400m a year) is five to six times Höegh's, and then absorbed cuts to broker estimates in February (after the company's "lower-for-longer" renewals) and the company's guidance cut in May. Since mid-2026 the industry's second boom (charter rates doubling, Chinese exports up 72% in the third quarter) has driven both stocks and the co-movement has returned. So the company-specific part of the share price has been about one policy risk and one cost-driven guidance cut. It has not been about self-help, logistics or the contract book.

The multiple. The 24-month-forward price-to-earnings multiple is 9.5x, against a five-year median of 4.4x and a two-year median of 6.8x. Over twelve months the share price rose 110%; by my arithmetic the 24-month-forward consensus earnings rose about 50% to 55% and the multiple about 35% to 40%. The rise in the multiple came with the industry's re-rating: Höegh's multiple is also at the 100th percentile of its history, both have a sell-side revision score of 15, and both raised equity within six weeks of each other at almost the same price (Höegh at NOK 167 on 25 August 2026; the company at NOK 168 on 6 October 2026). In the years 2022 to 2025, when the company earned more than it does now with a longer backlog, the market paid 3x to 6x.

What this means for predictability. Delivery of contracted earnings will not, by itself, hold the multiple. The multiple will follow the charter rate, monthly Chinese export data, Suez headlines and Höegh's monthly updates, with a beta near 2 to shipping. That is a source of risk the company cannot manage. It is also the one way the gap could work for an investor: if the industry de-rates while the company's contracted earnings are confirmed, the company's cover would become worth paying for in relative terms, as it was in 2026 when DNB Carnegie rated the company Hold and Höegh Sell. That is a relative case, not an absolute one.
