Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F4521st of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have outperformed and underperformed, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. On the stock-market patterns, Wallenius Wilhelmsen fits the late-cycle template: multiples at the top of their own range, a high orderbook, broad estimate upgrades, and a 110% share-price rise over 12 months. Shipping shares historically underperform from this point. On the operating patterns it fits the contracted-carrier template, which outperforms spot carriers late in the cycle and in downturns. The investment return from here historically comes from payouts, not from a higher multiple.

Precedents and rules of thumb, with the company's fit:

  1. Shipping equities peak before earnings, when the orderbook is high and price-to-book is well above 1x. Buy near 0.6x NAV, sell above about 1.5x. Fit: P/B about 1.8x on FY2026 book (DNB's NOK 210 target implies 2.9x) against a 10-year average of 0.7–0.9x. P/E is at the 100th percentile of its 10-year history; the 24-month forward P/E of 9.5x is at the 85th percentile of five years (median 4.4x). Car-carrier shares are up 119% over 12 months, "at or near the dearest points of their ranges" (sector digest). This fits the underperformance pattern.
  2. Supply cycles. The car-carrier orderbook peak of 2008 led to a decade of poor returns: the company's ROIC was 4.7% and EBITDA USD 571m in 2019; net debt reached 6.4x EBITDA in 2020 and the dividend was cancelled. Today's orderbook peaked at 42% (July 2024) and is now 21%, with deliveries ending around 2028 and yards full until 2030. Partial fit. The supply wave is smaller and shorter than 2008–2012, and yard capacity has halved since 2008.
  3. Contract duration decides how fast the peak unwinds (sector digest: "a contract reprices only at renewal"). DNB rated Wallenius Wilhelmsen Hold against Höegh Sell in 2026 because of backlog safety. Fits the favourable pattern.
  4. Integrated against pure play. Pure plays (Höegh) earn higher peak margins (43.5% against 34.4%) and have larger share-price swings in both directions. Integrated operators hold up better in downturns (the logistics buffer). Fits the defensive pattern.
  5. Self-help in downturns. The company's 2020 record: USD 210m of savings from 32 initiatives, 15 ships in cold lay-up, 7 charters redelivered, slow steaming at 13.7 knots. Fits the resilient-operator pattern.
  6. "Lazy cyclical extrapolation" and "False Friends" (Wisdom Library). The risk report's Analysis of Competing Hypotheses names the FALSE FRIEND profile as the winner. Caution: the library's own entry "the vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest" warns that this method structurally favours the false-friend hypothesis. The verdict is a prompt to test, not a proof. Partial fit: near-term cash is real and contracted. What may be "false" is the extrapolation of peak rates into 2028–2030 in consensus and in the multiple. That is visible in FY2028 EPS consensus rising 112% in six months on one broker's change of export assumption.
  7. Peer read-across. Japanese car-carrier profits are already falling (MOL −93% year on year in Q2 2026; NYK guiding automotive profit down; Morgan Stanley sees NYK ROE falling from 10.8% (FY2027) to 4.2% (FY2028)). Historically the earnings of the most spot-exposed operators turn first.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F3020th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. A realistic fair trough is NOK 85–100, a fall of about 40–48% from NOK 162.8. That is roughly 1x book, about 6–7x EV/EBITDA on a mid-cycle EBITDA of USD 1.0bn, and back to the November 2025 low (NOK 79.5). A stress trough is NOK 55–70 (−57% to −66%): 0.6x book, or 6x on USD 1.0bn with higher net debt after capex. Dividends cushion the total-return downside: DNB expects about NOK 40–43 cumulatively through 2028, roughly 25% of the price. The market would price a trough about 6–12 months before 2028 earnings show it, so 2027 is the risk window, even with strong 2027 earnings.

Workings (USD/NOK about 9.6; 440m shares; net debt including leases about USD 2.3–2.5bn after the 2027 capex peak; EUKOR put about USD 0.4bn):

Method Assumption Implied price (NOK) Change
P/B 0.9x (10-year average) on FY2027 book USD 9.75 about 85 −48%
P/B 0.6x (bear case) about 56 −65%
EV/EBITDA 7x on USD 1.0bn mid-cycle (DNB's old normalised figure) about 90 −45%
EV/EBITDA 6x on USD 1.0bn about 67 −59%
EV/EBITDA 2.5x (risk report's bear) equity close to nil not a fair trough
24-month forward P/E 10-year median 5.3x on about USD 1.9 about 97 −40%
24-month forward P/E 10th percentile 3.7x about 68 −58%

At the trough, forward EPS would also be falling, so the P/E route overstates the floor.

Cross-check on today's price. NOK 163 equals about 7x EV/EBITDA on USD 1.4bn, DNB's new "long-term" EBITDA. Today's price therefore already capitalises a structurally higher mid-cycle. The downside is a reversion of that assumption towards the 2010–2019 average (USD 0.85–1.0bn), not an earnings collapse.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C569th of 25

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead has real, dated, contract-founded positives: renewals locked at peak, a likely first 2027 guide, logistics progress, and a 2028 free-cash-flow inflection followed by the HMG repricing opportunity in 2029–2030. But most of it is known and already reflected in a stock up 110% in a year and at the top of its valuation range. Policy and supply negatives arrive on the same calendar. For the next set's purposes, the narrative supports confidence that 2027 earnings rise and that the dividend capacity is real. It does not support assuming a higher multiple. Returns from here are more likely to come from payouts than from re-rating.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–63

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The earlier answers are highly congruent on the shape of the case and on what drives it. One idea runs through both sets and holds together under test: the calendar of the company's contracts, not the spot cycle, sets its earnings. The places where the answers jar are few, but they are not random. They cluster in three areas: (1) everything below EBITDA and in cash (the minority, depreciation, leases, dividends); (2) the gap between what management says about renewal prices and what the company's reported numbers yet show; and (3) the year 2028, where the first set's "weakest year for the industry" sits uneasily beside the second set's "protected year" for the company. None of the three overturns the case. Each lowers the confidence that can be placed in earnings per share, cash and 2028, as distinct from 2027 EBITDA.

Where the answers support each other.

  • Pricing power that is cyclical and tied to contract dates (first set C-45, C-17) is the same idea as the second set's price path: up in 2027 on the renewing 41%, held in 2028, given back in 2029–2030 (P-04, P-05).
  • A chief executive who tracks rather than sandbags (C-70) matches the delivery record the second set leaned on: EBITDA lands within 5% of guidance, net profit undershoots (P-06).
  • High gearing of earnings per share to EBITDA (C-49, C-75) matches the second set's 1.9 times.
  • The late-cycle reading of the shares (C-82, C-83, C-84) matches the model's negative return at the guideline multiple. The two are consistent in a way worth stating: even holding the multiple at the top of its ten-year range (7.69x), the return is negative, because the two-year-forward earnings window moves from 2027–2028 into 2029–2030, where the second set has earnings falling. At the ten-year median multiple (5.3x) on those 2029–2030 earnings the share value would be about NOK 85 (my arithmetic), which is the first set's fair trough of NOK 85 to 100.

Where they jar, and how I resolve each.

  1. Dividends: the liquidity floor against the owners' need for cash. The first set recorded both that liquidity is at the USD 1bn floor (C-19, C-58) and that the controlling families depend on the dividend (C-09, C-87: Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding draws almost half its free cash flow to equity from the company). The second set let the floor win and cut the 2027 dividend to 1.05 a share against a consensus of 1.58. The company's conduct points the other way. It paid about USD 427m for the second half of 2025 against about USD 413m of attributable net profit, and USD 258m for the first half of 2026 (including a USD 100m extraordinary dividend) against about USD 283m, with liquidity at 1,026; that is 90% to 100% of profit (my arithmetic on FactSet's reported net income). It then raised USD 300m of equity for the new ships a month later, and presented the raise as a way to keep a robust balance sheet while continuing to deliver competitive returns to shareholders. Post-delivery bank loans for about 70% of the price are arranged for 11 of the 14 Shapers. The second set's equity free cash flow leaves those loans out, so it understates the cash the board will regard as available: about USD 370m of free cash flow in 2027 plus roughly USD 500m to 600m of delivery loans, less scheduled repayments. I therefore raise dividends paid to 1.40 in 2027 and 1.45 in 2028 (about 70% to 78% of earnings after the minority). The cost is higher debt: net debt including leases of about 1.35 to 1.45 times EBITDA at end-2027, far inside the 3.0 times limit. The second set's conclusion that consensus is far too generous on 2027 dividends does not survive: like for like, I am a little below the consensus median and well below DNB Carnegie (1.83 and 2.01).

  2. Charter costs: a rising headwind (first set C-05) that falls below EBITDA (second set) against a lease book that is in fact shrinking. See the preface. The second set was right that long-term charters sit below EBITDA under IFRS 16, and right that EBITDA therefore flatters. It was too pessimistic about the size: the company is replacing and buying out chartered ships, not re-fixing them at USD 80,000 to 110,000 a day. I lower depreciation by USD 15m to 25m a year. The first set's "net headwind" on input costs was a fair description of the first half of 2026 (the May guidance cut) and is a poor description of 2027.

  3. Management's words on renewal prices against the reported numbers. Both sets rest the 2027 forecast on the statement that 2027–2029 contracts carry rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions", and the second set turned that into a like-for-like rise of about USD 18 per cbm on the renewing tranche. No number the company has yet reported shows it. The book's average rate went from 55 to 54 during 2026; the third-quarter rise in the reported rate was mostly mix; and DNB Carnegie, which upgraded after the awards, models the reported rate down to USD 60.6 in 2027 where the second set has it up to 68.1. The assumption is congruent with the awards, with peers' prints (Höegh Autoliners above USD 100 per cbm on new contracts; USD 81.8 across its book in October 2026) and with the market, but it remains an inference. It is tested on one date, 17 February 2027 (D-05).

  4. 2028: weakest year for the industry, protected year for the company. The reconciliation offered is that contracts hold the price. They do; they do not hold the volume. Contracts oblige customers to deliver volumes, but the chief executive has said the company "works with them to defer or move commitments" when volumes fall short, and there is no evidence of take-or-pay. The second set's 2028 has volume up 0.5% in a year when DNB Carnegie has effective fleet supply growing faster than demand (6.9% against 4.9% a year over 2026–2029), customers' own fleets growing (BYD eight ships, SAIC Anji 21) and local assembly starting (BYD Hungary about 200,000 units in 2027). 2028 is therefore the year in which the most likely outcome and the average outcome differ most. I keep the base case and flag the skew.

  5. "Sold out" against falling volume. The first set's capacity-capped frame is true out of Asia only. Third-quarter volume fell 3% on the quarter because trades out of Europe and North America and high-and-heavy cargo (26% of volume) are weak. On those trades volume depends on demand, not on ships.

  6. Sell-side: "no cushion" (first set C-74) against "consensus too low in 2028" (second set). Both hold. Near-year estimates have been raised hard; the 2028 median is held down by one outlier and one stale estimate.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F387th of 8

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The narrative is complete on the cycle and thin on the company. Everything that makes the case attractive is fully articulated by the sell-side and is in a share price at the top of its history: the Chinese export boom, the capacity shortage, charter rates, renewals "at market", the dividend yield, the fleet renewal. The standard objections are equally well aired and explain the two Hold ratings: a cyclical peak, the orderbook, Suez, trade barriers, port fees. What is not articulated is what our own work adds, and it divides unevenly. One element is favourable and only half known: 2028 is protected by contract. Several are unfavourable and hardly known at all: the quality of EBITDA under lease accounting, the minority, the coincidence of contract expiries at the end of 2029, the EUKOR put, the pull-forward of Chinese shipments, the exclusion of port fees from adjusted EBITDA, and contracts that fix price but not volume. For investor commentary the files hold no evidence: the report written for this question covered brokers only, and in practice one broker. I can infer investors' position from the price and the register, not from what they say.

Each dynamic: who discusses it, how well known it is, and which way it cuts against what is priced.

Dynamic Who discusses it How well known Effect relative to what is priced
Chinese export boom; EU hybrid cap, VAT rebate cut, tariffs, local assembly Every shipping broker, the autos analysts, the company Fully Priced. Risk is on the downside if growth slows faster than the 10% to 15% now assumed
Capacity shortage, charter rates at USD 95,000 a day, orderbook back to 27% of the fleet Every broker Fully Priced
Return to Suez DNB Carnegie (gradual through 2027; rerouting adds about 6% to demand), Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Jefferies Fully. The size is often overstated (10% to 15% of capacity in commentary; about 5% in the models) A known risk to the multiple; small for contracted 2027 earnings
US port fees DNB Carnegie, law firms The risk: fully. That adjusted EBITDA leaves the cost out and that recovery from customers is unproven: hardly Mildly negative when understood
2027 renewals at higher prices The company, DNB Carnegie Direction: fully. Size: unknown to all. That the reported rate will mislead because of mix: DNB Carnegie and the company only Neutral: our base is in line with the leading estimates
2028 held by three-year contracts In the estimates of DNB Carnegie and Clarksons Securities; not in Nordea's, Pareto's or Fearnley's Half known Favourable: our 2028 is 8% to 9% above the FactSet median and about 14% above Bloomberg's
Dividend yield and the parent's need for it DNB Carnegie (10.3% for 2027) The attraction: fully. The limits (liquidity floor, peak capital spending, lease principal): modelled by one broker, tested by none Slightly negative: we expect about 8% on today's price, not over 10%
Lease accounting: charter costs below EBITDA; lease principal of USD 350m to 430m a year as a claim on cash DNB Carnegie models it; nobody debates it. Valuation on a multiple of EBITDA passes over it Hardly Negative for the quality of the headline; less so than the second set thought, because the lease book is shrinking
EUKOR minority of 9% to 10% of profit DNB Carnegie models USD 81m to 88m; no discussion Hardly (our own model omits it) Negative where earnings before the minority are used
Contract expiries concentrated at end-2029 (the 2026 three-year contracts, HMG, and Hyundai Glovis's HMG contract on the same date) Nobody. DNB Carnegie presents the lock-in only as a positive Not at all Negative from 2028, when 2029–2030 enters the forward window
EUKOR put and call from 2031, priced on EUKOR's profits; HMG as customer, co-owner and owner of the rival carrier; Hyundai Glovis adding 20% to its fleet in 2027 Korean brokers on Glovis; DNB Carnegie on the put in 2024 Hardly Negative for 2029–2031
Shipments pulled forward ahead of December 2026 and April 2027, with a gap after Autos analysts (Jefferies, Bernstein); not shipping analysts Hardly, among shipping investors Negative for volume in the second half of 2027
Ordering restarted, including the company's own 4 + 8 ships at near-peak prices; both listed car carriers raised equity at about NOK 167 to 168 Reported as facts; the inference is not drawn Facts known Companies that know the market best are selling shares and buying ships at the top of the cycle
Cars carried in containers (2m to 4m a year) returning to car carriers NH Investment & Securities, DNB Carnegie Known as a support. The other side (a container orderbook of 41% to 44% of the fleet making boxes cheap again in 2027) is hardly discussed Two-sided

What the brokers actually disagree about. It is not 2027. Four of five are within 2% of each other on 2027 EBITDA. It is the multiple to pay for contracted earnings at a cyclical peak. Clarksons Securities has the highest 2028 forecast (1,912) and the lowest price target (USD 16.2, below the share price) and rates the shares Hold. Nordea has 2028 EBITDA falling to 1,666 and rates them Buy with a target of USD 21.6. Ratings and forecasts do not line up, which shows that the argument is about valuation method: net asset value and mid-cycle earnings on one side, contracted cash flow and yield on the other. DNB Carnegie's answer, seven times a long-term EBITDA it raised from USD 1.0bn to 1.4bn, has become the market's: today's price is about seven times USD 1.4bn (the first set's cross-check). The keystone of the narrative is therefore an assumption about the 2030s, which no report over the next 18 months can confirm.

Investor commentary. There is none in the files, and I do not invent it. What the price and the register show:

  • Ownership by momentum and yield. The shares are up 110% in twelve months, in the 95th percentile of the dashboard's universe for relative strength, with the 200-day average rising in every one of the last twelve months. The number of shareholders doubled in the two years to November 2025 (first set). These are consistent with a broad following drawn by the dividend and the trend.
  • A stall at the placement price. The price (NOK 162.8) is below the October placement price (NOK 168) and 6% below its 50-day average. Holders from the placement are at a loss.
  • Some scepticism is already expressed. Short interest was 2.2 days of trading against 0.5 at Höegh Autoliners (first set).
  • The yield will hold up. On my dividend inputs the shares yield about 8% on dividends paid in 2027. A yield buyer is unlikely to be forced out by the dividend itself, contrary to the second set's concern.

The next 18 months against 18 months to three years.

  • Next 18 months. The events that will move the narrative are all known and watched: the February guidance, the port-fee dates, Suez, monthly Chinese exports. The little-known items that can bite inside this period are three: earnings per share and cash falling short of what EBITDA suggests; a volume gap in the second half of 2027; and the fading of estimate upgrades.
  • Eighteen months to three years. This is where the unknown items concentrate: the end-2029 expiries, the first sight of falling 2029 estimates, the Jinling instalments and options (with the possibility of raising equity again), the put in 2031, and the argument over what mid-cycle EBITDA is. None is discussed today. Each arrives in the forward window by the calendar alone, from the second half of 2027.

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 10 November 2026, 14:13 to 14:34 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO): First set of questions — the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-09/10 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (second set). All money in USD unless stated; the share price is in NOK.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface for the Consumer: what the files establish, the one frame that matters, and the data traps

Source check (the caution in the instruction). The latest Earnings Review does cover the period to 30 September 2026: it states the Q3 2026 interim report was published on 4 November 2026, and its Q3 figures (revenue USD 1,401m, attributable net profit USD 192m, basic EPS USD 0.45) match the FactSet block's Q3-FY2026 column (revenue 1,401; net income 192; EPS 0.454). It can be relied on. The next results date is 17 February 2027.

The one frame that matters for the model. Wallenius Wilhelmsen's earnings for 2027 to 2029 are being fixed now, in Q4 2026, when it renews about 40% of its shipping volume. Car-carrier capacity is at its tightest on record. Fleet utilisation is 96% to 98%, one-year charter rates for a 6,500 CEU ship reached USD 100k a day in October 2026, and Asia–Europe spot rates peaked at USD 170 per cbm. The company's legacy contract book averages about USD 54 per cbm. Three consequences follow, and the rest of these answers keep returning to them:

  1. The spot cycle mainly affects the 2028–2030 renewal rounds, the cost of the roughly one third of the fleet that is chartered in, H&H and spot cargo, and the stock-market multiple. It does not move the contracted 2027 revenue line much.
  2. The renewal rates for 2027 are the largest single unknown in the model. They are commercially confidential, and the reported KPI ("net freight rate per cbm") is distorted by cargo mix. Even DNB Carnegie, the most bullish broker, models the net freight rate down from USD 63.40 per cbm in 2026 to USD 60.60 in 2027 and 2028. Consensus therefore embeds little like-for-like repricing.
  3. One large block of volume cannot reprice. The Hyundai Motor Group (HMG)/EUKOR contract (USD 4.2bn over five years from late 2024, at about USD 55 per cbm, with HMG committing at least 50% of its exports) is fixed to 2029. By my arithmetic that is roughly USD 840m of revenue a year, about a quarter of shipping volume, fixed near the legacy average.

Data traps verified in the files (read before using any figure):

  • Two EBITDA bases. The company's adjusted EBITDA equals the brokers' basis (FY2025: 1,811 company, 1,814 FactSet brokers' basis). FactSet's "operating" EBITDA runs about USD 415m a year (about USD 100m a quarter) higher (FY2025: 2,229; Q3 2026: 528 against a company-adjusted 428). Model on the company/brokers' basis only.
  • FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line is after dividends. I checked this for every year from FY2019 to FY2025: it equals operating cash flow less capex less dividends paid (FY2025: 1,640 − 245 − 989 = 406; FY2024: 1,644 − 195 − 738 = 711). The peer table's "FCF/net income 0.4x" for FY2025 is therefore not weak conversion. Pre-dividend FCF (OCF − capex) was about 1,395 in FY2025, 1.37x reported net income. The consensus FCF line (840/961/1,196) appears to be pre-dividend (OCF 1,455 − capex 567 ≈ 888). No FCF line deducts lease payments, and lease liabilities were USD 1,538m at end-2025.
  • Share count. 17.15m new shares were issued in October 2026 at NOK 168, taking the count from 422.8m to about 440m (+4.1%). FactSet diluted shares are 439.5m. FY2027 per-share figures must use about 440m.
  • Net debt. The company's net interest-bearing debt (USD 2,119m at Q3 2026; leverage 1.3x) includes leases. FactSet's enterprise-value snapshot uses debt of only USD 1,187m, which appears to exclude leases, and so understates EV by about USD 1.5bn. The EUKOR put liability (about USD 386m after the April 2026 deferral to 2031) is debt-like and appears in neither.
  • Thin coverage. Five brokers. The FY2028 consensus is unusually dispersed (EBITDA 1,390 at Fearnley to 1,912 at Clarksons Securities; EPS 1.55 to 2.24).
  • Dividends per share in FactSet are dated by payment period, not by the year the dividend is declared for.
  • Conflicting figures in the digests: Q2 2026 EBITDA margin (27.7%/28% company; "36.2%" in one Höegh digest, on another basis); market share (16.1% of capacity at end-2025 per DNB Carnegie, against the older "20%"); fleet (115 to 129 vessels, depending on date and whether Government and chartered ships are counted). The Q2 Earnings Review attributes 2026 balance-sheet quotes to "CFO Torbjørn Wist", who left in July 2024; the CFO is Bjørnar Bukholm.
  • FX. USD/NOK is about 9.6 (FactSet: USD 16.95 a share against NOK 163.2). I use that rate for conversions.

Anchor figures (company basis unless stated): revenue 2023/2024/2025 = 5,149/5,308/5,240. Adjusted EBITDA 2024 = 1,901 (record); 2025 = 1,811; 2026 guidance about 1,600. Year-to-date 2026 adjusted EBITDA = 389 + 361 + 428 = 1,178, so Q4 needs about 422 (Q3 was 428). Shipping backlog at Q3 2026 was USD 5.8bn with a 2.8-year duration, down from 7.8bn in Q3 2025, followed by USD 1.7bn of awards after the quarter. Logistics backlog: USD 2.6bn, 7.9 years. Q2 2026 volume was 14.5m cbm, so roughly 57–60m cbm a year. Net freight rate: USD 41.4 (2020), 64.6 (2025), 63.2 (Q1 2026), 61.4 (Q2 2026). Fleet: 121 shipping vessels at Q1 2026 (80 owned, 41 chartered in) plus 10 Government vessels. Shaper newbuild capex remaining was USD 1.4bn at Q2 2026 (schedule at end-2025: 430/665/342 for 2026/2027/2028), plus a new Jinling order of 4 firm and 8 option vessels (about USD 1.5bn if all 12 are taken), delivering from mid-2030. Price NOK 162.8 (9 November 2026); 52-week range NOK 79.5–186; market cap about USD 7.45bn.

Two simple sensitivities for the model (my arithmetic, approximate): USD 1 per cbm of net freight rate is worth about USD 57–60m of annual revenue and EBITDA, roughly 3.5% of 2026 EBITDA guidance. Every USD 10 per cbm achieved above the USD 54 legacy average on the 40% renewal tranche (about 23m cbm) is worth about USD 230–240m a year, roughly 14–15% of 2026 EBITDA, before any cost offsets. The FY2027 consensus EBITDA increase over FY2026 (+USD 208m, median) is equivalent to only about USD 9 per cbm on that tranche. Consensus is therefore not pricing a large repricing, or it assumes most of it is absorbed by costs (charter re-fixing, port fees, ETS, weaker H&H mix).

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO), the Oslo-listed global roll-on/roll-off ocean carrier and vehicle logistics group, with three segments: Shipping Services (deep-sea transport of cars, trucks and high-and-heavy cargo, including the EUKOR Car Carriers joint venture with Hyundai Motor Group), Logistics Services (marine terminals, vehicle and equipment processing centres, inland distribution) and Government Services (US-flag vessels serving the US government)

Request: T-P1

Peers: Höegh Autoliners (Oslo-listed pure-play car carrier, the closest listed comparator); Hyundai Glovis (Korean car carrier with captive Hyundai Motor Group volumes); NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha (Japanese car carrier majors inside diversified shipping groups, with separately reported automotive or car carrier segments); Grimaldi Group (private Mediterranean and Atlantic RoRo operator); COSCO Shipping and China Merchants Energy Shipping (Chinese state-linked car carrier operators); CEVA Logistics (finished vehicle logistics inside CMA CGM, a land-side comparator) Inputs: What is the split of the company's cost base between owned-vessel costs (vessel operating expenses, depreciation), chartered-in capacity (short-term charters in operating costs versus long-term charters under IFRS 16), voyage costs (fuel net of bunker adjustment factor recovery, port and cargo handling), terminal and processing fixed costs, and SG&A? How many of the chartered-in vessels (41 at Q1 2026, per the company) roll off in 2027 and 2028, and at what rates are comparable 6,500 to 7,000 CEU charters being fixed now (the company reported one-year rates of USD 100k per day for 6,500 CEU and USD 110k per day for 7,000 CEU dual-fuel in October 2026)? How did margins move with volumes and rates for the company and its peers in 2008-2010, 2015-2016, 2018-2020 (including the Q2 2020 ocean volume fall of 45% the company reported) and 2021-2024? What did past cost programmes deliver against what was promised: the merger synergy target of USD 120 million, the USD 100 million performance programme, the 32 cash-saving initiatives worth USD 210 million in 2020, and the vessel lay-ups, charter redeliveries and slow steaming used then? What is the status of the current programmes: SG&A savings of more than USD 30 million, the USD 50 million digital programme with Cognizant, and the target to raise the Logistics cash EBITDA margin from 2% to 10% by 2028? How do the operating costs and fuel use of the Shaper-class newbuilds (up to 11,700-12,100 CEU, dual-fuel) compare with chartered 6,500 CEU tonnage, and how does Höegh's stated comparison of Aurora-class cash cost against market charters translate to the company's fleet? How fixed are terminal and vehicle processing costs when OEM volumes fall? Focus: How much of the company's EBITDA would survive a fall in volumes and in renewal rates in 2028-2029, given that about a third of its fleet is chartered in and 14 newbuilds deliver in 2026-2028, and how much cost could realistically be taken out, and how fast, in that downturn? Is the Logistics margin target achievable, judged against peer finished-vehicle-logistics margins?

Request: T-P2

Peers: Höegh Autoliners (pure-play peer repricing contracts faster); Hyundai Glovis (captive and spot cargo with Chinese OEMs); NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha (Japanese majors; contract mix and recent profit trends); Grimaldi Group (private competitor on Atlantic and Mediterranean trades); Chinese state-linked carriers COSCO Shipping and China Merchants Energy Shipping, and OEM-owned fleets such as BYD's and SAIC Anji's (alternative capacity for Chinese exporters) Operating conditions: a capacity shortage with record charter and spot rates (2021-2026); oversupply with falling utilisation (2009-2013 and 2016-2020); a sudden collapse in demand (the 2020 pandemic); fuel price spikes with lagged surcharge recovery (2022 and 2026); regulatory and political cost shocks (EU ETS phase-in, FuelEU Maritime, US USTR port fees on foreign-built car carriers); and a return of car carriers to the Suez Canal and Red Sea route Inputs: At what rates per cubic metre, and for what durations, are the company's 2027-2029 renewals being signed, against its legacy contract book average of about USD 54 per cbm (company presentation, 17 September 2026) and against Höegh's new contract net rates above USD 100 per cbm (Höegh, Q2 2026)? What terms did the November 2026 awards carry (USD 700 million over three years with a leading Asian manufacturer; USD 950 million plus a USD 100 million option with a leading equipment manufacturer)? How did the contracting approach DNB Carnegie described as "lower-for-longer" extensions (12 February 2026) change by Q3 2026? What are the terms of the Hyundai Motor Group/EUKOR contract (reported as USD 4.2 billion over five years from late 2024 at about USD 55 per cbm), and when and how does it reprice? Do contracts set volume shares or minimum volumes? How do bunker adjustment factor (BAF 2.0) and EU ETS clauses work, and how complete is recovery? Can the USTR port fees be recovered from customers under existing contracts? What drives the reported net freight rate per cbm through cargo mix (high-and-heavy versus cars, Asia versus Atlantic trades), as distinct from like-for-like price? How did car carrier contract and spot rates behave in each named condition, with dates and figures? Are customers paying green premiums for low-carbon services, and how much? What price ceiling does the shipping of cars in containers and on flat racks set? Focus: What like-for-like price increase is the company achieving on the 2027 renewals, how much of it reaches EBITDA after cost pass-through and port-fee recovery, and how well would those rates hold at the next renewals in 2028-2030 if fleet utilisation falls and the Red Sea reopens?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The company's earnings path for 2027-2030 depends on interacting loops of capacity scarcity, contracting, newbuild ordering, scrapping, trade policy and routing, which can reinforce or offset one another and change the timing of the cycle turn. Peers: Höegh Autoliners (pure-play car carrier, most exposed to the rate cycle); Hyundai Glovis (captive volumes and fleet expansion); NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha (incumbent fleets and ordering behaviour); Clarkson (shipbroker with research on ordering, scrapping and charter cycles); Chinese state-linked carriers COSCO Shipping and China Merchants Energy Shipping (new capacity behaviour) Inputs: Start from these possible dynamics. (1) High rates lead to newbuild ordering, deliveries and later oversupply: the global car carrier orderbook peaked at 42.4% of the fleet in July 2024 and was about 21% in 2026 (DNB Carnegie), with about 460,000 CEU due in 2027 (NH Investment & Securities, 12 October 2026) and shipyard slots available only from 2030 (company, 24 September 2026). (2) High rates suppress scrapping, which stores up capacity for the downturn: zero car carrier recycling in 2024 and 2025 per Höegh and DNB Carnegie, while about 10% of the fleet will exceed 30 years by 2030 (Pareto Securities, 16 September 2026). (3) Scarcity pushes cars into containers and flat racks, and those volumes return to car carriers as capacity frees, absorbing new deliveries: 1.0 to 1.5 million cars in H1 2026 (NH Investment & Securities). (4) Chinese domestic weakness pushes exports, which triggers import restrictions and local assembly abroad and reduces long-haul tonne-miles: the EU-China hybrid cap from 1 December 2026, China's export VAT rebate cut from April 2027, Brazil's and Mexico's tariffs, BYD's Hungarian and Brazilian plants. (5) Scarcity lengthens customer contracts, which locks in earnings and delays the effect of a market downturn on carriers with long backlogs. (6) Red Sea and Hormuz rerouting absorbs capacity but raises fuel costs with lagged recovery. (7) Carbon regulation (EU ETS, FuelEU Maritime, IMO measures) accelerates the retirement of older ships and favours dual-fuel fleets. Focus: Which of these loops will dominate in 2027-2029, in what order, and what do past car carrier and container shipping cycles show about how long each loop ran and what stopped it, so that the timing of a rate turn and its effect on contract renewals can be judged?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The company's earnings per share depend heavily on items below EBITDA and on the share count, which are changing at the same time: newbuild depreciation and financing, the EUKOR minority and put option, a low tonnage-tax rate, recurring vessel sale gains, and the October 2026 share issue. Peers: Höegh Autoliners (pure-play peer with a newbuild programme, tonnage-tax regime and recent equity raise); Hyundai Glovis (car carrier with large fleet expansion and KRW reporting); Wilh. Wilhelmsen Holding (parent holding company, for dividend and capital-allocation interplay); Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and NYK Line (car carrier earnings within diversified groups, for normalised-earnings comparison) Inputs:

  • Shaper-class delivery dates and remaining instalments: USD 1.4 billion remaining at Q2 2026 (company); schedule of USD 430 million, 665 million and 342 million for 2026-2028 at end-2025 (company).
  • Depreciation lives (27-30 years per company accounting policy) and the resulting depreciation step-up.
  • Post-delivery bank financing for 11 of 14 newbuilds on 18-22 year profiles (company).
  • Capex and payment schedule for the four firm and eight optional 9,900 CEU vessels ordered at China Merchants Jinling for delivery from mid-2030 (company, 28 October 2026).
  • Lease liabilities and lease payments for chartered-in vessels.
  • Net interest cost and interest-rate hedging (about 70% hedged at end-2025 per company).
  • The share of group profit and dividends going to the 20% non-controlling holder in EUKOR, and the accounting and KRW sensitivity of the EUKOR put option deferred to 2031 (company; USD 81 million per 10% KRW move).
  • The effective tax rate under the Norwegian tonnage-tax regime and Pillar Two.
  • Vessel sale gains: for example, the Asian Captain sale expected in Q4 2026 with a USD 40 million gain (company).
  • The share count after the 17.15 million new shares issued in October 2026.
  • Dividend policy (30-50% of net profit semi-annually plus extraordinary distributions) and its interaction with capex and the company's USD 1 billion minimum liquidity target.
  • The possible cost of US port fees if not recovered. Focus: What is a well-evidenced bridge from adjusted EBITDA to earnings per share and to free cash flow to equity for 2026-2029, and which of these inputs are most likely to move earnings per share materially away from the sell-side's assumptions?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 10 November 2026, 20:29 to 20:44 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO): Second set of questions — the operating and financial model, FY2026 to FY2030

Written 2026-11-10 for the Investment Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets. All money in USD millions unless stated; per-share figures in USD; the share price is in NOK (USD/NOK about 9.6). "EBITDA" means the company's adjusted EBITDA, which equals the brokers' basis (FY2025: 1,811 company, 1,814 FactSet brokers' basis). FactSet's "operating" EBITDA is about USD 415m a year higher and is not used anywhere below.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface for the Consumer: the base case in one page, and what changed since the first set

The five judgements that drive everything below.

  1. 2027 is a price year, and the price reaches EBITDA better than the first set implied, but EPS worse. About 40% of shipping volume (about 23m cbm) renews for 2027 into the tightest market on record. I model a like-for-like uplift of about USD 18 per cbm on that tranche (from about USD 56 on the expiring contracts to about USD 74), about 80% effective within 2027: about USD 330m of revenue. After mix, cost inflation, an expected port-fee cost and bunker normalisation, EBITDA rises by about USD 270m to 1,870, 2.4% above consensus. But D&A (+35) and net financial items (-20) rise faster than consensus assumes, so my FY2027 EPS (1.96) is slightly below consensus (1.99). Record-level EBITDA will not produce record EPS: EPS stays about 15–19% below FY2024–FY2025 throughout the forecast.
  2. The charter-cost offset sits mostly below EBITDA, not in it (a correction to the first set). Under IFRS 16 the 34 long-term chartered-in vessels are leases: re-fixing them at USD 80–110k a day raises depreciation, interest and lease cash payments, not operating costs. Only short-term charters (USD 122m of "vessel charter expenses" in FY2025) sit in EBITDA. So EBITDA flatters the repricing; equity free cash flow and EPS bear the cost.
  3. 2028 is protected, not a down year. By the end of 2026 the company will have contracted "the vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity, and the Hyundai Motor Group (HMG)/EUKOR block is fixed to end-2029. My FY2028 EBITDA (1,830) is 9% above the median (1,679), which is dragged down by a stale September estimate (Pareto) and one outlier (Fearnley, 1,390); the two most recent full updates (DNB Carnegie 1,908, Clarksons Securities 1,912) are above me.
  4. The earnings risk is pushed to 2029–2030, where three things coincide: the 3-year contracts signed in 2026 for 2027–2029 all expire together at end-2029, the HMG/EUKOR contract (about a quarter of volume at about USD 55 per cbm) renews for 2030, and the first 4 new Jinling ships arrive (mid-2030) with their capex. I model EBITDA falling to 1,760 (2029) and 1,660 (2030), still well above the 2010s (571 in 2019) and above DNB's "long-term" USD 1.4bn.
  5. Cash, not earnings, is the binding constraint in 2026–2027. Equity free cash flow after lease principal (about USD 380–410m a year, never shown in FactSet's free cash flow or consensus) falls to about USD 370–390m in 2026–2027 against about USD 1.0bn in 2023–2025, because Shaper capex peaks. Dividends must fall from USD 2.34 a share paid in 2025 to about USD 1.05 paid in 2027 unless the company borrows to pay them; net debt including leases rises from 1,728 (end-2025) to about 2.4–2.5bn by 2028, still only about 1.3–1.4x EBITDA. Free cash flow inflects in 2028–2029 as Shaper capex ends.

What changed in the files since the first set, and where I disagree with it.

  • The US port-fee date has moved. The first set treated about 10 November 2026 as a binary it could not resolve. The pricing report cites the USTR's proposal of a two-month extension of the Section 301 suspension (Holland & Knight, 26 October 2026), and China's reciprocal fees are suspended to January 2027 (Global Ship Lease presentation, 9 November 2026). Q4 2026 is therefore very probably free of port fees; the binary moves to about January 2027. I also note that the company added back the USD 21m of Q4 2025 port fees to adjusted EBITDA, so port fees may not appear in adjusted EBITDA or guidance even when they cost cash.
  • The leading broker has pushed the downturn out by a year. In August DNB Carnegie had one-year charter rates for a 6,500 CEU ship falling to USD 51.3k a day in 2027 and utilisation to 85% (2027) and 80% (2028); its 3 November 2026 path is USD 75.6k (2026), 79.7k (2027), 62.0k (2028), 57.8k (2029), with utilisation still 88% in 2029. The first set's C-10 used the August path. This supports holding 2027 pricing and moving the normalisation into 2028–2029.
  • The orderbook loop has restarted. The PCTC orderbook fell from 42% (July 2024) to 21% (March 2026) and rebounded to 27% by October 2026 (DNB Carnegie, 3 November 2026), including the company's own Jinling order and Höegh's six extra Aurora ships. The first set described ordering as a finished wave; it is not.
  • Shaper commitment and logistics have moved on. Remaining Shaper capex fell to about USD 1.2bn at 30 September 2026 (from 1.4bn at Q2). The Logistics cash EBITDA margin reached 7.6% in Q3 2026 (from 1% in late 2025; target 10% by 2028), so the target is three-quarters delivered, two years early.
  • I disagree with the first set on two modelling points. (a) Its sensitivity framing ("USD 10 per cbm on the renewal tranche is worth USD 230–240m of EBITDA ... before cost offsets, mainly charter re-fixing") overstates how much of the charter cost hits EBITDA (judgement 2). (b) It reconciled the FY2026 consensus EPS of 1.66 to the Asian Captain gain. The brokers' basis excluded gains in FY2025 (brokers' EPS 2.20 against reported 2.41, the difference being mostly the USD 135m MIRRAT gain), so 1.66 probably does not include the gain; it is simply about 4% too high on underlying Q4 earnings (my adjusted FY2026 EPS is 1.60; about 1.68 including the gain).
  • A volume figure conflicts: Q2 2026 shipping volume is 14.5m cbm in the Q2 review and 13.6m cbm in a Höegh digest. I use 14.5m (about 57–58m cbm a year).

Shipping is the swing segment: about 85% of EBITDA and all of the price leverage. Logistics' accounting margin includes terminal and processing lease add-backs under IFRS 16; its cash EBITDA margin was 7.6% in Q3 2026 against the company's 10% target for 2028. Government is held flat (the 2025 base included vessel transfers; Q2 2026 missed DNB Carnegie by 33%).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Price on the renewal tranche is the main driver. About 40% of shipping volume (about 23m cbm) renews for 2027. I assume a like-for-like uplift of about USD 18 per cbm (from an expiring average of about USD 54–58 to about USD 74), about 80% effective within 2027: about USD 330m of revenue, of which about 70% (USD 230m) reaches EBITDA after cargo-mix dilution (more Chinese cars, less high-and-heavy), a probability-weighted port-fee cost (about USD 30m) and cost inflation. Benchmarks: Höegh signs new contracts above USD 100 per cbm net; spot USD 170 per cbm; one-year charters USD 95–110k a day; management says 2027–2029 contracts carry rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions".
  • 2028: the 2027 renewals carry over (+USD 60m), a further 20–25% of volume renews at about +USD 5–6 per cbm (+60m), and spot and short-term trades (about 10% of volume) soften by about USD 15 per cbm (-85m) as one-year charter rates fall to about USD 62k a day (DNB Carnegie, 3 November 2026). EBITDA roughly flat.
  • 2029–2030: spot weaker; the three-year 2027 contracts reset about USD 9 per cbm lower for 2030 while the HMG/EUKOR block (about a quarter of volume, fixed at about USD 55 per cbm to end-2029) reprices about USD 7 higher. Reported net freight rate: 63.3 (2026), 68.1, 67.3, 65.0, 62.0 (2030).
  • Volume is fleet-capped: 57.5m cbm (2026), +2% in 2027, about +0.5% a year after. Shapers (14 ships; about 3 delivered by end-2026, about 10 by end-2027, all 14 in 2028) replace chartered and old tonnage more than they add capacity. Contracts set a share of each customer's volume, not minimums.
  • Costs: bunker normalisation adds about USD 30m in 2027 (H1 2026 carried about USD 55–60m of excess lagged fuel cost; Brent at USD 111 in October creates a smaller lag in Q4 2026 and Q1 2027). Cost self-help (logistics margin programme, USD 30m+ SG&A savings, Shaper fuel efficiency, fewer short-term charters) adds about USD 95–120m by 2028 but roughly offsets about USD 40m a year of cost inflation.
  • IFRS 16: re-fixing the 34 long-term chartered vessels at peak rates raises D&A, lease interest and lease payments rather than EBITDA; only short-term charters (USD 122m in FY2025) sit in EBITDA.
  • D&A: nine-month 2026 run-rate USD 515m; Shapers add about USD 4m a year each; right-of-use depreciation rises in 2027 and falls from 2028; first 4 Jinling ships from mid-2030.
  • Net financial items: consensus-consistent for 2026 (about -125); higher from 2027 as post-delivery Shaper loans are drawn (about USD 500m in 2027), cash sits near the USD 1bn liquidity floor and lease interest rises. About 70% hedged.
  • Tax: Norwegian tonnage tax; reported 3.6–6.5% in 2022–2025; Pillar Two top-up negligible (USD 0.8m in Q3 2026).
  • Capex: remaining Shaper commitment about USD 1.2bn at 30 September 2026 (about 150 in Q4 2026, about 680 in 2027, about 350 in 2028); Jinling 4 firm ships (about USD 500m; deposits 2026–2029, about 60–70% on delivery in 2030); option deposits from 2028 assuming the 8 options are declared gradually; maintenance, dry-docking, terminals and digital about USD 150m a year.
  • Lease payments are the principal portion only (Q3 2026 lease repayments were USD 130m including interest; lease liabilities USD 1,459m); lease interest is inside net financial items. New leases (non-cash additions of about USD 200–300m a year) raise net debt including leases.
  • Disposals: Asian Captain (Q4 2026; about USD 52m of proceeds, USD 40m gain) and further old-ship sales as Shapers arrive.
  • Equity: 17.15m new shares at NOK 168 on 6 October 2026, about USD 295m net, taking the count from 422.8m to about 440.0m; entered as new shares issued for cash, so the share-count line excludes them. No buybacks assumed.
  • Dividends per share are the amounts paid in each fiscal year (cash-flow basis): 2026 = USD 1.01 (H2 2025, paid H1 2026) + 0.61 (H1 2026, paid September 2026) on 422.8m shares. 2027 falls to about 1.05 (no extraordinary while capex peaks and liquidity sits near the USD 1bn floor); extraordinaries resume in 2028–2029 as Shaper capex ends. Policy: 30–50% of net profit semi-annually plus extraordinaries.
  • Minority interest is not a line on this form. HMG owns 20% of EUKOR: about 9–10% of group net profit (about USD 71m in 2026, 86m in 2027, 80m, 76m and 73m) and about USD 60m a year of cash dividends to the minority. The model's EPS and free cash flow will be about 9–10% above my cross-check unless it deducts these.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026: the only formal guidance is adjusted EBITDA of "about USD 1.6bn" (cut from USD 1.65–1.75bn on 6 May 2026; reiterated in August and November). Year to date USD 1,178m; my Q4 is USD 420m (Q3 428), giving USD 1,598m: in line with guidance and 1% below the consensus median (1,619). The US port fees are very probably suspended through Q4 (USTR proposed a two-month extension of the suspension, Holland & Knight, 26 October 2026; China's reciprocal fees suspended to January 2027), so the Q4 risk is the bunker lag from Brent at USD 111.
  • FY2027 and beyond: no formal guidance. Informal: the "vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity to be contracted by end-2026 at rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions"; logistics quarterly EBITDA "starting with a five" as the target. My FY2027 EBITDA (1,870) is consistent with that commentary and 2.4% above the consensus median (1,827); the first formal 2027 guidance is expected with Q4 2026 results on 17 February 2027.
  • Mid-term targets: ROCE of at least 12% over the cycle (my forecasts imply roughly 15–16% in 2027–2028 and about 12% in 2030 on a capital base rising from about USD 6.3bn to 7.5bn with the newbuilds; company-adjusted ROCE was 18.4% in 2025); leverage below 3.0x net debt/EBITDA (my peak 1.55x); liquidity of at least USD 1bn (the binding constraint in 2026–2027, which is why my 2027 dividend is low); Logistics cash EBITDA margin 10% by 2028 (7.6% in Q3 2026; I assume it is reached).
  • Against consensus: FY2026 EBITDA -1%, EPS -4% (1.60 against 1.66); FY2027 EBITDA +2%, EPS -2% (1.96 against 1.99; my D&A and net financial items are higher); FY2028 EBITDA +9%, EPS +6% (1.85 against 1.75; consensus embeds a normalisation the contract book prevents, and its implied 2028 net financial cost of USD 61m is an artefact); FY2027 dividends paid well below consensus (1.05 against 1.58, payment-dated).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • EBITDA: the company's adjusted EBITDA equals the brokers' basis (FY2025: 1,811 company, 1,814 FactSet brokers' basis). Adjusted EBITDA excludes digital transformation and restructuring costs and, in FY2025, the USD 21m of US port fees (added back). If port fees resume, the company may again add them back, so reported EBITDA and cash could fall below adjusted EBITDA; my inputs include a probability-weighted port-fee cost in the margin. FactSet's "operating" EBITDA (FY2025: 2,229) is about USD 415m a year higher and must not be used with these inputs. Vessel sale gains (USD 40m Asian Captain in Q4 2026) are outside adjusted EBITDA.
  • EPS: the brokers' adjusted EPS excluded gains in FY2025 (2.20 against reported basic 2.41, the difference being mostly the USD 135m MIRRAT gain net of add-backs). My cross-check EPS is on that basis: adjusted EBITDA less D&A less net financial items, less 5% tax, less the EUKOR minority, over the weighted share count (426.8m in 2026; about 440m after). Including the Asian Captain gain, FY2026 reported EPS would be about 1.68.
  • Shares: FY2026 per-share figures use the weighted 426.8m; FY2027 onwards about 440m (17.15m new shares from 6 October 2026). FactSet's diluted count is 439.5m.
  • Net debt: the company's net interest-bearing debt (USD 2,119m at Q3 2026) includes lease liabilities (USD 1,459m). FactSet's enterprise-value snapshot uses debt of USD 1,187m and excludes leases. The EUKOR put liability (about USD 386m, exercisable from 2031) is debt-like and in neither.
  • Free cash flow: FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line is after dividends (FY2025: 1,640 - 245 - 989 = 406); consensus free cash flow (840 / 961 / 1,196) appears to be operating cash flow less capex, before dividends and before lease payments. My cross-check free cash flow is adjusted EBITDA less tax, net financial items (including lease interest), the change in working capital, capex and lease principal: equity free cash flow before all dividends (including about USD 60m a year to the EUKOR minority). It is about USD 380–410m a year lower than a consensus-style measure because of lease principal.
  • Dividends per share: FactSet's consensus DPS (1.37 / 1.58 / 1.35) is dated by payment period; my inputs are also amounts paid in the year, so they are comparable.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

EPS on the brokers' adjusted basis, after the EUKOR minority and excluding vessel sale gains (FY2026 including the Asian Captain gain: about 1.68). Free cash flow is equity free cash flow after capex and lease principal, before all dividends; USD millions. The model's own EPS and free cash flow will be about 9–10% higher than these figures if it does not deduct the EUKOR minority (about USD 71–86m of profit a year), and its FY2026 EPS may differ slightly because the actual nine months carried their own net financial items and tax.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO), the Oslo-listed global roll-on/roll-off ocean carrier and vehicle logistics group, with three segments: Shipping Services (deep-sea transport of cars, trucks and high-and-heavy cargo, including the EUKOR Car Carriers joint venture, 20% owned by Hyundai Motor Group), Logistics Services (marine terminals, vehicle and equipment processing centres, inland distribution) and Government Services (US-flag vessels serving the US government)

Request: T-D1

Peers: Höegh Autoliners (Oslo-listed pure-play car carrier repricing its contract book faster, with its own Aurora newbuild programme and an August 2026 equity raise); Hyundai Glovis (car carrier with captive Hyundai Motor Group volumes, competing for the same Hyundai Motor Group cargo and expanding its fleet in 2027); NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha (Japanese car-carrier incumbents inside diversified groups, whose car-carrier profits have started to fall); Grimaldi Group (private Atlantic and Mediterranean RoRo competitor); COSCO Shipping and China Merchants Energy Shipping (Chinese state-linked car-carrier operators adding capacity); CEVA Logistics (finished-vehicle logistics inside CMA CGM, a comparator for the logistics margin programme) Operating conditions: a record capacity shortage with one-year charter rates for a 6,500 CEU ship around USD 95,000–110,000 a day (October–November 2026); the large contract renewal round for 2027–2029 signed in late 2026; a fleet renewal in which owned dual-fuel newbuilds replace chartered tonnage; deliveries of about 400,000–460,000 CEU of new car carriers in 2027 (DNB Carnegie, 3 November 2026; NH Investment & Securities, 12 October 2026); earlier oversupply periods (2012–2018) and the 2020 demand collapse Inputs: How have car carriers that repriced large parts of their contract books at a cyclical peak converted the higher rates into EBITDA, earnings per share and free cash flow, and how much did cost inflation, cargo-mix changes and higher charter costs absorb (for example Wallenius Wilhelmsen's 2024 renewal season, when it renewed about 45% of volume at higher rates while volumes fell 8%, and Hyundai Glovis's 2025 affiliate contract renewal)? What is Wallenius Wilhelmsen's record of converting price into profit between 2021 and 2026, given that its net freight rate rose from USD 41.4 per cbm (2020) to USD 64.6 (2025) while adjusted EBITDA fell from USD 1,901m (2024) to USD 1,811m (2025) and to about USD 1.6bn guided for 2026? How credible is the company's statement (Q3 2026) that it will contract "the vast majority" of 2027–2029 capacity by end-2026 at rates "closely reflecting current strong market conditions", against its earlier "lower-for-longer" contracting described by DNB Carnegie (12 February 2026)? Has the company demonstrated the capability to deliver its Logistics cash EBITDA margin target of 10% by 2028 (7.6% reported for Q3 2026), and its SG&A savings of more than USD 30m through the Cognizant partnership? Has it delivered its Shaper newbuild programme on time and on budget (an USD 80m cost overrun from engine reconfiguration was disclosed)? What precedents exist of car carriers or other contract-based shipping companies moving from a cost-driven profit trough to a price-led recovery while renewing their fleets, and what distinguished those that succeeded? Focus: Whether Wallenius Wilhelmsen's demonstrated commercial, cost and fleet-delivery record makes a price-led profit recovery in 2027 plausible, and how much of the repricing peers in similar positions actually kept as profit and cash once fleet renewal, lease and financing costs are counted.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Höegh Autoliners (closest listed comparator; faster repricing, owned fleet); Hyundai Glovis (captive cargo, fleet growth); NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha (incumbent car-carrier segments with falling profits in 2026); COSCO Shipping and China Merchants Energy Shipping (state-linked new capacity); Clarkson (shipbroker research on ordering, scrapping and charter cycles); Maersk and Hapag-Lloyd (container lines, for the Suez Canal return and car-in-container substitution) Operating conditions: the 2027 contract renewal of a large share of volume; Chinese vehicle exports under the EU–China hybrid import cap of 350,000 units a year from 1 December 2026 and China's cut of the new-energy-vehicle export VAT rebate from 13% to 9% on 1 April 2027; the return of ships to the Suez Canal and Red Sea route; possible resumption of US Section 301 port fees on foreign-built car carriers; a car-carrier orderbook that fell from 42% of the fleet (July 2024) to 21% (March 2026) and rose to 27% (October 2026) per DNB Carnegie; zero car-carrier scrapping in 2024 and 2025 Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers that separate Wallenius Wilhelmsen's 2027–2030 results from 2021–2026: the renewal rate on the contracts signed for 2027–2029, the repricing of the Hyundai Motor Group/EUKOR contract (reported as USD 4.2bn over five years from late 2024 at about USD 55 per cbm, running to end-2029), spot and short-term cargo rates, short-term charter costs, bunker surcharge timing, and US port fees? What has been the historical dispersion of car-carrier EBITDA and net freight rates around turning points (2008–2010, 2012–2018, 2020, 2021–2024), and how fast did contract rates follow charter and spot rates down? What did the Q4 2025 experience show about port-fee cost and its recovery (the company recorded USD 21m in Q4 2025 and later added it back to adjusted EBITDA)? How sensitive are car-carrier earnings to a return of car carriers to the Suez route (DNB Carnegie's base case: a gradual return through 2027) and to Chinese export volumes (DNB Carnegie scenarios of up to 115% upside and 65% downside to rates)? How often have shipping forecasts that rested on one or two drivers (renewal rates, Chinese exports) been met? How wide is the current sell-side range for 2028 (FactSet: EBITDA USD 1,390m at Fearnley Securities to USD 1,912m at Clarksons Securities, November 2026) and what explains it? Focus: Which one or two variables decide Wallenius Wilhelmsen's earnings over the next 18 months, how wide and how skewed the range of outcomes for 2027 is given that much of the 2027 book is being contracted now, and how the dispersion widens for 2029–2030 when the 2027-signed contracts and the Hyundai Motor Group contract expire.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 15 November 2026, 14:21 to 14:44 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO): Third and fourth sets of questions — how predictable the forecasts are, and how far guidance, sell-side estimates and the market narrative already reflect our assessment

Written 2026-11-14 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Money in USD millions unless stated; per-share figures in USD; the share price is in NOK (NOK 162.8 on 9 November 2026). "EBITDA" means the company's adjusted EBITDA, which is the brokers' basis. "The base case" means the second set's forecasts as calculated by the Financial Analyst's model (model update 1), with the three input changes I make below.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface for the Consumer: bases, the model's flagged gaps, my input changes, and what is new

1. Two earnings-per-share bases are in use. Do not mix them.

  • Earnings per share before the minority is what the Financial Analyst's model shows (FY2027: 2.16; 2.20 after my changes). The model has no line for the 20% of EUKOR Car Carriers owned by Hyundai Motor Group (HMG).
  • Earnings per share after the minority is what the brokers forecast and what shareholders receive (FY2027: the second set's 1.96; 2.00 after my changes; consensus 1.99). Every comparison with consensus, and every use of a historical price-to-earnings multiple, must use this basis.

2. The gaps the model summary flags, explained.

(a) Earnings per share: the Analyst's cross-check is 8% to 10% below the model in every year. The whole gap is the EUKOR minority; it does not show that any input is wrong. My arithmetic on the second set's own figures:

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Model EPS (before the minority) 1.73 2.16 2.03 1.92 1.66
Minority, second set (USD m; per share) 71; 0.17 86; 0.20 80; 0.18 76; 0.17 73; 0.17
Second set's cross-check EPS (after the minority) 1.60 1.96 1.85 1.74 1.49

The subtraction reproduces the cross-check to the cent in FY2027 to FY2030. For FY2026 the second set's own bridge (pre-tax profit 783, less tax 39, less minority 71, over 426.8m shares) gives 1.58, not the 1.60 it wrote; I use 1.58. Free cash flow shows no gap (differences of less than USD 3m). Three consequences follow, and the memo must carry them:

  1. The model summary's "Viewforth against consensus" lines for net income (+8% to +21%) and earnings per share (+4.5%, +8.3%, +16.0%) are not like for like. They compare our earnings before the minority with consensus after it. Like for like, after my input changes: FY2026 1.58 against 1.66 (−5%); FY2027 2.00 against 1.99 (in line); FY2028 1.89 against 1.75 (+8%).
  2. The return at the guideline fair multiple is flattered by the same mismatch. The guideline multiple of 7.69x appears to come from the stock's ten-year history of 24-month-forward price-to-earnings multiples on consensus earnings (the financials block gives the ten-year 90th percentile as 7.70x), and consensus earnings are after the minority. The model applies it to earnings before the minority. On the model's basis today's multiple is 8.4x, so the fall to 7.69x costs 8%. On earnings after the minority today's multiple is about 9.2x (FactSet's own figure on consensus is 9.53x), so the fall to 7.69x costs about 16%. My arithmetic, keeping the model's earnings change and dividends: the 18-month return is about −12%, not −4.2%; the three-year return is about −14%, not −7.1%. My dividend change below adds back about two and three percentage points respectively.
  3. The model's free cash flow is before about USD 60m a year of cash dividends to the EUKOR minority.

(b) The phasing note ("the periods were moved by −22.1 points") is a second break of basis, not an input error. The model holds the nine reported months of 2026 on FactSet's "operating" EBITDA (498 + 463 + 528 = 1,489), which runs about USD 100m a quarter above the company's adjusted EBITDA (1,178). To reach the full-year 1,599 it therefore sets the fourth quarter at about USD 110m (a 7.8% margin) instead of about 420 (29.9%). The year is right; the quarter is not. Two cautions follow. First, the model's history row (EBITDA 2,257, 2,346 and 2,229 for FY2023 to FY2025; margins of 42% to 44%) is on FactSet's operating basis and its forecast row on the company basis, so the fall the table shows from 2,229 to 1,599 is mostly a change of basis; the true fall is from 1,811 to about 1,600. Second, any quarterly or trailing-twelve-month figure taken from the model across the fourth quarter of 2026 is distorted; the summary already says to use the forward split of the return, not the trailing one.

The Financial Analyst should show, beside the return at the guideline fair multiple, the same return calculated on earnings per share after the EUKOR minority interest (about 9% to 10% of group net profit), or confirm that the guideline fair multiple of 7.69x is meant to be applied to earnings before minority interests; and should label the FY2023 to FY2025 EBITDA row and the reported quarters of 2026 as FactSet "operating" EBITDA, about USD 415m a year above the company-adjusted basis on which the forecasts are made.

3. The three forecast inputs I change (recorded in inputs-changed.md).

Input FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 Second set
Depreciation and amortisation −690 −705 −720 −720 −735 −690 / −725 / −740 / −735 / −745
Dividend per share (paid in the year) 1.62 1.40 1.45 1.40 1.10 1.62 / 1.05 / 1.30 / 1.40 / 1.10
Lease payments (principal) −430 −410 −390 −350 −320 −380 / −410 / −390 / −350 / −320
  • Depreciation. The second set raised depreciation because it expected the 34 long-term chartered ships to be re-fixed at peak charter rates. The research written since shows the opposite: the lease book is shrinking. Lease liabilities fell from 1,538 (end-2025) to 1,459 (30 September 2026); lease additions on vessels were 194 in nine months against 465 in 2025; the long-term chartered fleet fell from 35 to 34 and short-term charters from six to two; the company holds twelve purchase options or obligations declarable to 2031 and exercised one (Grand Uranus) about USD 50m below market value. Shaper deliveries add about USD 4m a year each, largely offset by falling right-of-use depreciation. DNB Carnegie models depreciation flat at 696 to 2028; consensus is 701 and 720.
  • Dividends. See D-01: the second set let the liquidity floor decide the 2027 dividend; the company's conduct and its pre-arranged ship loans say otherwise.
  • Lease payments, FY2026 only. Lease principal repaid in the first nine months of 2026 was already 327 (Q3 2026 report, note 10), so the second set's 380 for the year leaves only 53 for the fourth quarter against a quarterly run-rate of about 100.

I do not change revenue growth, the EBITDA margin, net financial items or tax. The base case is the single most likely path, not the average of the possible paths: because the range of outcomes is skewed down (D-04), the average is lower, about USD 1.83bn of EBITDA in 2027 (equal to consensus) and about USD 1.77bn to 1.79bn in 2028.

4. What is new since the second set was written (reports dated 11 to 14 November 2026).

  • US port fees. The US Trade Representative formally extended the suspension to 9 January 2027 on 9 November 2026 (Holland & Knight; KPMG), and China matched it to 10 January 2027. Trade lawyers expect further short extensions of two to three months (Squire Patton Boggs, 3 November 2026). 2027 therefore opens with a rolling threat, not a single date.
  • Third-quarter detail. The net freight rate rose 5% on the quarter to USD 64.4 per cbm, but customer and trade mix gave USD 2.2 of the rise and price only USD 0.8. Shipping volume fell 3% on the quarter to 13.2m cbm. Shipping EBITDA rose 19% on the quarter to 355, helped by USD 47m less net fuel cost.
  • The contract book's average rate is disclosed, and it has not moved. The average net rate in the shipping book of business was USD 55 per cbm at the Q4 2025 call (11 February 2026) and USD 54 at Q3 2026 (4 November 2026), although about 40% of annual volume was extended in between. 41% of shipping capacity is up for renewal for the coming year.
  • Expectations for Chinese exports are cooling at the edges. The China Passenger Car Association expects export growth to slow to about 10% in 2027 (9 November 2026); S&P Global cut its 2027 Chinese production forecast by 1.2m units (Deutsche Bank, 13 November 2026); a supplier reports inventory building in transit channels and "overly aggressive" export expectations (Himax, 5 November 2026); Esgian reported that the deep-sea roll-on/roll-off market "shows signs of peaking" in October (12 November 2026). Jefferies and Bernstein expect shipments to be pulled forward into the fourth quarter of 2026 and the first quarter of 2027, ahead of the EU hybrid cap and the Chinese VAT rebate cut.
  • Suez. Container lines are returning (weekly transits at 50% to 69% of pre-crisis levels); car carriers are not. A full return would release about 5% of car-carrier capacity (Morgan Stanley; Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha says 4% to 5%), not 10% to 15%.
  • Fleet. The second Shaper (Royal Tern) was delivered on 20 October 2026; twelve remain.

5. Conflicts in the data.

  • EUKOR put liability: about 386 after the April 2026 deferral (first and second sets; the company's Q2 2026 call) against 897 "at Q3 2026" in one new report, which appears to repeat the pre-deferral figure. I use 386 until the Q4 accounts settle it.
  • Shipping volume is quoted on two bases (Q2 2026: 14.5m cbm in DNB Carnegie's notes, 13.6m in another source; the Q3 figure of 13.2m is on the lower basis). The second set's revenue arithmetic is unaffected, but per-cbm sensitivities are about 6% smaller on the lower basis (USD 1 per cbm is about USD 54m a year, not 57m to 60m).
  • Consensus dividends are probably not on the "paid in the year" basis the second set assumed. The two dividends actually paid in 2026 total 1.62 a share; consensus for FY2026 is 1.37 and DNB Carnegie's 1.48. They are more likely dividends declared for the year, which would put the dividend for the second half of 2026 (payable in the first half of 2027) at about 0.76 on consensus and 0.87 at DNB Carnegie.
  • There are two consensus levels. FactSet's median for FY2027 EBITDA is 1,827, but its mean is 1,782, and DNB Carnegie says its own 1,829 and 1,908 are 6% and 19% above Bloomberg consensus, which puts Bloomberg at about 1,725 for 2027 and about 1,603 for 2028. The median flatters where the average broker sits.
  • The new AlphaSense reports contain errors of attribution (for example, they call the chief executive the chief financial officer and give the company's stake in EUKOR as 40%; it is 80%). I use only figures that agree with company documents or the earlier sets.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of WAWI-NO with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-13 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.
