Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Wallenius Wilhelmsen ASA (WAWI-NO), the Oslo-listed global roll-on/roll-off ocean carrier and vehicle logistics group, with three segments: Shipping Services (deep-sea transport of cars, trucks and high-and-heavy cargo, including the EUKOR Car Carriers joint venture, 20% owned by Hyundai Motor Group), Logistics Services (marine terminals, vehicle and equipment processing centres, inland distribution) and Government Services (US-flag vessels serving the US government)

Request: OWN-1

Question: What do the ocean contracts of Wallenius Wilhelmsen (WAWI-NO) with vehicle and equipment manufacturers commit each side to, and how have such contracts behaved when volumes or market rates fell? Specifically: do its multi-year contracts of affreightment (including the three-year contracts announced in October and November 2026 with an Asian vehicle manufacturer, valued at about USD 700m, and with an equipment manufacturer, valued at about USD 950m, and the Hyundai Motor Group contract with EUKOR Car Carriers running to 31 December 2029) fix a share of the customer's volume, a minimum volume, or take-or-pay or dead-freight payments; which clauses allow rates to change during the term (bunker adjustment, currency, regulatory surcharges such as US port fees and EU emissions costs, renegotiation or hardship clauses, extension options); and what have company executives, former commercial managers, customers' logistics buyers and brokers said about how binding the volume and rate terms are in practice (the company's chief executive has said that customers are obliged to deliver volumes under contract but that the company works with them to defer or move commitments when volumes fall short)? How did contracted volumes, contract rates, early renegotiations and re-tenders develop at Wallenius Wilhelmsen, Höegh Autoliners, Hyundai Glovis, NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha in earlier periods when vehicle exports or charter rates fell (2008 to 2010, 2012 to 2018, 2020, and 2024 to 2025 when Wallenius Wilhelmsen's volumes fell while its net freight rate rose), and in comparable contract shipping businesses whose customers held long contracts signed at peak rates (container-ship lessors and liquefied-natural-gas carriers): how often did customers ship less than contracted, seek rate cuts or re-tender early, and how much of the disclosed contract backlog was actually earned as revenue?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How have the share prices and valuation multiples of Wallenius Wilhelmsen (WAWI-NO) and of listed car-carrier and contract-shipping peers behaved around earlier cycle peaks, and what does that record indicate about where the shares stand in their cycle now? For Wallenius Wilhelmsen, Höegh Autoliners, Hyundai Glovis, Gram Car Carriers (until its acquisition by MSC in 2024), NYK Line, Mitsui O.S.K. Lines and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha, and for shipping companies that locked in multi-year contracts at peak rates (for example the container-ship lessors Danaos, Global Ship Lease and Costamare in 2021 to 2023, and owners of liquefied-natural-gas carriers on long charters): what forward price-to-earnings, enterprise-value-to-EBITDA, price-to-book and price-to-net-asset-value multiples did the market pay at the peak of charter rates, at the peak of sell-side estimate upgrades and at the peak of reported earnings; in what order and with what lags did share prices, charter rates, the orderbook, sell-side estimates and reported earnings peak; how far did multiples and share prices then fall and how much of the fall did dividends offset; and did companies with long contract backlogs hold higher multiples than spot-exposed peers? What valuation methods and normalised or mid-cycle earnings assumptions do brokers use for Wallenius Wilhelmsen today (for example DNB Carnegie's target price of NOK 210 on 5 November 2026, based on seven times enterprise value to a long-term EBITDA of about USD 1.4bn), how have those assumptions changed over the last two years, and what do the equity issues by the companies themselves (Höegh Autoliners at NOK 167 a share on 25 August 2026; Wallenius Wilhelmsen at NOK 168 a share on 6 October 2026) and transactions by insiders and major shareholders since then indicate?

Request: OWN-3

Question: Beyond DNB Carnegie, what do the other brokers covering Wallenius Wilhelmsen (WAWI-NO) and the investors who own or follow the shares actually say about the company? For each of Fearnley Securities (Hold), Clarksons Securities (Hold), Nordea (Buy), Pareto Securities (Buy) (ratings as shown by FactSet on 9 November 2026) and any other house publishing on the company or on car carriers (for example Arctic Securities, SEB, SpareBank 1 Markets, Danske Bank, Kepler Cheuvreux, ABG Sundal Collier, Morgan Stanley and Jefferies): the rating, the target price and their changes over the last twelve months; the forecasts for 2027 and 2028 of the net freight rate, shipping volume, adjusted EBITDA, depreciation, net financial items, minority interests, earnings per share, capital spending, lease payments and dividends per share; the valuation method; and the stated reasons for the view, in particular why the two Hold ratings stand, and what each house expects for the company's first 2027 guidance and for the dividend for the second half of 2026, both due with the fourth-quarter report on 17 February 2027. For investors: what do fund-manager letters, shipping-specialist investors and commentators (on X/Twitter, Substack, Seeking Alpha, and Norwegian investor forums and financial press such as Finansavisen, Dagens Næringsliv and Kapital), short-interest and securities-lending data, changes among the largest shareholders and in the number of private shareholders, and the reception of the October 2026 private placement show about how the shares are owned and discussed; which topics dominate (for example the dividend yield, Chinese vehicle exports, the cycle peak, the Suez Canal, US port fees), and which topics relevant to earnings per share and cash flow (lease payments, the EUKOR minority interest, the timing of contract expiries) are absent from that commentary?
